Sources: \longtok{platform\_modal/scripts/partial\_\allowbreak{}correlation\_\allowbreak{}zvf.py};
\longtok{platform\_modal/scripts/pcd\_\allowbreak{}vs\_\allowbreak{}zvf.py};
\longtok{platform\_hybrid/experiments/modal/modal\_\allowbreak{}samestack\_\allowbreak{}ppo\_\allowbreak{}grpo.py};
Table 4.2. Current repaired scripts cannot by themselves certify the
bytes used in older runs.

For \(v_{1,g}=Gv_{0,g}/(G-1)\) (sample variance), exact ties are
invariant, but equal positive cutoffs satisfy
\(\mathrm{ZVF}^{(1)}_\tau\le\mathrm{ZVF}^{(0)}_\tau\). Equal
classification requires \(\tau_1=G\tau_0/(G-1)\). Switching only the
advantage denominator gives

\[\frac{A_i^{(1)}}{A_i^{(0)}}=\frac{\sqrt{v_{0,g}}+\varepsilon_A}{\sqrt{G/(G-1)}\sqrt{v_{0,g}}+\varepsilon_A}\]

for nonzero numerators. This tends to \(\sqrt{(G-1)/G}\) when the
stabiliser is negligible, or 0.7071 at G = 2. No recorded learning-rate
compensation for a convention switch was established. This advantage
scaling is not an exact scaling law for Adam parameter updates. The
stored sweep is therefore interpreted under its executed normalisation,
not under a silently substituted sample variance.

Gradient utilisation is the corresponding complement,
\(\mathrm{GU}_{0,t}=1-\mathrm{ZVF}_{0,t}\) for exact ties and
\(\mathrm{GU}^{(0)}_{\tau,t}=1-\mathrm{ZVF}^{(0)}_{\tau,t}\) for a
threshold. The complement is a descriptive accounting identity, not a
claim about optimiser behaviour. Its scope must be read against the
runner actually used, so the loss form of every study is listed in Table
4.2, read from the runner code rather than from the internal reports'
prose. Three facts in the table govern how ZVF is interpreted. First, an
exact-tie group has zero reward-relative surrogate gradient only where
the runner actually centres advantages within that group. Near-ties
counted by \(\mathrm{ZVF}^{(0)}_{\tau}\) need not have exactly zero
advantages. TRL's dump records β = 0.04 and an on-device reference
model, its launcher leaves β unspecified, the PPO roster inherits
adaptive-KL defaults, and the managed dump has β = null. None of these
is evidence of zero executed KL; unknown remains unknown. Even verified
zero-KL runs can update through optimiser state, regularisation or
auxiliary losses. Second, the inspected custom-loss Tinker reference
runners are REINFORCE with a group-mean baseline (the actor's historical
source binding is incomplete; Appendix D): the loss is \(-A_i\) times
the summed token log-probability, averaged over completions, with no
importance ratio and no clipping, and this thesis calls them that rather
than GRPO in the strict sense. Third, the runners labelled GRPO are not
one loss: the same-stack and Dr.~GRPO studies use a clipped ratio with
two inner epochs, the custom-loss Tinker reference loop does not, while
separate SDK \texttt{importance\_sampling} runners do use a ratio
without clipping. The Phase-1 four-method run divides by no σ even in
its ``grpo'' arm. The scope must consequently be checked per row,
including auxiliary losses, masks and source-binding gaps, rather than
inferred from the algorithm label.

Budget matching is defined per experiment rather than inferred from a
common step count. The primary arithmetic G sweep uses 16 prompts per
step for 40 steps, so G = 2, 4, 8 and 16 require 1,280, 2,560, 5,120 and
10,240 generated completions respectively; it is a fixed-prompt,
fixed-step sweep, not a matched-token comparison. The original PPO/GRPO
comparison fixes 128 completions per step but changes prompt exposure;
the later GSM8K comparison fixes 64 completions per step with the same
issue. The separate Qwen3-8B panel fixes 2,560 rollouts while changing
optimiser steps, and the curriculum re-test explicitly fixes a 30k-token
budget. Equal rollout counts do not establish equal generated tokens,
wall-clock cost or optimisation opportunity. Each result is interpreted
against the budget it actually controls.

Three properties make the definition more than a restatement of the
training loop. First, it is non-tautological under a simple null: if
completions were independent Bernoulli draws with per-prompt success
probability \(p_g\), then
\(\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{ZVF}_t] = \mathbb{E}_{p_g}[p_g^K + (1-p_g)^K]\), a
quantity that depends on the model's accuracy distribution and the group
size rather than on the observed batch. The identity
\(\mathrm{pass@}G - p^G = 1 - h_G(p)\) follows algebraically for the
model expectations under the same i.i.d.-Bernoulli model and is
therefore reported as an algebraic remark rather than as evidence.
Second, it is checked against that null on instrumented runs: a
Qwen2.5-0.5B sweep over G ∈ \{2, 4, 8, 16\}, three seeds and 40 steps
--- twelve runs and 480 logged steps --- compares per-step observed ZVF
with the Bernoulli-null prediction \(p^G + (1-p)^G\). Because the null
for each step is computed from the same step's mean reward, a high
correlation is partly mechanical, so the comparison is designed as a
shape check rather than a validation, and the baseline used is the
per-step average of the closed form (results in Chapter 6 §6.3.3).
Third, at larger scale the same instrument is run on Qwen3-8B against
GSM8K test problems through the Tinker managed API at G = 8, temperature
1.0, over 200 problems and three seeds (sampling only, no training;
these are the same three stored tensors whose ZVF P2 recomputes), with
the zero-variance groups decomposed into all-correct and all-wrong and
compared with the same null. Deviations from the null in either
direction are not read as evidence about dependence between completions.

That the quantity is portable across libraries is a separate, weaker
claim, and the project states its limits explicitly. A normaliser at
\longtok{platform\_modal/scripts/zvf\_\allowbreak{}compute\_\allowbreak{}cross\_\allowbreak{}framework.py} maps
each framework's reward and mask fields onto the common definition ---
TRL's \texttt{rewards} with \texttt{batch\_size\ /\ group\_size} and
\texttt{completion\_mask}; Tinker's \texttt{rollout.reward} with
\texttt{rollout.group\_id} and \texttt{rollout.eos\_mask}; OpenRLHF's
\texttt{reward\_score} with \texttt{prompt\_index} and
\texttt{attention\_mask}; veRL's \texttt{data\_source/reward} with
\texttt{uid} and \texttt{response\_mask} --- and emits JSONL records of
the form \texttt{\{step,\allowbreak{}\ zvf,\allowbreak{}\ batch\_\allowbreak{}\allowbreak{}reward\_\allowbreak{}\allowbreak{}mean,\allowbreak{}\ n\_\allowbreak{}groups,\allowbreak{}\ K\}}.
The scope correction attached to that parser is that it normalises
reward logs and does not make its runner canonical GRPO; because the
project does not bundle raw per-group rollout JSONL for every framework,
no fresh four-way pointwise ZVF overlay is claimed, only the narrower
implementation-level portability claim. The threshold ε is fixed in
advance, and its sensitivity is reported with the P2 results in Chapter
6 §6.3.1. Boundary conditions are declared where the diagnostic does not
apply: dense or continuous reward signals, K = 1, SFT-saturated
baselines and non-outcome-reward RL all fall outside the setting in
which the definition is meaningful.

The methodological point of this section is that ZVF is a diagnostic and
not a metric. The design commits to that position before the results
rather than inferring it from them, and it takes four precautions.
First, because the statistic is unsigned, a high value is read as
consistent with either a solved or an unsolved task and never as a score
in one direction; the heterogeneous 368-record export is audited in
Chapter 7 §7.1 and §7.3. Second, a pooled association between ZVF and
performance is always broken down by task type before it is read,
because the task mix of a pool can set the sign of the association
(results in Chapter 6 §6.3.4). Third, the historical pooled association
is retained only as an audited source result: it mixes synthetic cells
and heterogeneous outcomes, including late training reward, and cannot
validate held-out prediction. Fourth, no step-level p-value is computed
on the 480 autocorrelated steps of the instrumented sweep, because
treating correlated within-run steps as independent samples would
manufacture significance. One further boundary is recorded for the same
reason: a variance-mitigation table in the internal report sources is a
declared simulation projection and not evidence, and its rows are read
as synthetic rather than as independent library deployments. The result
of these choices is a diagnostic that is useful for describing where
within a batch the learning signal is and is not, and that is explicitly
not proposed as a quantity to be maximised, ranked or reported as a
headline score.

\section{The Seven Core RL Studies}\label{the-seven-core-rl-studies}

Semester 3 delivered the shared code and the initial experimental
foundation; the Semester 4 programme --- the research direction, the
expanded experiments, the analysis and the P1--P8 internal reports ---
is the present author's solo continuation, and the roster records 206
analysis iterations behind the numbers reported in this thesis. The core
portfolio comprises seven RL studies, each documented in an unpublished
internal report with its own question and evidence boundary; the titles
below are working titles. Their results are in Chapters 6--7. The
unrelated supervised side study is archived separately in Appendix F.
The questions and boundaries are stated here because they are
methodological commitments: a study's question determines which
comparisons it may make, and the boundary states what it declines to
conclude.

P1 asks whether post-training outcomes admit a useful cross-scale law.
Its historical roster is too heterogeneous to answer: interrupted
three-to-five-step traces, single-seed frontier records, and
scale-dependent recipes do not identify asymptotic performance. The
revised analysis does not fit the interrupted anchors or interpret an
unsuccessful fit as evidence that scaling is absent. P1 is retained as a
design and identifiability audit, with matched-horizon multi-seed
comparisons left open.

P2, ``The Zero-Variance Fraction: A Descriptive Diagnostic for Signal
Starvation in GRPO'', asks how much of a training batch carries usable
group-relative signal, and whether that fraction predicts the final
outcome. The study characterises ZVF descriptively, treats the identity
of §4.3 as algebraic rather than empirical, audits a historical pooled
association whose mixed synthetic membership and heterogeneous targets
cannot establish held-out prediction, and marks the variance-mitigation
table as a simulation projection; the retired roster item that
previously carried a causal framing now lives under
\longtok{archive/absorbed/R02\_\allowbreak{}main\_\allowbreak{}zvf/}. Results are in Chapter 6
§6.3.

P3 measures group-relative contrast across G ∈ \{2, 4, 8, 16\}. Its
arithmetic training telemetry can describe how contrast changes under
the executed fixed-prompt recipe. Its saturated in-distribution
evaluation does not identify an accuracy optimum, and untrained G ≥ 32
token-budget grids are excluded from empirical claims. The all-pairs
preference reading is an interpretation, not a proved identity with DPO.

P4 asks about length dynamics and generalisation, but its present
evidence does not test uncensored verbosity. The 200-token GSM8K cap
leaves little upward length headroom and can penalise truncation before
the final answer. The 1,024-token extension also begins with
first-five-step means near its ceiling, lacks held-out accuracy outputs,
and has incomplete historical source binding; invalid original
Dr.~GRPO-labelled arms are excluded. The valid retained records support
logged capped training trajectories only, with no causal attribution to
token mask or normalisation. No new runs were performed for this
revision.

P5, ``Report the Stack, Not the Label: RL-for-LLM Results Are
Stack-Conditioned'', asks how much of a reported result is attributable
to the stack rather than to the algorithm label, and what a reportable
stack must contain. Three exhibits carry the argument: a nominally
matched-configuration backend audit, a same-label ``DAPO'' comparison
between true dynamic sampling on an open trainer and an asymmetric-clip
surrogate on a closed stack, and a twelve-cell head-to-head of four
labelled algorithms (GRPO, DAPO, Dr.~GRPO and GSPO; three seeds × 15
steps) on one fixed stack, distinct from the single-seed 40-step N2 run
of GRPO, AERO, GIFT and AReaL; their results are in Chapter 7 §7.1. The
study proposes an eight-item minimum reportable stack (seven
run-manifest fields and one evaluation item) --- loss form;
reference-policy and KL handling; sampler, backend and precision
including base-checkpoint identity by revision or hash; per-step ZVF and
gradient-utilisation trajectory; group-size schedule; a disjoint
held-out split; decontamination plus a parser-robustness probe; and
held-out pass@k curves alongside pass@1 --- and formalises an
RL-reproducibility threat model with flip-risk grades R0--R5. This is
the standard this thesis is written to.

P6, ``GRPO-Registry: A Machine-Readable Catalog of Group-Relative RL
Stacks and Their Variant Deltas'', asks whether stacks can be compared
mechanically rather than through prose. It defines stack records
carrying the seven manifest fields of the MIN-REPORT-RL standard, a
per-component variant-delta status, three variant-delta records verified
against the DAPO, Dr.~GRPO and GSPO papers, twenty seed stack entries
(28 on disk at this revision; §7.2), and a reference CLI with a 0--100
MIN-REPORT auditor badge and a \texttt{stackdiff} command that maps a
pair of entries to an R0--R5 flip-risk verdict. The study is explicitly
a resource study rather than a second ranking, and it cites P5's
seventeen-fold exhibit only to motivate the schema. Results are in
Chapter 7 §7.2.

P7, ``From Diagnostic to Controller'', asks whether a reward-contrast
diagnostic can guide group allocation. Under independent Bernoulli
rewards, \(h_G(p)=p^G+(1-p)^G\) is an exact tie-probability identity.
The historical score \(S=p(1-p)(1-h_G(p))\) is a heuristic, not the
expected gradient magnitude; the exact mean-absolute-centred-advantage
identity does not require the extra \(1-h_G\) factor (§7.3). Gradient
norms additionally depend on score vectors, masking, normalisation and
the optimiser. The 368-record export has unresolved provenance
composition, the archived gradient-correlation scalar lacks raw
gradients, and retrospective controller rankings are withdrawn. The
retained contribution is the implemented allocation rule and its
assumptions. A prospective static-G versus adaptive-G comparison with
matched realised tokens, frozen evaluation and execution receipts
remains unrun.

The historical non-RL side study is retained only as archived
supplementary material in Appendix F. It is not part of the seven-study
RL argument or any comparative evidence in this chapter.

\section{The Design of the E1--E14 Evaluation
Campaign}\label{the-design-of-the-e1e14-evaluation-campaign}

The E1--E14 campaign is a pipeline and evidence-governance demonstration
with incomplete historical training provenance. It is not a verified
held-out capability campaign and does not establish RL efficacy. The
reported actor is Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B with the historical seed809
adapter, trained for 40 steps; its lost execution-source/dirty-diff
binding and missing consumed-row inventory mean NFR-9 is Not Met. Public
model identity can be checked independently of those gaps, but cannot
repair them (Chapter 8 §8.1.1; Table 3.3). The recorded training-source
names are API-Bank-RLVR and SWE-Gym. Absence of benchmark names from
that list is weaker than verified content disjointness; E10 explicitly
records \texttt{heldout\_claim:\ false}. Native grading and scoped
receipts can still support particular evaluation records, with the
limitations in Chapter 8, without certifying the training run (Figure~\ref{fig:campaign}).


\fig{fig_campaign_design}
{Historical lane-scope map for the harness audit}
{Historical lane-scope map for the harness audit. Original contracts and exploratory protocol-override replacements are kept separate. Replacement completion labels describe retained coverage, not satisfaction of the prospective fail-closed gates. Missing or private source payloads limit independent reproduction; a source hash is not execution authentication. Full metric definitions and denominators are in Chapter~8 and Table~C.3.}
{fig:campaign}



The choice of a 40-step actor is reported as the available lightweight
training snapshot. No contemporaneous evidence in this review
establishes whether cost, quota or an efficacy target dictated that
horizon, so no causal rationale is invented. The paired small-scale
trained-versus-base results are inconclusive. This chapter claims only
the observable evaluation workflow and its failures, not that 40 steps
were adequate training or that every lane followed every proposed gate.

Evaluators are the suites' own scorers, used natively rather than
reimplemented, and they are pinned by revision. The surviving native
runners live under \texttt{zvf-program/flagship/}, including
\longtok{e1\_\allowbreak{}swe\_\allowbreak{}bench\_\allowbreak{}pro\_\allowbreak{}full\_\allowbreak{}eval.py},
\longtok{e11\_\allowbreak{}verilog\_\allowbreak{}eval\_\allowbreak{}decontamination.py},
\longtok{eval\_\allowbreak{}pavlov\_\allowbreak{}sdab.py}, \longtok{eval\_\allowbreak{}pavlov\_\allowbreak{}xlam.py},
\longtok{mle\_\allowbreak{}bench\_\allowbreak{}eval.py} and \longtok{eval\_\allowbreak{}apex\_\allowbreak{}agents.py}. The
evaluation-side revisions pinned for the decontamination audit are
LAB-Bench \longtok{998a8e0a40cf116c80e1b0e7a805ebb5fb9fa838}, AgentDojo
\longtok{089ed468cf3ed0322acc66b0211f26d9d90dbf60} and Omni-MATH
\longtok{40ba231d8f16e29ecd40e6407e2c8640145a8f62}, with the
training-side envelope pinned at API-Bank-RLVR
\longtok{bf67c42626f02c305514b1df16dcabc5fc616333} (597 rows, MIT) and
SWE-Gym \longtok{bb94ed9e39bbeb96a7fcbfb533b80f25a7fd59cb} (2,438 train
rows, MIT). Using the native scorer is a substantive choice and not a
convenience: when the Omni-MATH lane produced two rows whose judged
outputs were truncated before any equivalence verdict, the native
scorer's omission of missing-verdict rows was adjudicated as correct
official behaviour rather than a parser bug, and the lane closed with no
re-judge because re-judging would deviate from the official scorer and
generate new evidence that could have a small nonzero effect on the
score; retaining the historical native result was a protocol choice.

For the training-selection protocol, sampling is frozen rather than
merely recorded: temperature 0.1, top-p 0.95, one sample per row, a
maximum of 128 response tokens, with the service seed fixed where the
provider supports it and otherwise recorded in the receipt. Prompt and
response limits are 1,200 and 128 tokens respectively, and the same
source requires the strict verifier implementation to be hashed before
paid work, with a perfect call defined as a verifier score of exactly
1.0 and no parser, target, split or verifier change permitted
mid-campaign. Freezing the sampler addresses the failure mode in which a
benchmark score quietly improves because decoding became more favourable
between runs; it also fixes the comparison between the base reference
and the adapter, which is evaluated side by side on untouched manifests.
The 128-token setting belongs to that protocol only. The E1--E14 lanes
were decoded under each suite's own settings, which range from 1,024 to
8,192 output tokens at temperatures 0 to 0.2, and are recorded per lane
in the serving table of Chapter 8 §8.1.2. The actor's own training run
also departed from the protocol in several fields (§8.1.1).

The prospective receipt model is freeze-before-paid-work, and it is the
part of the campaign design that carries the most methodological weight.
Before any paid phase a run must record an immutable base-model
revision, tokenizer revision, container or environment digest and
adapter revision; the selection slice and the disjoint holdout are
re-created from a pinned dataset revision and sealed, are write-once,
are not mounted in training, and the word ``holdout'' is reserved for a
suite or manifest only once its split, task-ID, license, runtime and
decontamination receipts are complete. The selection slice is used for
arm decisions and is therefore unsuitable for confirmatory claims; the
final evaluation manifests are never used to choose an arm, alter a
prompt, or re-run a failed suite with a more favourable split. Tracking
is verified before spend rather than after: an online Weights \& Biases
run must be initialised and its Tinker run identifier recorded before
the first paid step, every step records loss, reward, configuration,
seed, split identity and cumulative cost, and every checkpoint is
published to Hugging Face with a unique repository or revision URL, with
a failure of any of these stopping the run fail-closed. If a receipt is
missing, the phase is blocked rather than silently shortened. At handoff
the protocol requires four separate evidence classes to be reported
apart: completed receipts; operational and provenance receipts;
prospective gates and budget projections; and admissible final results,
the last existing only if every gate passed.

That last distinction has teeth in practice, and the campaign's
disposition record shows it. The portfolio-level protocol defines a
predeclared equal-domain macro average over sixteen domains, a
conjunctive coverage gate, an integrity gate, a global improvement gate
requiring the lower bound of a one-sided 95\% interval for the portfolio
difference to exceed zero, a domain no-regression guard with a
predeclared five-percentage-point tolerance, and a safety guard bounding
any violation increase by two percentage points --- and it states that a
cost cap may not be met by dropping domains or suites, and that a suite
or domain which is missing, underpowered, contaminated or outside the
cost cap must be labelled incomplete or blocked, ``not negative and not
positive''. The portfolio training phase was never launched, so the
campaign does not report a portfolio result; the recorded outcome is a
set of per-lane evaluations of the one adapter, and the protocol's own
instruction that a partial subset is not a portfolio result and may not
be relabelled as one is honoured throughout.

Lane dispositions are recorded in a seven-state taxonomy rather than as
pass or fail: COMPLETE, for lanes with verified full-coverage results
and no open gate (E8 public, E10 benign, E11); COMPLETE\_\allowbreak{}TERMINAL\_\allowbreak{}NOTE,
for a lane complete under the native protocol with a recorded terminal
note (E14 replacement); CLOSED\_EXTERNAL, for lanes with no local or
paid path, closed by written closure records (E3, E7, E8-private,
E10-private, E12, E14-private); PENDING\_QUOTA, for lanes gated on cloud
quota resolution alone (E6, E9); AMENDMENT\_\allowbreak{}ACCEPTED\_\allowbreak{}LAUNCH\_\allowbreak{}PENDING
(E2, E13); REBUILD\_\allowbreak{}READY\_\allowbreak{}LAUNCH\_\allowbreak{}PENDING (E1 wave10, E5 successor27);
and CLOSED\_PARTIAL (E4). Those are the states of the 19 September
ledger. The three pending states were emptied on 27 September, when the
E1, E2, E5, E6, E9 and E13 replacement scopes were run to completion
(§8.4). This report assigns them COMPLETE on coverage, with two
qualifications recorded against the label: E6 still has 108 tasks
awaiting a judge, and E1's coverage spans two contracts reported
separately. The taxonomy exists so that a lane which has not been
measured is not silently absorbed into a percentage. Carried alongside
it is the project's evidence rule, stated in the ledger of record and
repeated in every results artefact: original-contract and
replacement-scope numbers are never pooled. The rule is not cosmetic.
The original-contract E1 lane and its replacement scope are different
suites with different task populations, and a table that added them
would produce a number that corresponds to no benchmark.

Decontamination is handled as a declared deterministic procedure with
its own stated limits. The check hashes complete UTF-8 fields and then
normalised fields with SHA-256; for fields of at least eight tokens it
hashes each consecutive eight-token sequence and computes exact set
Jaccard similarity for every training and evaluation pair sharing a
shingle; and it tests whether an entire normalised evaluation field is a
token-bounded contiguous substring of a training field. Against the
pinned training envelope, the per-suite inventories were 1,967 LAB-Bench
tasks over 13,113 text fields with 5,105 eligible for eight-token near
checks, 97 AgentDojo tasks over 71,966 fields with 9,382 eligible, and
4,428 Omni-MATH tasks over 13,284 fields with 9,394 eligible; the count
of near pairs at or above 0.80 was zero in all three suites, and the
maximum eight-gram Jaccard similarity was 0 for LAB-Bench and AgentDojo
and 0.13414634146341464 for Omni-MATH, below the declared 0.80
threshold, with all 1,338 candidate pairs sharing an eight-gram
examined. Short exact matches of zero to three normalised tokens were
retained and enumerated rather than dropped --- the longest examples
being seven characters in the LAB and Omni inventories and at most
seventeen raw characters in AgentDojo, such as ``Paris'' and
``Conference Room A'' --- and the audit states plainly that these
collisions do not demonstrate duplication of a task but do make an
unqualified ``zero overlap'' claim incorrect. The audit's scope limits
are recorded with equal care: it does not reconstruct which training
records were consumed: 512 is the size of the training pool recorded in
the E11 decontamination receipt
(\longtok{selected\_\allowbreak{}training\_\allowbreak{}row\_\allowbreak{}count}), from which the 40-step run
drew 80 prompts at two per step, and the transform and consumed-row
inventory that would identify them were not recorded; it establishes a
content comparison against the verified historical source envelope
rather than proving global pretraining cleanliness, semantic
disjointness, character-edit disjointness or image cleanliness; ASCII
normalisation loses mathematical punctuation and non-ASCII text; and
fields shorter than eight tokens fall outside the near-match and
containment rule. The audit itself is receipted: it re-verifies Weights
\& Biases run \texttt{withheld-id} against
\longtok{training\_\allowbreak{}identity\_\allowbreak{}verified.json}, matches the 23,733 original
source-field document hashes to the historical E11 receipt, adds 2,507
decoded nested API-Bank strings to reach 26,240 source documents, and
records 43,977,748 bytes downloaded against a 500 MiB cap, with the
checker not having enumerated the roughly 2.58 billion possible document
pairs (26,240 training documents × 98,363 evaluation fields). The
near-duplicate check covered only the 23,881 evaluation fields eligible
for eight-token checks; what was not enumerated is the all-pairs
comparison, including shorter fields and character-edit overlap.

The proposed gate and the executed path must also be distinguished. The
27 September replacements used thin native runners under a separate
authorisation rather than the reconstructed fail-closed controllers
(§5.9). Their receipts record that deviation; these runs are not
evidence that the prospective controllers enforced their guarantees.

The campaign records incomplete execution explicitly. Some September
lanes remained blocked by infrastructure or readiness conditions and
were completed on 27 September after changes to the execution
environment. The per-lane protocol deviations and results remain
separate (§8.4). Private account state, access routes and operational
approvals are omitted from the public derivative.

The campaign also carries its integrity accounting in the same register.
Eleven of eleven deterministic checks passed on 2026-09-19, covering
exact divisions, receipt presence, sealed-artifact hash equality and
lost-source absence probes. A judgment layer was added for
classification, faithfulness and value-ordering questions and then
partly invalidated by an external service outage: both
blocker-classification batteries returned degenerate distributions after
the incident, their receipts are retained as incident evidence, and the
per-lane blocker column therefore rests on document evidence and not on
model judgment. A design rule was banked from the healthy window and is
followed in this thesis: arithmetic and string lookups stay in code, and
model judgment is reserved for classification, faithfulness and value
ordering, because a parity probe was answered incorrectly even while the
service was healthy.

What the campaign does not claim follows directly from the design. It
does not claim improvement over any baseline anywhere, because no lane
carries a test establishing it. The one trained-versus-base comparison
is the paired small-scale rerun of Table 8.6, with 5 to 151 identical
items per lane, and its outcome is reported in Chapter 8; the campaign's
role in this thesis is to fix the evaluation protocol, the receipt
discipline and the disposition taxonomy that any future improvement
claim would have to satisfy. It does not claim that the fourteen suites
constitute a portfolio result. It does not pool original-contract with
replacement-scope figures, and it does not average across suites. The
single-actor campaign does not rank model families; its separately
paired base comparison remains small-sample and inconclusive. And where
the record is incomplete --- a lost execution source, an unavailable
external benchmark scope --- the incompleteness is written into the
ledger rather than resolved by inference.

\section{C1 Frozen Policy Diagnostic and Reference
Review}\label{c1-frozen-policy-diagnostic-and-reference-review}

C1 completed 4,096 fixed-order screening questions with eight
independently seeded responses per question. Eligibility required all
eight outputs to be parseable, uncapped and assigned zero exact-match
reward by frozen parser v2. After the full screen, the first 64 of 87
eligible questions received 24 additional responses each. Selection was
truncated deterministically; the 23 other eligible questions were not
followed up. The primary endpoint is whether at least one of the 24
fresh responses receives parser-defined exact-match reward one. No
question was removed or relabeled after review. {[}S1, S13{]}

The new workload contains 32,768 screening responses and 1,536 fresh
follow-up responses, totaling 34,304 new draws. A disjoint historical
smoke cohort contributes 1,024 retained draws imported once, giving
35,328 recorded unique draws. The historical smoke is an operational
quality record, not part of the primary denominator. The 512 initial
responses for the selected 64 questions are already included within
screening, and must not be counted again. {[}S1, S10{]}

The model and tokenizer were Qwen/Qwen3-8B at revision
\longtok{b968826d9c46dd6066d109eabc6255188de91218}, with BF16 and
thinking disabled. The dataset was openai/gsm8k, main configuration,
train split, revision \longtok{740312add88f781978c0658806c59bc2815b9866}.
Generation used temperature 0.7, top-p 0.8, top-k 20, min-p 0, a
1,024-token generation cap and 4,096-token context. The recorded
execution used one NVIDIA A100-SXM4-80GB, vLLM 0.11.0, PyTorch 2.8.0 and
Transformers 4.57.1, with eager execution enabled and prefix caching
disabled. Fixed request seeds do not imply bitwise reproducibility
across hardware or allocations. {[}S12, S13{]}

Screening had 504 parser failures and 19 cap hits among 32,768 outputs.
By design the selected initial groups had neither. Follow-up had ten
parser failures and four cap hits among 1,536 outputs. Those quality
counts do not certify semantic correctness. The primary denominator
remains 64 regardless of later quality flags. Descriptive prefixes of
eight, sixteen and twenty-four fresh draws yield 9/64, 10/64 and 11/64
parser events. They are nested observations, not independent comparisons
or evidence of an optimal rollout budget. {[}S1{]}

The Wilson interval uses a question-level binomial working model within
the selected cohort. It is not design-based or population coverage: both
outcome-dependent eligibility and fixed-order truncation restrict the
estimand. The observed 87/4,096 eligibility count similarly describes
this fixed screen, not benchmark-wide mathematical failure prevalence.

The first review solved the questions before comparing reference
answers, then inspected the required model outputs. A second reference
review was separately masked from the first judgments and model outputs.
Final reconciliation compared the exact question, reference and
annotations, retaining disagreements rather than forcing consensus. All
stages were assistant-assisted. Separate masking and repeat review do
not provide independent human validation or rule out shared model
biases. {[}S2, S6{]}

The first output audit covers all 64 cases, all 512 initial responses
and 143 required fresh responses: 655 responses in total, including all
38 primary-success outputs. The selection rule for the other 105 fresh
responses is not recovered, so the 143 form a selected audit set rather
than a random sample; whether the follow-up cap hits are a subset of the
parser failures is likewise not recorded in the retained public ledger.
The final mechanical coverage receipt verifies those identities and
stored logic; it is not an independent mathematical adjudication. Six
initially missing reasoning-caveat fields were clarified in a separately
attributed supplement without altering the original fields, scores or
verdicts. No coverage or annotation defect remains unresolved in the
final receipt. {[}S7{]}

For the eleven event questions, reconciliation read all 88 initial
outputs and all 38 primary-success fresh outputs in full. All 126
explicit final-number extractions are faithful and uncapped. Two
unit-sensitive non-events add sixteen inspected initial outputs. This is
not a claim to have validated reasoning in every one of the 1,536 fresh
responses. The first audit identified five frozen-v2 extraction false
negatives and no extraction false positives within its reviewed set;
these selected-case observations do not establish population parser
precision. {[}S2, S3, S5, S7{]}

The conservative witness rule requires a reference value supported in
both rounds without material ambiguity, eight faithful uncapped initial
claims independently judged wrong, and at least one faithful uncapped
fresh primary-success claim independently judged numerically correct.
Sound final arithmetic and sound visible reasoning are recorded
separately. No post-review filtering changes the 64-question
denominator.

Scientific source labels are keyed in Appendix G. The reported
generation configuration and manifest (S12 and S13) have unavailable
exact-byte checksum bindings in this package; their settings above are
reported provenance rather than a fresh verification of those source
bytes. Operational smoke provenance (S10) is not supplied as a
scientific source in this derivative. This design is distinct from the
historical V2 diagnostic.



\chapter{Implementation Details}\label{implementation-details}

This chapter describes what was built. Its organising claim is that the
engineering contribution of the project is a \textbf{dispatch and
evidence layer} rather than a training algorithm: the algorithmic
content of GRPO is inherited from the literature, but the work of making
six reinforcement-learning frameworks and six compute backends
addressable through one Tinker-style interface --- and of recording the
evidence needed to trace outputs to their configuration and artefacts,
while marking incomplete historical bindings --- is where the
implementation effort sits. The chapter proceeds from the abstraction
(the adapter architecture), through the concrete training loop, to the
two engineering episodes that most shaped the codebase (the parallel
logging defect and the Colab white-box path), and finally to the
evaluation harness and the recovery re-implementations undertaken after
an execution-source loss in the final phase of the project (Figure~\ref{fig:receipts}).


\fig{fig_receipt_pipeline}
{The evidence chain from sealed request through execution and native grading to a deterministic ledger check}
{The evidence chain from sealed request through execution and native grading to a deterministic ledger check. A break anywhere in the chain prevents promotion to a fully execution-verified result. Recovered summaries remain separately labelled with their limits. Upper row: the chain; the 11/11 PASS badge is the 2026-09-19 deterministic ledger check of eleven named claims, not of every figure (\longtok{LEDGER\_\allowbreak{}CODE\_\allowbreak{}CHECK\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-19.json}). Lower row: the E1 wave-10 recovery, where the lost execution source stops the chain.}
{fig:receipts}



\section{Two Rosters, One Dispatch
Surface}\label{two-rosters-one-dispatch-surface}

A reader approaching the project's comparison material will encounter
two different framework rosters, and conflating them is the single most
likely misreading of the implementation. The internal reports' shared
methods note states the distinction explicitly: the cross-launcher
LLM-RL scaffold referenced in the framework-gap discussion is a
seven-library roster --- Tinker, TRL, SkyRL, veRL, OpenRLHF, Atropos,
and HF reference launchers --- that targets LLM post-training launchers
specifically, while the cross-RL-library evidence reported elsewhere
uses a different roster. Critically for this chapter, the same note
records that ``only Tinker and TRL produced completed runs in this
release; veRL and OpenRLHF entries in the
\texttt{framework\_comparison.json} artefact are dry-run placeholders''.
The veRL and OpenRLHF entries in that artefact carry peak and
last-ten-average reward values, but they are the output of the harness
resolving a launch plan, not the output of a training run, and they must
never be pooled with real results or read as framework comparison
evidence.

Atropos occupies a different position again. Rather than being a cell in
the dispatch matrix, it is integrated through a dedicated package under
\texttt{platform\_tinker/atropos/}, a \texttt{tinker\_atropos} module
containing \texttt{config.py}, \texttt{api.py}, \texttt{trainer.py},
\texttt{types.py}, \texttt{dataset.py}, \texttt{stats\_utils.py}, and an
\texttt{environments/} directory with per-task environment servers. Its
integration model is the Atropos managed-server pattern: a
three-terminal workflow in which \texttt{run-api} starts the Atropos
API, \texttt{launch\_training.py\ -\/-config\ configs/default.yaml}
starts Tinker training against a \longtok{TINKER\_\allowbreak{}API\_\allowbreak{}KEY}, and an
environment server --- a built-in one or any external Atropos
environment pointed at the same config file --- serves rollouts. The
README's quickstart example runs a fifty-step training run on
Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct against a GSM8K environment, and the environment
contract is that the \texttt{env} and \texttt{openai} sections are
consumed by the environment server while the \texttt{tinker} section is
consumed by the trainer and ignored by the environment. This separation
is what allows an arbitrary existing Atropos environment to be reused
against the Tinker backend without modification to the environment.

\section{The Adapter Architecture: What versus
Where}\label{the-adapter-architecture-what-versus-where}

The dispatch surface is built on one orthogonal decomposition, stated in
the backends module docstring: ``A \texttt{Backend} knows \textbf{where}
to run one experiment cell (local GPU, Modal, Colab, vast.ai, GCP, HF
Spaces); the framework dimension (trl/tinker/verl/openrlhf/skyrl) is the
\textbf{what}. \texttt{plan()} resolves a cell into a concrete
\texttt{LaunchPlan} without spending compute, so \texttt{-\/-dry-run}
and the matrix test work uniformly''. The \texttt{LaunchPlan} dataclass
carries \texttt{backend}, \texttt{framework}, \texttt{command},
\texttt{driver\_file}, \texttt{output}, \texttt{env}, and
\texttt{notes}, with a \texttt{format()} method for printing; the
default \texttt{\_execute} runs
\texttt{subprocess.run(plan.command,\allowbreak{}\ shell=\allowbreak{}True,\allowbreak{}\ check=\allowbreak{}True)}. Because
plan resolution is separated from execution, the entire matrix can be
validated without a GPU, an API key, or a dollar of spend --- a property
that the dry-run placeholders discussed above depend on, and which is
also the reason those placeholders must be labelled as such (Figure~\ref{fig:arch}).


\fig{fig_architecture}
{Six RL frameworks behind one Tinker-style API}
{Six RL frameworks behind one Tinker-style API. Five of the six are in the dispatch matrix (5 frameworks $\times$ 6 compute backends); only two executed (Tinker, TRL: last-10 mean training reward on Qwen/Qwen3-8B GSM8K, from \texttt{framework\_comparison.json}), two are dry-run launch plans and two were not executed. One configuration drives every framework, but the executed comparison bundled a different base checkpoint (Chapter~7), so outcome differences are reported with the stack rather than attributed to it.}
{fig:arch}



The matrix itself ships as data.
\longtok{platform\_local/unified/matrix.json} declares schema
\longtok{tinkerrl-framework-backend-matrix-v1} with thirty cells --- five
frameworks (trl, tinker, verl, openrlhf, skyrl) crossed with six
backends (local, modal, colab, vast, gcp, hfspaces), five framework
cells per backend and six backend cells per framework --- and a
\texttt{canonical\_experiment} block that pins the single configuration
every cell is meant to instantiate: \texttt{Qwen/Qwen3-8B} on GSM8K with
GRPO, thirty training steps, \texttt{num\_generations} 8, per-device
batch size 2, gradient accumulation 8, learning rate \texttt{1e-06},
LoRA rank 16 and alpha 32 over all linear modules, \texttt{beta} 0.0,
\texttt{epsilon} 0.2, maximum completion length 1024, and seed 211. Each
cell is a concrete plan naming a framework, a backend, the launch
command, the driver file and an output location; for example, the
\texttt{trl}/\texttt{local} cell runs
\texttt{python\ -m\ platform\_local.unified\ -\/-backend\ local\ -\/-framework\ trl}
and dispatches in-process to
\longtok{platform\_local/trl\_integrations/trainer.py}.

The launcher implements the dispatch. \texttt{UnifiedLauncher} holds
three registries: \texttt{FRAMEWORKS} mapping \texttt{skyrl},
\texttt{tinker}, \texttt{verl}, \texttt{openrlhf}, and \texttt{trl} to
human-readable labels; \texttt{BACKENDS} mapping \texttt{local},
\texttt{modal}, \texttt{colab}, \texttt{vast}, \texttt{gcp}, and
\texttt{hfspaces} to labels; and \texttt{ALGORITHMS} covering
\texttt{grpo}, \texttt{ppo}, \texttt{reinforce}, \texttt{dapo}, and
\texttt{dpo}. Dispatch is outer-on-backend and inner-on-framework, which
is why the per-framework handlers can each be written against the
framework's own idioms. \texttt{\_run\_tinker} shells to
\texttt{python\ -\allowbreak{}m\ platform\_\allowbreak{}tinker.tinkerrl.grpo\_\allowbreak{}cli\ -\allowbreak{}\/\allowbreak{}-\allowbreak{}preset\ \textless{}spec.task\textgreater{}};
\texttt{\_run\_openrlhf} delegates to
\texttt{platform\_\allowbreak{}modal/\allowbreak{}openrlhf/\allowbreak{}trainer.py:\allowbreak{}run\_\allowbreak{}\allowbreak{}openrlhf\_\allowbreak{}\allowbreak{}training(\{\}\allowbreak{},\allowbreak{}\ output\_\allowbreak{}dir=\allowbreak{}"\allowbreak{}./\allowbreak{}checkpoints/\allowbreak{}openrlhf"\allowbreak{})},
which drives
\texttt{openrlhf.cli.train\_\allowbreak{}\allowbreak{}ppo\_\allowbreak{}\allowbreak{}ray\ -\allowbreak{}\/\allowbreak{}-\allowbreak{}advantage\_\allowbreak{}estimator\ group\_\allowbreak{}norm};
\texttt{\_run\_trl} generates a resumable GRPO training script through
\longtok{trl\_integrations.trainer.generate\_\allowbreak{}trl\_\allowbreak{}train\_\allowbreak{}script} into a
temporary directory and executes it; \texttt{\_run\_verl} imports
\texttt{verl.config} and \texttt{verl.trainer} directly; and
\texttt{\_run\_skyrl} requires a \texttt{SKYRL\_CHECKOUT} environment
variable and emits
\texttt{uv\ run\ -\/-extra\ gpu\ -\/-extra\ tinker\ -m\ skyrl.tinker.api}.
For the \texttt{local} and \texttt{colab} backends the launcher runs
\texttt{dispatch\_framework()} in-process, a path the code comments
identify as the one that breaks the Colab entry's self-recursion --- an
implementation detail worth recording because it indicates that the
backend and framework dimensions are not perfectly orthogonal in
practice, and the launcher carries framework-specific knowledge
precisely at the points where they intersect.

Fairness across frameworks is addressed by configuration auditing rather
than by assertion. The
\longtok{platform\_hybrid/experiments/framework\_\allowbreak{}config\_\allowbreak{}dumps/}
supplement ships one proposed configuration template per framework for
the intended Qwen3-8B + GSM8K comparison, and the accompanying README
recomputes the field partition from those templates: of 44 tracked leaf
fields, 25 are identical across all four frameworks (LoRA rank, alpha,
target modules and dropout; GRPO group size, rollouts per group and max
new tokens; sampling temperature, top-p, top-k and do-sample; optimizer
name, learning rate, weight decay, betas and epsilon; reward type; the
task, seed and source-git-sha metadata; four PPO settings; and bf16),
while 11 are Tinker-managed --- serialised as
\texttt{null\ \ \#\ managed\_\allowbreak{}\allowbreak{}by\_\allowbreak{}\allowbreak{}tinker} in the Tinker dump --- covering
\texttt{kl.beta}, \texttt{kl.surrogate}, importance-sampling
granularity, reference-model offload and recompute, tokens per optimizer
step, reward broadcast precision, gradient checkpointing, micro-batch
size, gradient accumulation, and tensor-parallel size. The same README
records two disclaimers that matter more than the identical count:
\texttt{model} and \texttt{model\_sha256} are \emph{not} identical,
because the Tinker template names \texttt{Qwen/Qwen3-8B-Base} while TRL,
veRL and OpenRLHF use the instruction-tuned \texttt{Qwen/Qwen3-8B}, and
\texttt{model\_sha256} is a placeholder rather than a verified checksum.
The partition is exhaustive, 25 + 11 + 8 = 44 (recomputed from the four
YAMLs by
\longtok{tools/review\_ledger/Thesis\_\allowbreak{}2026\_\allowbreak{}10\_\allowbreak{}11\_\allowbreak{}ESA\_\allowbreak{}Corrected\_\allowbreak{}config\_\allowbreak{}partition.py}).
Within the 11 Tinker-managed fields the three open frameworks also
differ among themselves: KL beta is 0.04 for TRL and veRL against 0.02
for OpenRLHF, and importance-sampling granularity is token-level for TRL
and veRL against sequence-level for OpenRLHF. The remaining eight fields
differ across the four templates and are documented rather than
harmonised: \texttt{model} and \texttt{model\_sha256} (above), framework
name and version, reference-model placement (\texttt{on\_device},
\texttt{managed}, \texttt{separate\_process} and \texttt{sharded\_ray}),
GPU memory utilisation, inference backend and number of minibatches. The
appendix's original ``byte-identical'' framing is retracted on the
strength of this partition; the accurate statement is that a majority of
template fields agree, without proving matched execution; the TRL
code/dump rank conflict remains unresolved, a known Base-versus-Instruct
confound survives, and eleven fields are not under user control at the
Tinker API level at all.

\section{The Custom-Loss Tinker Reference
Loop}\label{the-custom-loss-tinker-reference-loop}

The reference implementation of the training loop lives in
\longtok{platform\_tinker/tinkerrl/grpo.py} and is exposed through
\texttt{grpo\_cli.py}. The loop follows the standard four-stage shape
recorded in the Phase-1 report under ``The Tinker GRPO Loop'': sample a
group of \texttt{G} completions per prompt, compute group-relative
advantages, form the loss, and take an optimiser step. By default
advantages are group z-scores, \(A_i=(r_i-\mu_g)/(\sigma_g+10^{-8})\)
with the population \(\sigma_g\) (\texttt{normalize\_rewards}); with
\texttt{unbiased=True} they are the unscaled centred rewards
\(A_i=r_i-\mu_g\) (the Dr.~GRPO standard-deviation fix). The loss is not
the clipped, KL-regularised surrogate of the original GRPO paper. It is
REINFORCE with a group-mean baseline (z-scored by default),

\[L(\theta) = -\frac{1}{N} \sum_{i=1}^{N} A_i \sum_{t=1}^{T_i} \log \pi_\theta(y_{i,t} \mid x, y_{i,<t}) \;+\; \lambda_{\mathrm{NLL}}\Bigl(-\frac{1}{|M|}\sum_{i\in M}\frac{1}{T_i}\sum_{t=1}^{T_i}\log \pi_\theta(y_{i,t}\mid x,y_{i,<t})\Bigr)\]

where \(T_i\) is the passed response length, so only response tokens
enter both sums, \(M\) is the optional set of masked (correct) responses
and \(\lambda_{\mathrm{NLL}}\) the optional auxiliary coefficient
(default 0, which removes the second term). The policy term is a token
sum per response averaged over the \(N\) completions, with no importance
ratio, no clipping, no KL term and no reference policy. The inspected
current source masks the loss to response tokens and refreshes the
behaviour sampler every training step. Earlier candidate implementations
used full-sequence scoring or checkpoint-only refresh, but their binding
to the historical actor is unknown. The response-mask repair is dated 3
October 2026, after the campaign actor; it cannot retroactively certify
that run. Optional GSPO and auxiliary paths in the current source are
outside this default-path description. The implementation uses the
Tinker SDK's \texttt{ServiceClient} with
\longtok{create\_\allowbreak{}lora\_\allowbreak{}training\_\allowbreak{}client},
\longtok{forward\_\allowbreak{}backward\_\allowbreak{}custom} with
\longtok{loss\_\allowbreak{}type\_\allowbreak{}input="logprobs"} so that per-token
log-probabilities flow into a custom loss, \texttt{optim\_step} with
\texttt{AdamParams}, and \texttt{sample()} for rollouts; resumption uses
\longtok{create\_\allowbreak{}training\_\allowbreak{}client\_\allowbreak{}from\_\allowbreak{}state\_\allowbreak{}with\_\allowbreak{}optimizer}.

This custom-loss implementation is distinct from
\longtok{scripts/n2\_\allowbreak{}reward\_\allowbreak{}tensor\_\allowbreak{}20260704.py} and
\longtok{scripts/qp7\_\allowbreak{}adaptive\_\allowbreak{}g\_\allowbreak{}20260704.py}, which pass sampler
log-probabilities into the SDK \texttt{importance\_sampling} loss and
use mean-centred advantages without standard-deviation division. They
have ratio terms but no configured clipping. Table 4.2 lists these as
separate runner families. An SDK loss identifier establishes the
requested interface, not independently verified server internals or a
binding to the historical campaign actor. Conversely, the minimal PPO
comparator has a sequence-level clipped ratio objective: missing GAE and
critic warm-up make it a limited baseline, not an unclipped REINFORCE
implementation.

The single most consequential property of this loop for the project's
research questions is the degenerate case. When the group's reward
standard deviation \(\sigma_g\) is zero, every advantage \(A_i\) is
zero, and because the loss has no KL or auxiliary term, the group
contributes exactly zero gradient. This is the mechanism behind the
zero-variance-fraction diagnostic that runs through the project; it is
recorded in the Phase-1 report as the observation that motivated the
collapse analysis, and it is the reason \texttt{zero\_loss} and
\texttt{zero\_reward} are first-class outputs of the training CLI rather
than derived quantities. Zero reward is counted as an average reward
equal to zero; the available implementation in
\longtok{platform\_tinker/tinkerrl/grpo.py} increments
\longtok{zero\_\allowbreak{}loss\_\allowbreak{}steps} when
\texttt{abs(loss\_val)\ \textless{}\ 1e-6}, and the CLI prints that
counter. The exact predicate/source binding of the historical actor
receipt remains unverified; NFR-9 is Not Met for that historical actor,
and its count is not a count of zero-gradient or zero-parameter-update
steps.

The CLI exposes eight presets --- \texttt{tooluse\_synth},
\texttt{tooluse\_baseline}, \texttt{tooluse\_heldout},
\texttt{tooluse\_xlam}, \texttt{gsm8k}, \texttt{math100},
\texttt{pavlov\_xlam}, and \texttt{pavlov\_portfolio} --- each resolving
a dataset factory and a reward function through a
\texttt{DATASET\_FACTORIES} registry and a \texttt{REWARD\_MAP}
(\texttt{ExactMathReward} for GSM8K, \texttt{StrictToolCallReward} for
\texttt{pavlov\_xlam}, \texttt{PavlovNonXLAMReward} for
\texttt{pavlov\_portfolio}). Tracking and campaign gates are enforced
before a run starts, through \texttt{config.validate\_tracking()} and
\longtok{config.validate\_\allowbreak{}campaign\_\allowbreak{}gate()}, and the CLI refuses to
launch without a \longtok{TINKER\_\allowbreak{}API\_\allowbreak{}KEY}. Each seed's result is
printed as a structured record containing \texttt{run\_id},
\texttt{sampler}, \texttt{avg\_first5}, \texttt{avg\_last10},
\texttt{peak\_reward}, \texttt{zero\_loss}, \texttt{zero\_reward},
\texttt{heldout\_reward}, and \texttt{reward\_trace}, so that a run's
evidence is emitted at the point of execution rather than reconstructed
afterwards. The eight presets replaced an earlier collection of
standalone \texttt{grpo\_*.py} scripts, with \texttt{legacy\_main}
retained to translate the old flag surface.

\section{The Parallel Runner and a Real Logging
Defect}\label{the-parallel-runner-and-a-real-logging-defect}

Sweep execution is handled by \texttt{parallel\_sweep.py} and
\texttt{campaign.py}, with the runner's design shaped directly by a
defect found during development. The defect is documented in the
group-size sweep findings and is worth quoting in full, because it is
the clearest illustration in the project of why logging infrastructure
deserves the same scrutiny as training code:

\begin{quote}
The threaded runner trains all G in parallel correctly, BUT
\texttt{wandb} uses a thread-unsafe global \texttt{run} --- with 4
threads calling \texttt{wandb.init(reinit=True)}, the G8/G16
\textbf{summary} writes failed (``Run is finished'') after another
thread finished its run. Per-step \texttt{wandb.log} mostly survived;
the summary dict didn't. \textbf{Fix for next parallel batch:} isolate
each run in its own PROCESS (multiprocessing / subprocess per config),
not a thread --- or use \texttt{wandb-core} service mode with explicit
per-run objects. Training itself was unaffected (all step data is in the
run log).
\end{quote}

Two aspects of this record are instructive. First, the failure was
silent with respect to training: steps completed and per-step metrics
were written; the logging diagnosis does not independently demonstrate
gradient correctness. Only the run summary --- the aggregated dictionary
from which a results table would be built --- was lost, which is
precisely the class of failure that corrupts downstream reporting while
leaving the primary artefact intact. Second, the remedy adopted was
structural rather than retry-based: isolating each run in its own
process removes the shared mutable global entirely, whereas retrying the
summary write would have left the race in place. The finding also notes
the alternative of \texttt{wandb-core} service mode with explicit
per-run objects, which would preserve in-process execution at the cost
of depending on service-mode semantics that the project chose not to
rely on.

The same findings file documents the group-size sweep that exposed the
defect, and its numbers are reported here with the qualifications the
source attaches to them. The sweep ran \texttt{G\ ∈\ \{2,\ 4,\ 8,\ 16\}}
on Qwen3.5-4B for six steps on GSM8K at seed 0 under the W\&B group
\texttt{p3-groupsize-sweep}, with total token counts of 9,663, 19,964,
34,084, and 68,055 respectively. At \texttt{G\ =\ 2} the run showed a
50\% batch-level collapse with a \texttt{+0.000} gain; at
\texttt{G\ =\ 4} a 17\% collapse with a \texttt{+0.125} gain. The source
flags the \texttt{G\ =\ 4} gain explicitly as supported by one example
at n = 8 on a single seed and therefore \emph{suggestive rather than
proven}, and records that the independent verification of the sweep
returned the verdict SUSPECT. These are not results this thesis claims;
they are the observations that motivated the group-size investigation
and that the later controlled work supersedes, and they are reported in
the register in which they were recorded.

\section{The Colab White-Box Path (Per-Layer Gradient
Profile)}\label{the-colab-white-box-path-per-layer-gradient-profile}

The per-layer gradient profile --- numbered P1 in the Phase-1 series,
and distinct from the P1 cross-scale study of Chapter 6 --- asks a
question the managed Tinker backend cannot answer. Per-layer gradient
norms require access to the model's internals during the backward pass,
and the managed service exposes no such interface. The implementation
therefore leaves the managed path entirely and runs on a Colab L4 GPU in
\longtok{platform\_hybrid/experiments/openings/p1\_\allowbreak{}layer\_\allowbreak{}profile.py},
using Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct with LoRA adapters on the q/k/v/o
projections, five GRPO-style advantage-weighted steps, and per-layer
LoRA gradient norms recorded at each step. The white-box path is thus a
deliberate architectural exception: the project accepts the loss of
managed-scale training in exchange for gradient visibility, and confines
the exception to a question that only gradient visibility can address.

The first-pass result appeared strong. Across 28 layers, the top-k layer
set identified at step 1 overlapped the final top-k set perfectly, with
a measured overlap of 1.0; the top 25\% of layers accounted for a 0.476
share of gradient mass; layer 0 carried a gradient norm of 63 against
6--17 elsewhere, and a secondary mid-late band appeared at layers
17--23. A scaled re-test disciplined this result. The re-run used
\longtok{p1\_\allowbreak{}layer\_\allowbreak{}profile\_\allowbreak{}scaled.py} on Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct, with 24
real GSM8K problems, ten steps, \texttt{G\ =\ 4}, 36 layers, and seeds
\{0, 1\}. The stored step-1 overlap fell to 0.111, and the top-25\%
concentration measured 0.39 with a standard deviation of 0.013. The
source's verdict is unambiguous: the original predictability finding
``was an artifact of the toy 1.5B/hardcoded-arithmetic setup''. A later
four-seed pass at the same configuration (seeds \{0, 1, 2, 3\})
supersedes the two-seed figure and gives a mean overlap of 0.083 ± 0.048
(mean ± SD), with concentration 0.39. The implementation compares the
top nine of 36 layers at step 1 with the top nine by gradient norm
summed over all ten steps, including step 1; it does not compare with an
independent final-step target. For two independent uniformly selected
nine-layer sets, expected intersection divided by nine is 9/36 = 0.25,
so describing 0.111 or 0.083 as the chance expectation was incorrect.
The observed overlap is below that simple reference, without
establishing a general anticorrelation or a statistically calibrated
null.

A companion freeze test asked whether the layer profile could be
exploited: if a small band of layers dominates the gradient, freezing
the rest should preserve the benefit at lower cost. The small comparison
did not establish performance preservation. The full 28-layer
configuration trained 2.18M parameters and produced a mean held-out gain
of \texttt{+0.0625} (seed 0 \texttt{+0.125}, seed 1 0.0). Freezing to a
hot band comprising layer 0 plus layers 12--27 --- 17 layers, 1.32M
parameters, or 0.61 times the full count --- produced \texttt{+0.000}.
The source concludes that ``aggressive freezing loses the
benefit\ldots{} The freeze lever does not work at this scale'', and
records that the comparison is underpowered at n = 8 held-out examples
over 2 seeds. The layer-ranking result did not replicate in the tested
3B configuration, which also changed task and recipe; scale is not
isolated as the cause. The two-seed, eight-example freezing comparison
is inconclusive about performance preservation or ineffectiveness. Both
observations are retained with those limits.

\section{Software Modules}\label{software-modules}

The following inventory describes the RL-related modules relevant to
this thesis. The unified launcher,
\longtok{platform\_local/unified/launcher.py}, maps one configuration
onto a chosen backend; \longtok{platform\_tinker/tinkerrl/grpo.py} is the
group-standardised REINFORCE reference loop against the managed backend;
\longtok{platform\_local/trl\_integrations/} and the \texttt{verl/}
integration hold the per-framework adapters, with
\texttt{platform\_modal/openrlhf/} and \texttt{platform\_hybrid/skyrl/}
completing the roster, and \texttt{platform\_tinker/atropos/} carrying
the Atropos integration described above. Analysis code is separated from
training code by intent: \longtok{p2\_\allowbreak{}collapse\_\allowbreak{}analysis.py} recomputes
zero-variance fraction and collapse statistics from stored reward
tensors, so the diagnostic is reproducible from evidence rather than
from a live run; \longtok{p1\_\allowbreak{}layer\_\allowbreak{}profile.py} implements the white-box
gradient probe; \texttt{parallel\_sweep.py} and \texttt{campaign.py}
drive sweeps.

The unrelated synthetic supervised fraud study is separated in Appendix
F and is not evidence for the RL research questions. Beyond these
modules, the \texttt{platform\_local/unified/} package carries
\texttt{canonical.py} for the canonical configuration and
\texttt{peft\_utils.py} for adapter handling, and the backends package
implements one module per backend (\texttt{local.py}, \texttt{modal.py},
\texttt{colab.py}, \texttt{vast.py}, \texttt{gcp.py},
\texttt{hfspaces.py}) behind the common \texttt{base.py} interface.

\section{The E1--E14 Evaluation
Harness}\label{the-e1e14-evaluation-harness}

\textbf{Measured-run boundary.} Implemented controls are distinct from
controls active in scored executions. The 27 September replacement
measurements used separate runners under the documented override (§5.9);
offline controller tests do not certify those runs. Runner revision,
active/bypassed gates, retry rules and retained artifacts must be
established per lane, with unresolved bindings left unknown (Figure~\ref{fig:harness}).


\fig{fig_harness}
{Evaluation-pipeline stages and their evidence boundaries}
{Evaluation-pipeline stages and their evidence boundaries. Prospective fail-closed gates are distinguished from the recorded historical executions, including six exploratory protocol-override replacements on 27 September. Offline gate tests do not authenticate lost training source or prove that a gate governed a measured run.}
{fig:harness}



The project implemented and tested a completion gate,
\longtok{zvf-program/flagship/e1\_\allowbreak{}e14\_\allowbreak{}completion\_\allowbreak{}gate.py}, schema
\texttt{e1-e14-completion-gate-v1}. The gate maintains five evidence
classes --- \texttt{COMPLETE\_EXACT}, \texttt{PARTIAL\_EXACT},
\texttt{PARTIAL\_RECOVERY}, \texttt{BLOCKED\_EXTERNAL}, and
\longtok{LOCAL\_\allowbreak{}SETUP\_\allowbreak{}READY\_\allowbreak{}PROVIDER\_\allowbreak{}INPUT\_\allowbreak{}REQUIRED} --- and maps
lane source statuses onto them, with unknown states failing closed
rather than defaulting to a classification. Its \texttt{LANE\_SPECS}
table binds each lane to a replacement scope, a task count, and the
runner or adapter that produces its evidence: E2 FrontierSWE at 17 tasks
(\longtok{pavlov\_\allowbreak{}frontier\_\allowbreak{}swe\_\allowbreak{}eval\_\allowbreak{}adapter.py},
\longtok{frontier\_\allowbreak{}swe\_\allowbreak{}eval.py}), E3 SDAB at 80, E4 BankerToolBench at
100 (\longtok{run\_\allowbreak{}harbor\_\allowbreak{}via\_\allowbreak{}tinker\_\allowbreak{}bridge.py}), E5 APEX-Agents at
480, E7 BinaryAudit at 46 (\longtok{modal\_\allowbreak{}non\_\allowbreak{}e11\_\allowbreak{}readiness.py}), E9
MLE-bench at 75 (\longtok{e9\_\allowbreak{}mle\_\allowbreak{}bench\_\allowbreak{}streaming.py}), E12 AppBench at
6, and lanes E6, E8, E10, E13 and E14 with their respective runners. The
gate's own header states its scope precisely: it neither invokes an
adapter nor accepts a package as a benchmark result.

The results the gate classifies are recorded in
\longtok{outputs/E1\_\allowbreak{}E14\_\allowbreak{}FINAL\_\allowbreak{}RESULTS\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-19.md}, verified by
eleven named deterministic checks on 2026-09-19 (scope in Appendix B)
and bound to surviving receipts. The single Tinker-trained actor is base
\texttt{Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B} at commit \texttt{995ad96e…} with adapter
\texttt{campaign-adapter} at commit \texttt{withheld-adapter-revision…},
in bfloat16. The document's opening constraint governs how everything
below may be read: original-contract and replacement-scope numbers are
never pooled, and per the project evidence rule no cross-suite average
is formed at any point.

The per-lane results the gate classified are reported once, in Chapter 8
(Tables 8.1--8.6), with each lane's scope and terminal state in Table
C.3; they are not repeated here.

A later secondary record alleges an E4 base-model rerun. Its original
job receipt and trajectories remain unavailable, and contradictory
chronology prevents admission of its aggregate as a measured result. The
bridge has a documented missing-stop failure mode, but its contribution
to the aggregate zero is unidentified (§8.5 and Appendix D.7). Precise
termination ranges, role-token counts, runtime and token totals are not
established by the recovered pending-state ledger and are not presented
as verified execution facts here.

\section{The Bridge and Sampler Path}\label{the-bridge-and-sampler-path}

Runners reach a Tinker-trained model through
\longtok{zvf-program/flagship/modal\_\allowbreak{}tinker\_\allowbreak{}openai\_\allowbreak{}bridge.py}, a
Modal-deployed OpenAI-compatible bridge whose own header states that
``the deployed bridge is infrastructure, not a benchmark result. Its
default paid-inference budget is zero''. The bridge declares app name
\texttt{campaign-serving-bridge}, model alias \texttt{campaign-actor}
over model id \texttt{Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B}, and pins its sampler at
{[}operational identifier withheld{]}, with the Hugging Face side pinned
by commit \texttt{withheld-adapter-revision}. Runtime limits are 16,384
maximum completion tokens against a 65,536-token model context, with an
8,192-token context prefix reserved; \longtok{DEFAULT\_\allowbreak{}MAX\_\allowbreak{}USD} is
\texttt{Decimal("0.00")}, so every paid path must be opted into
explicitly at launch. The pinned dependency set is \texttt{fastapi}
0.116.1, \texttt{huggingface-hub} 1.27.0, \texttt{tinker} 0.30.0,
\texttt{transformers} 5.5.4, and \texttt{wandb} 0.21.0.

The architectural point of the bridge is that it converts a Tinker
sampler into an OpenAI-compatible endpoint. A harness written against
the OpenAI chat-completions API --- which most agentic benchmark
harnesses are --- can therefore be pointed at a Tinker-trained actor
without modification, and the retained E4 diagnostic code addresses its
\texttt{/v1/responses} form. Execution of the alleged later rerun is not
authenticated by that code. The same pattern appears in the actor-side
configuration of the E1 recovery wave, where the actor is addressed as
an OpenAI-compatible chat-completions service at
\texttt{http://127.0.0.1:18015/v1} (container port 8000) under model
name \texttt{campaign-actor-bf16}, with \texttt{max\_tokens} 8192,
temperature 0, seed 809, top-p 0.95, and
\texttt{chat\_\allowbreak{}\allowbreak{}template\_\allowbreak{}\allowbreak{}kwargs\ \{"\allowbreak{}enable\_\allowbreak{}thinking"\allowbreak{}:\allowbreak{}\ false\}}. The
bridge and the local actor endpoint are two deployments of the same
interface contract, and keeping that contract stable across deployment
modes is what allows one harness to serve both.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Recovery Re-implementations under
\texttt{zvf-program/}}{Recovery Re-implementations under zvf-program/}}\label{recovery-re-implementations-under-zvf-program}

In the final phase of the project an execution-source loss --- deletion
of the historical temporary execution workspace directory --- removed
the finish-era recovery and build code, confined in scope to the E1
wave10 controller, the E9 restored dependencies, and the E6 validator,
while the replacement-lane native runners survived under
\texttt{zvf-program/flagship/}. Surviving requests, receipts and tests
constrain a replacement implementation and document known departures;
they do not establish behavioral equivalence to the lost implementation.
Four such re-implementations are worth describing, because together they
show what the evidence discipline looks like when it is expressed as
code rather than as convention. None of them produced a benchmark
number; their value is that their safety properties are testable
offline.

The companion E2 \texttt{launch\_gate.py} has a narrow check: it checks
only that the amendment acceptance record and the surviving CORE-Bench
harness entry points exist, which keeps the gate's claim small enough to
be true.

Read together, these four artefacts are the implementation answer to a
question the results chapters raise: how a project maintains evidential
integrity when its execution environment is partly lost and partly
unreachable. The answer taken here is to re-derive the controllers from
the sealed requests and the surviving test records, to keep every
re-implementation offline-testable, and to make the no-retry, no-replay,
hash-pinned, fail-closed properties explicit in code so that they can be
demonstrated rather than asserted. The cost of that discipline is
visible: four controllers, three of them complete and one a deliberate
stub, produced no new benchmark numbers in this phase. That is the
intended trade.

None of the four controllers ran the lanes in the end. On 27 September
2026 a single lead authorisation superseded the sealed-reservation and
owner-binding gates they enforce, and each lane was run with a thin
runner that calls the benchmark's native loop and grader directly. The
runners live under
\longtok{outputs/finish\_\allowbreak{}pending\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-27/\textless{}lane\textgreater{}/code/},
and each lane's \texttt{result.json} lists the bypass as a deviation
(§8.4). These fail-closed properties therefore describe offline-tested
replacement designs, not controls shown to have governed the measured
replacement lanes. Authorisation to take a different path does not
establish methodological equivalence. The campaign is reported as a
pipeline demonstration with scoped receipts and unresolved training
provenance (§4.5).



\part{Results}


\chapter{Results I: Core GRPO Studies
(P1--P4)}\label{results-i-core-grpo-studies-p1p4}

This chapter reports the core studies P1--P4: cross-scale training
traces, the Zero-Variance Fraction (ZVF), group-size comparisons and
capped response-length behaviour. Its strongest retained observations
concern diagnostic arithmetic and explicitly scoped stored-run
comparisons. Interrupted traces, synthetic projections and
evaluation-contaminated comparisons do not establish empirical scaling,
prediction or algorithm-ranking results (Figure~\ref{fig:scaling}).


\fig{fig_scaling_null}
{Logged-horizon inventory of the twelve historical cross-scale anchors, from the retained cross-scale TSV}
{Logged-horizon inventory of the twelve historical cross-scale anchors, from the retained cross-scale TSV. Five interrupted traces are excluded from scaling fits. The remaining seven are still heterogeneous single-seed records, not a controlled scaling experiment; no regression or asymptotic trend is asserted.}
{fig:scaling}



\section{Scope, Sources and Evidence
Boundary}\label{scope-sources-and-evidence-boundary}

The four pillars share one methodological commitment, which is the
organising principle of the whole project: each comparison names the
intended contrast, the controls actually held fixed and any coupled
changes that limit attribution. Where checkpoint, prompt exposure,
sampling or budget differ, the result describes those executed
configurations rather than an isolated algorithm effect. The reader
should treat this chapter as the primary result surface for P1--P4; the
corresponding historical LaTeX sources are preserved, while this chapter
distinguishes their reported outputs from corrections recomputed from
the named data and inspected analysis code.

Each study draws on a different evidence base, and the differences
matter. P1 historically describes a 70+ run roster across libraries and
model families, but its fitted anchor subset includes interrupted and
single-seed records and is not a verified common-budget population. P2's
retained measured evidence includes three stored sampling tensors and
separately identified measured sweeps. The five-seed, nine-row
mitigation panel is wholly synthetic and is excluded from empirical
prediction and intervention claims. P3's measured sweep covers G ∈ \{2,
4, 8, 16\}; every G = 32 and above number in the pillar is a joint-fit
reconstruction, not a trained arm. P4's controlled comparison is sixteen
runs across two algorithms and two tasks; its roster-level audit is
drawn from a separate, larger pool that mixes models, tasks and
libraries.

Historical marginal one-way ANOVAs were reported for 42 experiments,
with library/framework η² = 0.546, algorithm η² = 0.558, model family η²
= 0.471 and scale η² = 0.322. Their sum, 1.897, is not a partition of
variance. A verified row-level membership and crossed-factor census for
that exact historical subset is unavailable in this audit; these values
therefore do not support the claim that framework dominates algorithm or
estimate independent factor contributions. They are retained only as a
locator for the historical analysis, alongside the unsupported
single-seed comparisons in its twenty-test family. The case for explicit
configuration reporting rests on the observable mismatches described in
Chapters 4--5, not on this decomposition.

Throughout this chapter, ``statistically indistinguishable'' means that
the stated difference test did not detect a difference at its declared
level; it does not mean demonstrated equivalence. Equivalence is
reported only when both one-sided tests reject differences outside a
stated margin. Failure to establish equivalence and failure to detect a
difference are separate outcomes, and can occur together.
