\section{P1 --- Cross-Library and Cross-Scale Evidence
Limits}\label{p1-cross-library-and-cross-scale-evidence-limits}

P1 does not identify a post-training scaling law. Absolute training
reward combines pretrained starting capability with adaptation, whereas
a benefit estimand would compare matched pre- and post-training accuracy
at a common budget,
\(\Delta(N,B)=\mathrm{Acc}_{post}(N,B)-\mathrm{Acc}_{pre}(N)\). The
historical pool supplies neither that common endpoint nor independent
variation of model size and training recipe.

\subsection{Trace inventory and
exclusions}\label{trace-inventory-and-exclusions}

Of twelve cross-scale anchors, five terminate after only three to five
logged steps: Qwen3.5-27B, Qwen3-30B-MoE, Qwen3-30B-MoE-Instruct,
Qwen3-32B and Qwen3-235B-MoE. The other seven provide twenty to thirty
steps. Frontier anchors are single-seed, and learning rate, LoRA rank
and model identity covary with scale. The interrupted traces are
execution records, not completed learning curves or comparable terminal
outcomes.

The historical cross-scale power-law regressions, fitted saturation
rates, changepoints and compute-adjusted proxies are excluded from this
chapter's evidentiary results. Removing five short traces does not
repair the remaining recipe and starting-capability confounding, so no
replacement scaling fit is presented. In particular, a zero-intercept
saturation fit to an already capable pretrained policy cannot identify a
learning rate merely because its fitted parameter reaches an optimiser
boundary. The source analyses remain archived; their numerical slopes
and fit-derived intervals are not evidence for either a scaling law or
its absence.

\subsection{What the trace audit
establishes}\label{what-the-trace-audit-establishes}

The audit establishes missing design support, rather than a statistical
null about scale. Some recorded training rewards fall or fluctuate,
including the single Nemotron-120B trace, but short within-run
observations cannot identify a general learning-phase taxonomy. The
earlier dense-versus-MoE contrast also depends on a misclassified model
and is withdrawn. Categorical agreement does not repair this: where both
labelling procedures assign every trace the same category, Cohen's κ is
undefined because both observed and chance agreement equal one. A
common-budget, replicated pre/post comparison is still required to
answer the original scaling question.

\subsection{Saturated arithmetic as an evaluation-design
audit}\label{saturated-arithmetic-as-an-evaluation-design-audit}

The historical Qwen2.5-0.5B arithmetic PPO/GRPO comparison reports mean
evaluation accuracies of 0.992 and 0.990 respectively across five seeds.
These values are retained only to describe the stored records, not as an
isolated estimator comparison. Each arm drew its 200 evaluation problems
from the stateful training generator after a different number of
training draws. Within seeds, the evaluation sets share only 6, 6, 4, 7
and 3 items. Evaluation items previously drawn for training number 12,
16, 13, 15 and 13 for GRPO, versus 90, 86, 80, 83 and 74 for PPO. The
arms also expose different numbers of distinct training prompts per step
(16 versus 128), and both lie near the accuracy ceiling.

Consequently, the historical difference tests and equivalence tests are
not evidence that the algorithms agree, differ or have equal
performance. Pairing aggregate scores by seed does not restore missing
item pairing or remove unequal training overlap. Later runner code
contains a fixed-disjoint evaluation repair, but that code change does
not repair these stored runs. The unsaturated v1/v2 studies in §6.5.5
have separate evaluation contracts; Table 6.2 remains provisional.

The PPO arm retains its correct implementation description: a
sequence-level clipped policy-ratio objective with a learned value
baseline. Absence of generalised advantage estimation does not make a
clipped PPO surrogate REINFORCE. The critic's training quality and the
exposure mismatch limit the comparison independently of that name.

\section{P2 --- The Zero-Variance
Fraction}\label{p2-the-zero-variance-fraction}

The second pillar asks a mechanistic question: when GRPO stops learning,
is the cause visible in the reward statistics of the groups it samples?
The diagnostic proposed is the Zero-Variance Fraction (Figure~\ref{fig:zvflib}).


\fig{fig_zvf_by_library}
{Measured exact-tie fractions recomputed from the three stored Qwen3-8B GSM8K sampling tensors (200 prompts per seed)}
{Measured exact-tie fractions recomputed from the three stored Qwen3-8B GSM8K sampling tensors (200 prompts per seed). The 95/600 pooled count is descriptive; no synthetic variant panel, imputed AUROC or predictive validation is presented.}
{fig:zvflib}



\subsection{Definition and
measurement}\label{definition-and-measurement}

For a prompt x sampled from the training distribution, GRPO emits G
completions and scores each with a verifier. Centring those rewards
within the group produces the advantage signal. If all G completions
receive the same reward --- all correct, or all incorrect --- every
centred reward is zero and the group contributes no contrastive signal
to that step's update. The exact-tie fraction records how often this
occurs; the declared tolerance rule in §4.3 also counts near-ties. They
agree for the binary rewards below. Gradient utilisation is defined
descriptively as GU = 1 − ZVF.

What a zero-variance group does to the update depends on the loss form.
The source states the general case: in an objective with a KL penalty or
auxiliary loss, a zero-variance group does not make the total gradient
zero, and ZVF measures only the fraction of groups contributing zero
centred reward contrast. An exact-tie group contributes zero to a
verified group-centred policy surrogate, but the full update can include
KL, critic or other auxiliary losses and optimiser state. Table 4.2
contains both nonzero-KL configuration evidence and unknown managed
settings; neither may be relabelled zero. Near-ties counted by the ε =
10⁻⁶ diagnostic need not have exactly zero policy gradients. ZVF
therefore describes reward contrast, not measured useful compute or the
whole update.

The diagnostic arithmetic is checked by recomputation. The thesis
defines its variance convention in §4.3. The retained historical
tolerance/jitter recomputation uses sample variance (ddof = 1) ≤ 10⁻⁶,
whereas historical exact-tie checks and PCD use population variance
(ddof = 0). Its numerical results below are left under that historical
convention, not silently converted to the thesis convention. The Modal
training runners standardise rewards with NumPy's population standard
deviation and log their own near-zero-standard-deviation rule. These
conventions coincide on the binary exact ties here, but are not
interchangeable on continuous or jittered rewards. Three stored GSM8K
group tensors (Qwen3-8B, G = 8, sampling only, 200 problems per seed)
were re-read from disk and their ZVF recomputed from the raw per-group
rewards; the recomputed values are 0.130, 0.190 and 0.155, pooled
0.1583, matching the reported values exactly. This is the mechanical
check that the quantity in the tables is the quantity the code computes.
The earlier ≤0.4\% ε-sensitivity statement did not distinguish exact
ties from thresholded variance and is replaced here by a reproducible
sweep over these same 600 groups. With independent seeded δ
\textasciitilde{} U(0,10⁻⁴) jitter, exact ties fall from 95/600 to
0/600. At sample-variance ε = 10⁻⁹, 71/600 groups remain below
threshold; at ε = 10⁻⁸ through 10⁻², including the declared 10⁻⁶, all
95/600 remain. Unperturbed binary rewards retain 95/600 across this
range. At G = 8, jitter alone has sample variance at most 2.86 × 10⁻⁹,
so it cannot cross 10⁻⁶ in an originally tied group. This is the
theoretical worst case, not an observed maximum: for eight perturbations
in \([0, M]\) with \(M = 10^{-4}\), the unbiased sample variance
(\texttt{ddof=1}, the convention of this historical recomputation) is
maximised with four draws at 0 and four at \(M\), giving
\(\frac{G}{4(G-1)}M^2 = \frac{2}{7}\times10^{-8}\); the
population-variance (\texttt{ddof=0}) bound is \(2.50\times10^{-9}\),
and the expected sample variance of uniform jitter is
\(M^2/12 \approx 8.3\times10^{-10}\). PCD changes by +2.984 × 10⁻⁷. The
example diagnoses exact-tie sensitivity, not failure of tolerance-based
ZVF; it does not establish robustness to larger perturbations or other
reward scales.

\subsection{Measured evidence and excluded
projections}\label{measured-evidence-and-excluded-projections}

The nine-method, five-seed mitigation panel is entirely synthetic,
including its vanilla-GRPO row. The labels AERO, CPPO, NGRPO, SCAFGRPO,
MCGRPO, GIFT, AREAL and ES identify historical simulation rules in this
artifact, not verified independent deployments. Its rankings, synthetic
accuracies and collapse counts are excluded from the empirical results.
The historical 60-row range/autocorrelation analysis contains these 45
simulated rows, so its pooled magnitudes and warning lead times are also
excluded; no measured-only early-warning estimate is supplied by that
analysis. The measured evidence below is identified separately by
source, task and unit. Published CPPO, NGRPO, Scaf-GRPO, AERO, MC-GRPO,
GIFT, AReaL and evolution-strategy methods have their own definitions
\citep{lin2025cppo, nan2025ngrpo, zhang2025scafgrpo, zhang2026aero, kim2026mcgrpo, wang2025gift, fu2025areal, qiu2025es}.
Their existence does not authenticate these proxy implementations; AReaL
is a training system and evolution strategies are not intrinsically GRPO
variants.

\subsection{The all-correct wall and the mastery--incapacity
aliasing}\label{the-all-correct-wall-and-the-masteryincapacity-aliasing}

The mechanism behind large ZVF values on easy tasks is a saturation
effect that the project calls the all-correct wall. A matched-budget
panel was run at 2,560 rollouts per arm --- G = 2 over 160 steps against
G = 16 over 20 steps --- on Qwen3-8B with GSM8K, LoRA rank 4 and batch
size 8. The late G = 2 arms reach a mean training reward of
approximately 1.0 with ZVF between 0.75 and 1.0; the G = 16 arms hold
ZVF at or below 0.25 at the same rollout budget. The reading is that
when the policy solves the task, nearly every group becomes all-correct,
nearly every centred reward is zero, and the estimator loses its
contrast even though the reward signal is at its maximum. Signal
starvation in this regime is driven by ease, not by difficulty --- which
is the opposite of the intuition that motivates variance-mitigation
work.

This produces the pillar's most important structural caveat. ZVF is
unsigned and symmetric: an all-wrong group (\(K_x = 0\)) and an
all-correct group (\(K_x = G\)) score identically. The statistic
therefore cannot distinguish mastery from incapacity, and this aliasing
is a theoretical limitation on its interpretation; the historical pooled
correlation does not separately test the claimed held-out association.
Under an i.i.d.-Bernoulli model the closed form is
\(\mathrm{ZVF}(p_x, G) = h_G(p_x) = p_x^G + (1-p_x)^G\), and it follows
in one line. If the G rewards of a group are independent Bernoulli(p)
draws, the group has zero variance exactly when all G rewards are equal.
That happens when all are 1, with probability \(p^G\), or all are 0,
with probability \((1-p)^G\). The two events are disjoint, so
\(P(\text{zero variance}) = p^G + (1-p)^G\). At \(p = \tfrac12\) this is
\(h_G = 2^{1-G}\), so \(\ln h_G\) falls by \(\ln 2\) for each unit
increase in G. The project's source document for this closed form and
the aliasing analysis is headed as reasoning contributions and proposed
theorems from ChatGPT Pro Extended and Gemini Deep Think, ``NOT
experiments this paper ran''. That provenance is kept on record, and AI
assistance is declared in the front matter, but the result does not rest
on it: the derivation above is elementary and is the argument this
thesis relies on. What the project itself measured is the empirical ZVF
against the closed form, evaluated at the per-step training reward rate
because ZVF is a training-batch quantity. Recomputed from the per-step
training rewards in
\longtok{platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/groupsize\_\allowbreak{}zvf\_\allowbreak{}sweep.json}
(3 seeds × 40 steps per arm), the per-step-average null is 0.828, 0.709,
0.597 and 0.522. The measured mean ZVF is 0.838, 0.764, 0.691 and 0.631,
so the residuals are +0.010, +0.055, +0.094 and +0.109: measured ZVF
sits \emph{above} the null at every G, which is compatible with
heterogeneous prompt difficulty. Jensen's inequality bounds expected
degeneracy at true latent success probabilities, not the sign of a
finite-sample residual against an estimated plug-in baseline
\citep{nie2026starvation}. Gradient utilisation rises across the same
range from 0.162 to 0.369. The magnitude and interpretation of the gap
depend on the baseline; averaging over heterogeneous prompt success
probabilities is part of the formal null in §4.3. Evaluating the closed
form at the pooled training rate understates the mean of the per-step
plug-in predictions, because \(h_G\) is convex:
\(h_G(\overline p_t) \le \overline{h_G(p_t)}\). The per-step-average
plug-in baseline is \(\overline{h_8(p_t)} = 0.597\) at \(G = 8\), rather
than \(h_8(\overline p_t) = 0.326\) at the pooled all-step training mean
\(\overline p_t = 0.869\) (pooled means 0.840, 0.863, 0.869 and 0.873 at
G = 2, 4, 8 and 16; recomputed from the 480 step logs of
\longtok{groupsize\_\allowbreak{}zvf\_\allowbreak{}sweep.json}). This Jensen gap (+0.27 at G = 8)
is distinct from the Table E.1 correction, which replaced an earlier
source's plug-in at held-out accuracy (\(h_8(0.923) \approx 0.527\)) by
the per-step-average baseline. A held-out rate is a different quantity
and has no corresponding ordering guarantee. Even the per-step baseline
uses a batch-average estimate instead of the unknown per-prompt
probabilities in §4.3, so prompt-difficulty heterogeneity and estimation
error remain. Against the per-step-average baseline the measured ZVF at
\(G = 8\) (\(0.69\)) \emph{exceeds} the null by approximately \(0.09\).
The change from the pooled baseline to the per-step baseline is the
Jensen effect; it does not by itself explain the remaining 0.09
residual. The source consequently ``do{[}es{]} not read it as strong
evidence that the independence null is a lower bound''. This chapter
uses only the per-step-average baseline. It is a weak consistency check
on shape rather than evidence about dependence.

The formalisation's own summary of the same sweep, twelve runs and 480
logged steps, reports per-step observed ZVF correlating at r = 0.92 with
the Bernoulli-null prediction \(p^G + (1-p)^G\), with mean ZVF of 0.84,
0.76, 0.69 and 0.63 at G = 2, 4, 8 and 16 respectively. That correlation
is a shape check, not a validation: the null for each step is computed
from the same step's mean reward, so a high r is partly mechanical. At
larger scale the same instrument was run on Qwen3-8B against GSM8K test
problems through the Tinker managed API at G = 8, temperature 1.0, over
200 problems and three seeds (sampling only, no training; these are the
three stored tensors of §6.3.1), yielding a mean ZVF of 0.16 composed of
12.7\% all-correct groups, 3.2\% all-wrong groups and 84\% mixed groups;
the raw correlation with the mechanical null is approximately r = 0.93
with rank ρ ≈ 0.65, and the i.i.d. null over-predicts (approximately
0.28 against the observed 0.16). The direction of the deviation from the
null is therefore opposite in the two runs: above the null on the 0.5B
sweep, below it on this Qwen3-8B sampling run. The source does not state
which baseline produced the 0.28, and neither deviation is read as
evidence about dependence between completions.

A recomputation on the three stored Qwen3-8B tensors (600 groups, pooled
success rate 0.694) separates the candidate explanations and does not
reproduce the 0.28. At the pooled rate the homogeneous binomial null is
\(h_8(0.694) = 0.054\), against 0.158 observed, so the null
\emph{under}-predicts by a factor of about three. A beta-binomial fitted
to the per-group success counts (\(\alpha = 3.61\), \(\beta = 1.62\),
intraclass correlation 0.16) predicts ZVF 0.170, in an exploratory fit.
The repeated prompts across seeds and boundary at zero overdispersion
require an explicitly calibrated inferential procedure; no
likelihood-ratio rejection is claimed here. The fitted agreement shows
that prompt-difficulty heterogeneity can explain the excess, without
identifying it separately from conditional dependence or other latent
variation: to a good approximation ZVF is the mean of \(h_G(p_i)\) over
prompts with heterogeneous success probabilities \(p_i\), which is the
Jensen argument above applied across prompts rather than across steps. A
per-group plug-in residual of +0.1224 that appears in a P2 working note
is withdrawn: it is non-negative by construction (the same
sign-guaranteed quantity as the withdrawn ``anti-herding'' δ\_div of
§7.2, whose expectation is +0.105 under i.i.d. rollouts) and is not
evidence of dependence (recomputation: 16 October 2026 audit of
\longtok{results/tinker\_\allowbreak{}gsm8k\_\allowbreak{}zvf\_\allowbreak{}s\{42,123,456\}.json}).
Contemporaneous work studies the same quantity under other names --- the
Advantage Collapse Rate \citep{he2026avspo} and gradient starvation in
binary-reward GRPO \citep{nie2026starvation}, the latter proving that
heterogeneous prompt difficulty pushes the degeneracy rate above the
i.i.d. Bernoulli prediction --- so ZVF is not claimed here as a new
diagnostic; the contribution is its recomputation from stored tensors
and the reporting discipline around it.

\subsection{Predictive validity remains
unestablished}\label{predictive-validity-remains-unestablished}

The historical residual and collapse analyses mix provenance and
endpoints. Eighty source rows are grouped into 23 cells; six cells lack
ZVF and are excluded, leaving 17 finite cells, nine of which are
synthetic. The outcome field mixes late training reward, sampling and
other source metrics rather than a common held-out endpoint.
Residualising only the outcome is semipartial adjustment and does not
turn these records into an independent predictive test. The historical
coefficients, sample-size-based intervals and held-out interpretation
are therefore excluded from inferential claims.

The 52-row risk-index evaluation is also withdrawn as validation
evidence. Its 45 variance-mitigation rows are simulated, its seven
collapse anchors receive analysis-imputed feature values, and all 41
safe rows are synthetic. The resulting AUROCs and Hanley--McNeil
intervals are removed rather than presented as empirical discrimination.
A post-hoc fusion rule cannot be validated against class features
supplied by the same analysis. No replacement measured-only AUROC,
threshold or confidence interval is asserted.

The retained finding is definitional and directly checkable: ZVF records
observed reward homogeneity and is symmetric between all-correct and
all-wrong groups. Predictive or intervention value needs a separately
identified measured cohort, a common future endpoint, fixed feature and
threshold choices, and held-out runs. Neither ZVF nor PCD has met that
prospective comparison in the evidence reported here.

\section{P3 --- Group Size and Contrast
Density}\label{p3-group-size-and-contrast-density}

The third pillar studies G, which plays two roles simultaneously in
GRPO: it is a variance-reduction knob, like a Monte-Carlo baseline, and
it is a preference-density knob, since centring rewards within a group
induces implicit pairwise contrasts --- nonzero only for mixed-reward
groups. Practitioners often treat larger G as strictly better by analogy
to variance reduction, which conflates the two roles and ignores cost.
The measured evidence does not justify a group-size optimum. The
arithmetic accuracy endpoints are saturated in-distribution draws, so
their non-monotone point estimates cannot establish non-monotonic
trainability (Figure~\ref{fig:groupsize}).


\fig{fig_group_size}
{Measured reward-contrast summaries and completion counts under the fixed-prompt arithmetic sweep}
{Measured reward-contrast summaries and completion counts under the fixed-prompt arithmetic sweep. No reconstructed $G\ge32$ performance grid or contaminated arithmetic accuracy comparison is shown. Stored values are unchanged.}
{fig:groupsize}



\subsection{The measured arithmetic
sweep}\label{the-measured-arithmetic-sweep}

The sweep covers G ∈ \{2, 4, 8, 16\}, with three seeds per level and 40
steps on Qwen2.5-0.5B two-digit addition. Mean recorded evaluation
accuracies (± seed SE) are 0.9817 ± 0.0044, 0.9883 ± 0.0017, 0.9900 ±
0.0029 and 0.9783 ± 0.0060 respectively. The 200 evaluation problems are
fresh draws from the training generator, not a guaranteed disjoint test
split. Near-ceiling scores and only three seeds leave little sensitivity
to modest differences; these records do not establish an optimal G, a
genuine inverted-U response or algorithmic equivalence.

Mean observed ZVF falls from 0.838 to 0.764, 0.691 and 0.631 across the
same G levels. This describes changing reward-group statistics, not a
proved gain in held-out learning or useful compute. The runner fixes
prompts and optimiser steps, so larger groups consume more completions.
Its advantages use population-standard-deviation normalisation, its
response log probabilities are length-normalised, and its loss is
averaged over the full completion batch. The inspected runner uses Adam
at 10⁻⁴ across G with no G-specific learning-rate compensation; the
stored sweep records omit the executed learning rate and immutable
source binding. This is a coupled sampling-budget comparison, not an
equal-token intervention.

\subsection{Excluded reconstructions and attribution
claims}\label{excluded-reconstructions-and-attribution-claims}

The historical G ≥ 32 and million-token budget grids are joint-fit
reconstructions, not executed training arms. Their retention curves,
fitted asymptotes, critical-budget intervals and adaptive-saving
percentages are excluded from the main empirical results. They cannot
establish equivalence to a literature reference, locate a group-size
optimum or forecast savings. The measured matched-rollout panel in
§6.3.3 and the small matched-token checks below have their own, narrower
budgets and must not be extended into that unexecuted grid.

The four-axis factorisation does not repair the arithmetic comparison.
Its algorithm component inherits the different evaluation items and
unequal training overlap of §6.2.3, while the group-size component
inherits saturation and changing completion expenditure. Those shares
are descriptive functions of the supplied records, not estimates of
independently manipulated causes. The appropriate follow-on evidence is
the separately contracted unsaturated comparison in §6.5.5, whose v2
seed and exposure-matched analysis remains incomplete.

\subsection{Small curriculum and matched-token
checks}\label{small-curriculum-and-matched-token-checks}

The exploratory curriculum, loss-form and layer-freeze checks did not
establish a reliable held-out improvement. Their small samples and
differing budgets prevent a general conclusion of ineffectiveness.

A difficulty curriculum drives the reported zero-gradient fraction from
0.50 to 0.00 among accepted batches, at approximately 4.81 times the
sampling cost. Rejected candidates still consume sampling resources.
Both arms move from 0.80 to 0.85 on twenty held-out items in a single
eight-step seed: one additional correct item each. A later three-seed,
matched 30k-token comparison reports +0.028 in both arms. Equality of
these point estimates does not establish equivalence or rule out a
practically important effect. The source's independent verification
flags the original curriculum claim and a single-seed G = 4 ``win'' of
+0.125 as SUSPECT; the latter is one held-out item and is not evidence
of a group-size optimum.

The loss-form comparison records gains of +0.125 for the standard sum,
+0.000 for mean-normalisation and +0.042 for the surprise formulation,
with only 12--20 held-out items per arm. These coarse point estimates do
not establish a ranking or equality. The standard arm's recorded length
change is −16 tokens; neither shortening nor a small accuracy difference
tests the general uncensored length-bias mechanism.

The layer-ranking check begins with overlap 1.0 in a 1.5B
hardcoded-arithmetic toy, then records approximately 0.11 in two 3B
GSM8K seeds. A later four-seed pass gives mean overlap 0.083 ± 0.048
(mean ± SD), while top-quarter concentration remains approximately 0.39.
Its reference is 0.25 for independent uniform top-nine sets among 36
layers. The target is the aggregate across all steps, including step 1,
rather than an independent final-step target. Model size, task and
recipe changed together, so this is failure to reproduce the toy ranking
in the tested configuration, not an isolated effect of scale or a
general result about freezing (§5.5; source:
platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/p1\_layerfreeze/scaled\_\allowbreak{}4seed\_\allowbreak{}result.json).

The matched 30k-token check has a defined budget; the other small
comparisons do not jointly form a matched-cost intervention study. The
diagnostic's prescriptive value remains unresolved, and P2 is retained
as a monitoring instrument rather than a validated optimisation
objective.

\section{P4 --- Length Bias and Held-Out
Generalisation}\label{p4-length-bias-and-held-out-generalisation}

The fourth pillar tests the most widely repeated specific criticism of
GRPO: that it rewards verbosity, inflating completion length in a way
that Dr.~GRPO corrects. The available capped runs do not identify
unconstrained length bias; their observed lengths and rewards are
reported within that limitation (Figure~\ref{fig:lengthbias}).


\fig{fig_length_bias}
{Evidence boundaries for the length-bias question}
{Evidence boundaries for the length-bias question. Both GSM8K caps bind; the raised-cap first-five-step means are training telemetry, not pretraining baselines. No held-out raised-cap evaluation or causal mask comparison is available.}
{fig:lengthbias}



\subsection{Observed lengths under a binding
cap}\label{observed-lengths-under-a-binding-cap}

Sixteen controlled runs were analysed: ten on an arithmetic task with
Qwen2.5-0.5B over 40 steps, and six on GSM8K chain-of-thought with
Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct over 30 steps, split evenly between GRPO and
Dr.~GRPO. The arithmetic half is saturated: recorded evaluation accuracy
is 0.987 for GRPO and 0.992 for Dr.~GRPO (paired p = 0.35, five seeds),
and completions average 4.7 tokens, so its score differences and short
responses have little headroom. Only the GSM8K-CoT half has headroom. At
these 30--40-step horizons, neither algorithm exhibits the
verbosity-trap signature on either task: the pillar's length-bias flag,
which fires when completion length grows while reward stays flat or
falls, is zero on every one of the sixteen runs. That flag is not the
roster audit's peak-then-decay rule of §6.5.2. Recomputed from the
per-step logs, the peak-then-decay rule (peak before 65\% of training,
last-10 mean reward below 90\% of peak) fires on none of the ten
arithmetic runs but on five of the six GSM8K-CoT runs, three GRPO and
two Dr.~GRPO, because the reward trace on that task is noisy and peaks
early; length still falls in all five.

The summary correlations are as follows, given as mean ± SD across seeds
(n = 5 arithmetic, n = 3 GSM8K-CoT). On arithmetic, the rank correlation
between training step and completion length is −0.405 ± 0.176 for GRPO
and −0.307 ± 0.181 for Dr.~GRPO; on GSM8K-CoT it is −0.622 ± 0.301 and
−0.355 ± 0.255. The step-versus-reward correlations are +0.709 ± 0.082
and +0.753 ± 0.074 on arithmetic, and +0.046 ± 0.162 and +0.025 ± 0.091
on GSM8K-CoT. The length-versus-reward correlations are −0.419 ± 0.155
and −0.293 ± 0.182 on arithmetic, and −0.501 ± 0.258 and −0.618 ± 0.118
on GSM8K-CoT. Mean reward over the last ten steps is 0.988 for both
algorithms on arithmetic, and 0.265 for GRPO against 0.252 for Dr.~GRPO
on GSM8K-CoT. The direction of the length trend is negative everywhere:
all sixteen length slopes are negative, with the arithmetic GRPO slope
at −0.029 tokens per step and the GSM8K GRPO slope at −0.481, and the
count of runs with a positive horizon is zero. On the harder task,
GRPO's final-ten-step mean completion length is 184.4 tokens against
Dr.~GRPO's 189.3 --- a difference of +2.7\% in the direction opposite to
the critique.

The GSM8K result is heavily cap-censored. Initial mean completions are
about 194 of the allowed 200 tokens, leaving little upward range. A
correctness reward requiring an extractable final answer can penalise
cap truncation and thereby create downward pressure on observed length;
the surviving logs do not identify how much of the decline is caused by
this mechanism. The absence of observed expansion therefore does not
falsify normalisation-induced length bias or show that the unconstrained
policies would not expand. A matched comparison with demonstrably low
truncation, correctness-stratified lengths and actual gradient weights
remains unresolved.

\subsection{The roster-level verbosity-trap
audit}\label{the-roster-level-verbosity-trap-audit}

Set against the controlled null is a descriptive audit over the whole
experiment roster, and here the picture differs. The audit defines four
statistics per trace with at least five steps: an instability index
\(\sigma(r_{\mathrm{tail}})/|\mu(r_{\mathrm{tail}})|\) over the final
50\% of training; a monotonicity score, the fraction of consecutive
step-pairs with strictly improving reward; a regression severity, the
largest normalised drop from peak; and a binary verbosity-trap flag, set
when the peak occurs before 65\% of training and the terminal reward
falls below 90\% of peak.

On that definition, the eleven GRPO runs in the roster have a mean
instability index of 0.267 ± 0.335, against 0.785 ± 0.297 (mean ± spread
across runs; the source does not label the ±, and its size relative to
the mean implies an SD) for the seventeen PPO-labelled runs. Four of the
eleven GRPO runs, 36\% (Wilson 95\% CI {[}15\%, 65\%{]}, one run per
configuration), carry the flag on this definition, including both
Qwen3-8B runs and the Nemotron-120B run. That count is reported here as
a description of the roster, not as evidence of length bias, because the
flag does not isolate the behaviour it is named for. Three properties of
the rule prevent the stronger reading. Its thresholds are post hoc:
neither the 65\% peak position nor the 0.90 terminal-to-peak ratio is
pre-registered anywhere in the repository, and the rule cannot be traced
to a commit that predates the traces it judges. It is not specific to
length: the metric it tests is in-training reward, and the project's own
source reads the flag as ``a generic instability detector'' rather than
a length measure, so a run can carry it without any change in response
length. And its base rate is high: applied to the wider trace pool
rather than this eleven-run subset, the rule fires on the majority of
runs --- including PPO-labelled runs and the Dr.~GRPO control in the one
matched comparison --- so 36\% sits at or below the rate the rule
produces on traces with no decay at all. The project's own length data
point the other way: under the 200-token cap, the pooled mean completion
length over the six GRPO and Dr.~GRPO runs drifts from 194.4 tokens
(first five steps) to 183.5 tokens (last five steps), i.e.~shorter; per
arm, 195.0 → 180.3 (GRPO) and 193.9 → 186.8 (Dr.~GRPO) (recomputed from
\longtok{drgrpo\_\allowbreak{}gsm8k\_\allowbreak{}cot\_\allowbreak{}full.json}). These five-step windows differ
from the final-ten-step means quoted in §6.5.1. The run-level audit
therefore does not establish length inflation, and the capped comparison
does not establish its unconstrained absence. The Nemotron run records
the highest instability index in the pool at 1.194, with a regression
severity of 1.0 and a terminal-to-peak reward ratio of 0.57. The two
DeepSeek-V3.1 GRPO runs, by contrast, show markedly lower instability,
with a mean of 0.143. The PPO rate of 12/17 has a Wilson 95\% CI of
{[}47\%, 87\%{]}. These are descriptive uncertainty intervals for
heterogeneous roster counts; their overlap is not a formal test of a
rate difference, and the pool composition prevents an algorithm-level
stability comparison.

Two caveats on that comparison must travel with it. First, the PPO pool
is dominated by classic-RL library runs (SB3, CleanRL, Tianshou) that
failed to train at all, with last-10 rewards below 5\%; a near-zero
reward trajectory mechanically produces a high instability index.
Restricted to LLM-native PPO, the PPO Llama instability is 0.105, lower
than GRPO Llama's 0.228, and the source therefore explicitly declines to
claim that GRPO is more or less stable than PPO at the algorithmic
level. Second, the audit's reward metric is the in-training reward, not
a held-out one, and the audit pool is single-seed per configuration:
each Tinker configuration was run once and is reported descriptively.
The only multi-seed matched base-versus-RL control on Qwen3-8B is the
five-seed comparison discussed below.

An earlier draft of this report's summary attributed to P4 the claim
that Dr.~GRPO is the only algorithm without the verbosity-trap pattern.
That claim could not be located in any P4 source. What the sources state
is that 36\% of the audit's GRPO runs carry the flag; the audit does not
report a per-algorithm flag rate for Dr.~GRPO, and in the controlled
sixteen-run comparison the length-bias flag is zero for both algorithms
while the peak-then-decay rule fires on both (three of three GRPO and
two of three Dr.~GRPO runs on GSM8K-CoT). The finding recorded here is
therefore the sourced one: the peak-then-decay pattern is not specific
to GRPO, it appears in both arms of the controlled comparison without
any length inflation, and the project makes no claim that Dr.~GRPO is
uniquely free of it. The narrowest related statement the sources do
support is a length-drift one --- that Dr.~GRPO is the only algorithm
whose length keeps drifting upward after the initial compression phase
--- which is a different statistic and is not the verbosity-trap flag.

\subsection{Exploratory length
analyses}\label{exploratory-length-analyses}

Post-hoc sliding-window, rank-correlation and length--reward analyses
describe local variation in the same sixteen runs. They do not create
additional independent replications or remove the generation cap. With
five arithmetic seeds and three GSM8K seeds, estimates are sensitive to
individual runs; within-run windows are autocorrelated. Appendix E
records the statistical corrections and the historical exploratory test
family. None supplies a multiplicity-surviving confirmatory result about
length bias.

The historical coefficient-product percentages labelled ``proportion
mediated'' are not causal mediation estimates. Products and ratios of
marginal and partial correlations are not common-scale path
coefficients, and same-step observational reward, length and ZVF do not
establish a causal ordering. Local re-expansion flags likewise describe
the chosen rule rather than prevalence of an unconstrained verbosity
failure.

\subsection{Held-out generalisation and the scale
extension}\label{held-out-generalisation-and-the-scale-extension}

The pillar's held-out results are the part of the chapter closest to a
conventional capability claim, and they are reported with corresponding
care. The only multi-seed matched base-versus-RL control on Qwen3-8B is
a five-seed comparison on Qwen3-8B-Instruct, which moves from 82.0\% to
83.3\% with p = 0.26. Stated as a mean improvement over the pre-RL
checkpoint, the gain is +0.013 with a paired p = 0.256 at n = 5 ---
small and not statistically significant, and a matched pre-RL control
for the Qwen3-8B-Base checkpoint is not available. In summary, on this
near-ceiling configuration, GRPO does not produce a held-out improvement
that can be separated from noise at the available seed budget, and GRPO
and Dr.~GRPO produce held-out gains that are indistinguishable from each
other at that budget.

On Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct GSM8K-CoT, the stored mean pre/post changes are
+6.2 percentage points for GRPO and +5.0 for Dr.~GRPO over three seeds
and the same 200 test items. These are observed score changes with an
unresolved measurement problem: greedy pre-training evaluation of the
same base checkpoint yields two correctness vectors, scoring 40/200 and
41/200 but disagreeing on seven items. Thus item discordance can arise
before any training; the one-item aggregate difference does not bound
the noise at one item. The retained records do not identify whether the
cause is batching, padding, numerical kernels, model mode, sampling or
another execution difference. No specific cause or deterministic repair
is claimed without repeated unchanged-checkpoint evaluations and
execution receipts. The historical seed-level and McNemar tests
therefore do not isolate training benefit from evaluation variability.
For reference, the per-seed one-sample tests on the pre-to-post deltas
give \(t_2 = 13.98\), \(p = 0.0051\), 95\% CI {[}+0.043, +0.081{]}
(GRPO) and \(t_2 = 10.0\), \(p = 0.0099\), {[}+0.028, +0.072{]}
(Dr.~GRPO), but with n = 3 the exact two-sided sign-flip test cannot go
below \(p = 0.25\). The direct post-training comparison, which does not
depend on the discordant pre-training evaluations, is 0.2633 against
0.2550, a paired difference of +0.0083 (\(t_2 = 0.94\), \(p = 0.44\),
95\% CI {[}−0.030, +0.046{]}); it is inconclusive (recomputed from
\longtok{drgrpo\_\allowbreak{}gsm8k\_\allowbreak{}cot\_\allowbreak{}full.json}). The separately implemented v2
comparison below is reported under its own contract.

The raised-cap Qwen3-8B extension comprises six completed 30-step logs:
GRPO and a centred-advantage arm labelled \texttt{drgrpo}, seeds 42, 123
and 456, LoRA rank 4, G = 8, batch 4, learning rate 3 × 10⁻⁵ and
temperature 1.0. The source reports a 1,024-token cap. First-five-step
to last-ten-step mean lengths are, in seed order, GRPO 1004.05 → 904.98,
980.51 → 943.61 and 995.73 → 900.09 tokens; the centred arm records
999.43 → 931.38, 972.38 → 902.04 and 1000.21 → 878.23. These are
rollout-window means, not step-zero or unchanged-base evaluations. Every
early window is close to the cap; exact truncation fractions cannot be
recovered from mean lengths alone. No held-out accuracy, per-completion
correctness-stratified length or token-mask receipt is present in these
six files.

The extension cannot confirm canonical Dr.~GRPO or an uncapped
length-bias null. The historically adjacent \longtok{live\_\allowbreak{}zvf\_\allowbreak{}probe.py}
source at commit 1959effe sums full-sequence log probabilities, supplies
an extra final token-zero target and refreshes the sampler only at save
intervals; \texttt{-\/-loss\ drgrpo} changes reward standardisation,
without introducing canonical fixed-token loss normalisation. Later code
repairs are not retroactive execution evidence. The six receipts do not
bind an immutable executed source, and earlier Dr.~GRPO-labelled
attempts explicitly retained as \longtok{invalid\_\allowbreak{}actually\_\allowbreak{}grpo} must
not be pooled with them. The supportable result is only recorded length
compression and training reward under a raised but still potentially
binding cap; the intended held-out and controlled-length comparison
remains missing.

The arithmetic late-efficiency statistic is likewise exploratory: it
uses training-reward gain per unit length change on five seeds, under a
short-response ceiling. It does not supply a held-out benefit or resolve
the cap-censoring problem.

\subsection{Same-stack rerun on the unsaturated
configuration}\label{same-stack-rerun-on-the-unsaturated-configuration}

The historical arithmetic comparisons of §6.2.3 and §6.4.1 sit near
accuracy 0.99 and have the evaluation and budget limitations stated
there. To test the contrasts where headroom exists, they were rerun on 2
October 2026 (v1) on the configuration of §6.5.4: Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct,
GSM8K with a boxed-answer correctness reward and a 200-token
chain-of-thought cap, LoRA rank 16 on the attention projections,
learning rate 1 × 10⁻⁵, 30 steps, two inner epochs of the clipped
surrogate with per-response length normalisation, on one A10G per run.
Three arms share that stack and a fixed count of 64 generations per
step, but differ in advantage estimation, critic training and
distinct-prompt exposure: GRPO with G = 8 (8 prompts × 8 completions),
GRPO with G = 2 (32 × 2), and PPO with a learned value head on the
prompt-end hidden state (64 × 1, value loss 0.5 × MSE, batch-normalised
advantage). Each run evaluates the same 200 GSM8K \emph{test} items
greedily before and after training, so the evaluation set is disjoint
from the training split. Five paired seeds were run per arm; the unit is
the seed, and the analysis, including a ±2-point equivalence margin, was
fixed in the script before launch.

\textbf{Same-stack rerun v1 (superseded by Table 6.2) on
Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct GSM8K-CoT, five paired seeds, 200 test items,
200-token cap.}

In v1 both GRPO arms improve on every seed, with a mean change of
similar size to the separately recorded result in §6.5.4. The PPO arm
shows no observed accuracy gain: its training reward drifts down and its
post-training accuracy (0.195) is below its starting point (0.200). This
does not establish that no learning occurred. After training, GRPO at G
= 8 exceeded PPO by +0.050 {[}+0.015, +0.085{]} (paired \(t\), p =
0.016); the difference is positive for all five seeds, which puts the
exact two-sided sign-flip p at its n = 5 floor of 0.0625, and Holm
adjustment of the sign-flip p across the three v1 contrasts gives 0.19.
GRPO at G = 8 and at G = 2 differ by −0.005 {[}−0.038, +0.028{]} (p =
0.69); that interval is wider than the ±0.02 margin, so equivalence is
not established either (TOST p = 0.13). This v1 PPO contrast is
superseded by the v2 rerun below and is not a finding of this thesis.

Two design features of v1 prevent its PPO contrast from being read as an
estimator comparison. First, the 200-token cap was binding: mean
training completions are 190.16 (G8), 191.62 (G2) and 194.16 (PPO)
tokens under cap 200, with PPO last-ten length 196.19, and the starting
model scores only 0.200 under this cap, so the endpoint is performance
under a binding completion budget; the fraction of the gain attributable
to learning to finish within that budget was not identified. Second, the
critic was cold: a randomly initialised linear value head trained for 30
steps at the policy's learning rate, with no warm-up, no generalised
advantage estimation and no tuning, whose initial value loss of 0.5--13
sat far above the \textasciitilde0.08 floor, which raises a
critic-quality concern without identifying the signal-to-noise ratio of
PPO's executed advantages. The 64-prompt PPO batch also saw eight times
as many distinct prompts per step as GRPO at G = 8. Fixed generation
counts are not measured equality of generated tokens or total compute.

\textbf{v2 rerun (supersedes the v1 PPO contrast).} A second version
(v2;
platform\_hybrid/experiments/modal/modal\_\allowbreak{}samestack\_\allowbreak{}gsm8k\_\allowbreak{}cot\_\allowbreak{}v2.py,
analysis described as fixed on 3 October 2026 before launch) revises
those design choices. It keeps the model, LoRA configuration, policy
learning rate, 30 steps, 64 generations per step and the clipped
surrogate, and changes four things: the generation cap is 512 tokens
with per-completion truncation logged; the PPO critic is a
zero-initialised linear probe on the detached prompt-end hidden state
with its own optimiser (learning rate 10⁻³), four warm-up rollout
batches from the initial policy before any policy update, and a baseline
for each batch predicted before the probe sees that batch's rewards; the
reward scores only the last \texttt{\textbackslash{}boxed\{\}} answer,
numerically normalised; and evaluation uses the full 1,319-item GSM8K
test split, greedy, with the completions stored. An independent re-grade
of the stored completions agrees with the stored scores on every item (0
disagreements over 35,613 post-training item grades). Ten seeds were run
for each GRPO arm; at the time of writing seven of ten PPO seeds had
completed, and the pre-specified exposure-matched PPO arm (8 prompts × 8
completions with the learned baseline) was still running.

512-token cap, warm-started critic, 1,319 test items (provisional: PPO 7
of 10 seeds).}

Under the revised protocol, all three arms show sample-mean gains of
3.6--4.4 percentage points (0.036--0.044) from a base accuracy of 0.654,
and PPO learns. The GRPO G8 − PPO difference shrinks from v1's +5.0
percentage points (+0.050) to +0.76 points (+0.0076); its interval
includes zero, and an ordinary interim TOST at the stated ±2-point
(±0.02) margin returns p = 0.016; the incomplete planned analysis does
not establish a final equivalence claim. The minimum detectable
difference at n = 7 seeds is about 0.015 (80\% power, α = 0.05), so v2
could have detected a v1-sized effect, and a true difference of 0.05 is
rejected (p ≈ 4 × 10⁻⁵). GRPO G8 − G2 is +0.79 points (+0.0079) with an
unadjusted interval that just excludes zero; after Holm adjustment it is
not significant (p = 0.14). The supportable conclusion is an interim,
protocol-specific difference estimate. Critic training, generation cap,
scoring and evaluation scope changed together; this does not isolate any
one repair. Two qualifications remain: prompt exposure still differs
between GRPO G8 and PPO until the exposure-matched arm completes, and
the result is one model, one task and one 30-step budget.

\textbf{Interim analysis contract and unresolved declarations.} Table
6.2 is retained unchanged as the historical provisional snapshot, not a
final endpoint. Before a confirmatory interpretation, the analysis must
bind the planned seed IDs and missing/retried runs, the complete primary
contrast family (including exposure-matched PPO), the policy and
evaluator protocol identities, and the ordered test-item identities. The
record here does not establish whether equivalence was one primary claim
or several separately reportable claims; nor does it supply a justified
practical margin or a planned interim stopping rule. These remain
unresolved rather than retrospectively invented. The displayed Holm
values cover only the available contrasts and cannot stand in for the
final planned family. The ordinary TOST calculation is separate from the
printed 95\% difference interval; a corresponding unadjusted equivalence
interval would be 90\%. The margin is sizeable relative to the reported
training gains and requires substantive justification. No new
experiment, final verdict, or updated v2 number is introduced by this
clarification.

\textbf{Execution platform. {[}PENDING: cross-platform calibration{]}}
The remaining PPO and exposure-matched (ppo\_8x8) seeds were trained on
Google Colab A100 GPUs because Modal was unavailable; the earlier runs
used Modal A10G. Greedy bf16 decoding is not identical across these
GPUs: only about 481 of the 1,319 base-model completions match the Modal
A10G text, and base accuracy is 0.6626 on A100 against 0.6535 and 0.6550
on the two Modal states. Contrasts are therefore paired only within the
same platform and seed, and the platform effect is measured by
re-running GRPO G = 8 at all 10 seeds on A100 and testing it for
equivalence at ±2 points (±0.02).

\section{Cross-Pillar Observations and
Limitations}\label{cross-pillar-observations-and-limitations}

The core studies establish no general algorithm ranking or scaling law.
The short and interrupted scale traces cannot identify post-training
scaling. The saturated arithmetic PPO/GRPO records have unequal training
overlap and different evaluation items, so they do not estimate an
isolated estimator effect. The G sweep changes sampling expenditure and
has little accuracy headroom. Synthetic mitigation panels,
imputed-feature risk scores and unexecuted large-budget reconstructions
are excluded from empirical support.

ZVF has a stronger but narrower footing: its arithmetic is reproducible
from stored rewards, and its unsigned definition necessarily aliases
all-correct with all-wrong groups. That does not establish incremental
prediction, useful gradients or a beneficial control policy. Capped
response-length compression likewise does not identify the policies'
unconstrained length behaviour. The raised-cap P4 extension adds
training logs, not the missing held-out or censoring controls.

The unsaturated v2 comparison in Table 6.2 is the principal interim
algorithm comparison. Its numerical snapshot remains unchanged and
provisional, with incomplete planned seeds and an exposure-matched arm.
The earlier v1 gap is superseded; the available v2 differences are
protocol-specific and cannot establish final equivalence or identify
which jointly changed component explains the change. Completing the
declared comparison and its provenance is an empirical task, not a
conclusion supplied by this source-only revision.



\chapter{Results II: Reporting, Registry and Controller
(P5--P7)}\label{results-ii-reporting-registry-and-controller-p5p7}

The three internal reports covered in this chapter form a chain rather
than a set of independent results. P5 makes a claim about the epistemic
status of reinforcement-learning results for language models and
proposes a checkable reporting standard to match it. P6 turns the
standard into a machine-readable artifact with a validator, a badge and
a diff tool. P7 takes the diagnostic that the standard requires authors
to publish --- the zero-variance fraction (ZVF) --- and asks whether it
is mechanistically predictable and ready to control rollout allocation.
Each report is explicit about where its claim stops, and this chapter
reports their evidence in the same register.

\section{A Minimum Reporting Standard for Stack-Conditioned Results
(P5)}\label{a-minimum-reporting-standard-for-stack-conditioned-results-p5}

P5 proposes reporting the configuration needed to interpret an RL
result. This is an engineering contribution, not proof that framework
explains a particular share of outcome variance. The inspected
comparisons expose concrete ambiguities: the managed Tinker/TRL exhibit
associates 0.856 and 0.050 last-10 training rewards with different
Base/Instruct checkpoints, as well as different loss, parser, prompt,
sampling and optimiser settings. It cannot assign the gap to backend or
checkpoint. Near-zero reward at the start of a run does not diagnose a
parser or template failure without the corresponding outputs and scoring
checks.

A smaller managed head-to-head contains twelve records, four labels and
three seeds each, on Qwen3.5-4B/GSM8K. Mean last-10 reward spans
0.710--0.744 over fifteen steps. This is a short-horizon configuration
comparison; the small band does not establish canonical implementation
equivalence or equivalence of performance. An open dynamic-sampling arm
reporting zero ZVF and a managed asymmetric-clip surrogate sharing the
DAPO label are likewise different recipes on different tasks/models.
Discarding homogeneous groups makes accepted-batch ZVF zero by
construction and does not make collection free.

\subsection{Corpus provenance}\label{corpus-provenance}

The historical pool sizes refer to different units and snapshots. They
must not be read as counts of completed RL training runs.

Sources:
\longtok{platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/publication\_fixes/zvf\_confound/zvf\_\allowbreak{}audit\_\allowbreak{}histories.json};
\longtok{platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/p5p8/minreport\_audit.tsv}

The current mega directory contains 111 cells; the historical 98-ID set
is recovered by matching the audit IDs, not by treating the current
directory size as the old sample. A service record, manifest or tensor
establishes the corresponding retained artifact, not independently
authenticated executed source. The 368-record model-level tables
therefore remain heterogeneous logged summaries, and are not an
independent validation of the Bernoulli theory. The 98-cell sampling
grid cannot support claims about variation in trained GRPO outcomes,
algorithm-versus-stack dominance or seed variance in learning.

\subsection{Reporting requirements and demonstrated
tooling}\label{reporting-requirements-and-demonstrated-tooling}

The standard has eight items: (1) loss form, including ratio, clipping,
token mask, advantage normalisation and sampling filters; (2) reference
policy and KL handling; (3) sampler, backend, precision and checkpoint
identity; (4) per-step reward/ZVF telemetry; (5) group-size and
escalation schedule; (6) a disjoint evaluation split; (7) contamination
and parser checks; and (8) held-out pass@k with uncertainty. The first
seven describe the run; the eighth describes evaluation. This list is
justified by the identifiable failure modes, without claiming its
optimality.

The badge audit reports zero gold, zero silver, 99 bronze and four wood
badges over its 103 manifests. That is evidence about declared coverage.
In the historical 98 sampling manifests every top-level field is
present, yet key KL and loss details are inapplicable or unavailable;
applying a training-reporting rubric to these records cannot certify
actual RL implementation. The reported cell-level random-forest fits,
variance shares and fingerprint-distance correlations remain exploratory
analyses of this sampling grid. They are excluded as evidence of
predictive sufficiency or transfer to independently trained runs. Shared
configuration, identifiers and outcome-derived fields require grouped
holdouts and a separately bound feature definition before such a claim
could be assessed.

The exact-tie jitter example provides a concrete reason to publish
definitions: tiny reward perturbations remove all 95 exact ties among
600 groups, while the historical sample-variance tolerance of 10⁻⁶
retains all 95 and PCD changes negligibly (§6.3.1). It does not
establish that PCD is a superior controller or that a reporting badge
predicts learning.

\section{The GRPO-Registry (P6)}\label{the-grpo-registry-p6}

\textbf{Validation boundary.} Structural schema validity, internal
agreement, source binding, executed-code fidelity and outcome prediction
are distinct properties. Seven eligible declarations can agree without
proving execution honesty; comparisons sharing entries also need
dependence-aware uncertainty. The badge reports coverage. The cell-level
predictive estimates below do not establish transfer: saved feature
definitions, grouped folds and independent-run/configuration holdouts
are needed to rule out shared-configuration or outcome-derived
information and test incremental value beyond reward, G and task.

P6's premise is that the deltas defining GRPO variants --- DAPO's
asymmetric clip, dynamic sampling and token-level loss; Dr.~GRPO's
dropped length and standard-deviation normalisation; GSPO's
sequence-level importance ratio \citep{zheng2025gspo} --- live in paper
appendices and framework defaults rather than in any machine-readable
form. The registry is the response, and it ships as three artifacts at
\texttt{registry/}: \texttt{schema.json}, \texttt{entries/*.json} and
\texttt{query.py}.

The schema defines two record types under a single \texttt{oneOf}, both
carrying \texttt{schema\_version}. The stack record's core is
\texttt{min\_report}, one sub-object per minimum-report item, with
base-checkpoint identity required inside the \texttt{sampler\_backend}
block precisely because the audit that motivated item 3 found a managed
backend silently pinning a different base checkpoint. Two design
decisions carry weight. The first is the distinction between an
unreported field and a field reported as absent: a null means the author
said nothing, whereas an explicit \texttt{false}, \texttt{0.0} or
\texttt{"none"} means the author made a checkable claim --- ``a stack
that says `we use no KL term' is making a checkable claim; a stack that
says nothing is not.'' The second is that item 8, the held-out pass@k
requirement, is attached under outcomes as an evaluation-report field
rather than folded into the run manifest, preserving the eight-item
standard's split between what describes a run and what describes a
result. Per-component delta status is recorded on the stack side in
\longtok{variant\_\allowbreak{}deltas\_\allowbreak{}applied}, with each entry's status drawn from
\{implemented, surrogate, absent, unknown\}. The shipped entry for the
managed DAPO run makes the point concretely: \texttt{clip\_higher} is
recorded as \texttt{surrogate} with the note that the asymmetric clip
was set through the user-facing config while the underlying managed loss
form is unverifiable, \texttt{dynamic\_sampling} is recorded as
\texttt{absent}, and \longtok{token\_\allowbreak{}level\_\allowbreak{}loss} is \texttt{unknown}
because it is managed by the runtime. An optional \texttt{ci\_method}
object (method, bootstrap count, seed, confidence level, source) is
populated on the seven derived entries with \texttt{n\_boot\ =\ 2000}
and a percentile 95\% interval, and left null on the remaining
twenty-four.

Three variant-delta records were verified against their source papers:
DAPO with five components (clip higher at ε\_low = 0.2 and ε\_high =
0.28, dynamic sampling, token-level loss, overlong reward shaping, KL
removed); Dr.~GRPO with two removals (the 1/\textbar o\_i\textbar{}
response-length normalisation in the loss and the division by the
within-group reward standard deviation in the advantage); and GSPO with
a length-normalised sequence-likelihood importance ratio and
sequence-level clipping.

The seed population described in the P6 report is twenty stack records
in five families, all of which pass validation. The
\texttt{registry/entries/} directory at this revision holds 28 stack
records and 20 variant-delta files, so the population counts, badge
spans and coverage percentages below describe the earlier 20-record
snapshot rather than the current directory, where the badge spans 43--93
(mean 62.68; §7.2). Family A holds four framework config dumps
(Qwen3-8B, GSM8K, seed 42) scoring badges 74, 60, 60 and 62; family B
holds the four open Colab trainer arms at toy scale, scoring 96, 96, 96
and 96; family C is the managed head-to-head at 57, 62, 62 and 60;
family D contributes three variant records from a same-stack four-method
run at 67 each; and family E contributes five method records from a
five-seed risk-index batch at 43 each. The family sizes are therefore 4
+ 4 + 4 + 3 + 5 = 20. The finding worth stating plainly is why the
ordering looks the way it does. The config dumps were assembled for a
reviewer-requested fairness audit and score only 60 to 74: they nail
items 2, 3 and 5, expose item 1 only partially, and cover items 6 and 7
not at all. The open Colab arms score 96 not because the runs are better
but because an open backward pass, coupled with explicit declarations
that no KL term and no decontamination check were used, makes every
field reportable. Reproducibility supplements of the kind commonly
shipped with RL papers do not automatically satisfy MIN-REPORT.

The historical registry snapshot contains 31 records: twenty stack
declarations and eleven variant-delta records, all parsing successfully
with no broken delta pointers. These are structural checks, not
execution certification. Among ten DAPO-labelled declarations, the delta
statuses are distributed as shown below; unknown is a reporting state,
not a verified implementation.

The registry was stress-tested rather than asserted. A batch of
adversarial perturbations with B = 4000 bootstrap resamples --- 13
categories × 31 entries × 10 mutations = 4,030 generated, of which 800
were no-ops for their entry and were skipped, leaving 3,230 applied ---
moved the overall robustness statistic from 0.864 {[}0.756, 0.878{]}
before the corresponding schema bumps to 1.000 {[}1.000, 1.000{]} after
them, closing three silent-failure categories: a missing identifier
pattern check (0.645 to 1.000), MIN-REPORT leaves declared in
\texttt{properties} but not in \texttt{required} (0.000 to 1.000, fixed
by promoting key presence to a schema invariant), and free-form delta
references (0.000 to 1.000, fixed by replacing \texttt{delta\_id} with
an enum generated from the entry filenames). A separate consistency
check cross-references the variant-delta records against the MIN-REPORT
blocks: of 31 triples from 13 entries, 7 are MATCH, 0 are MISMATCH, 0
are MISSING\_REPORT, 5 are SURROGATE\_OBS and 19 are NOT\_APPLICABLE,
giving an internal-consistency match rate of 7/7 = 1.000 with a Wilson
95\% CI of {[}0.65, 1.00{]} (a percentile bootstrap of 7/7 is degenerate
at {[}1, 1{]}).

A separately recorded same-stack comparison supplies an illustrative
case; it does not validate the schema causally. Re-executing four
GRPO-family methods (GRPO, AERO, GIFT, AREAL) on a single identical
stack --- managed sampler, G = 8, seed 0, 40 steps, 16 prompts per step
--- with the full per-prompt reward tensor logged at every step,
produces reward deltas of magnitude ≤ 2 percentage points. Two of the
three show within-run intervals that exclude zero on the side of a
\emph{decrease} (AERO at −0.014 {[}−0.023, −0.005{]} and AREAL at −0.020
{[}−0.032, −0.008{]}). Those intervals are paired bootstrap intervals
over the last 10 of 40 steps of a single seed-0 run per method, so they
carry no seed variance and are not replication. GIFT's delta is +0.016
{[}−0.007, +0.040{]}. Exhaustively enumerated sign-flip calculations
over the same 10 paired steps, Holm-adjusted across the three contrasts,
give p = 0.047 for AERO, 0.047 for AREAL and 0.23 for GIFT; when
prompt-level variation is included (160 paired prompt-steps), the AERO
decrease is no longer distinguishable from zero (Holm p = 0.20). The
observed magnitudes describe these traces only. Inferential exactness
requires exchangeability or sign-symmetry assumptions, which enumeration
alone does not establish for sequential steps. Nested prompt-steps are
not additional training replications. Whether any difference exceeds
seed-to-seed noise was not tested, so neither the claim that all
differences are within noise nor its negation is established. The
largest same-stack effects are not on reward at all: AERO and AREAL
lengthen completions by approximately 31 tokens and tighten the length
coefficient of variation, and GIFT's loss shift of −1.67×10⁴ is a
baseline constant. A later registry extension measured an
``anti-herding'' residual
\(\delta_{\mathrm{div}} = \mathrm{ZVF}^{\mathrm{iid}} - \mathrm{ZVF}^{\mathrm{obs}}\)
on the same tensors; this analysis is \textbf{withdrawn}. The source
reported a stack-mean of 0.049 with a range of {[}0.039, 0.053{]}; only
GIFT showed a borderline-significant reduction against GRPO (p = 0.066,
CI {[}−0.021, +0.001{]}), which it read as resolving a synthesis range
of {[}0.13, 0.23{]} downwards. The reading does not survive: with
\(\mathrm{ZVF}^{\mathrm{iid}}\) computed by plugging each group's own
success rate into \(h_G\), a positive \(\delta_{\mathrm{div}}\) is
guaranteed by construction --- its expectation is +0.105 even under
i.i.d. rollouts with no herding at all --- so neither its sign nor its
size is evidence of a diversity effect, and the associated iso-G table
is withdrawn with it.

The reference CLI, \texttt{registry/query.py}, implements four verbs
using only the standard library: \texttt{list};
\texttt{query\ -\/-item\ reference\_kl} returning FULL, PARTIAL or
UNREPORTED; \texttt{badge}; and \texttt{stackdiff}, which maps a pair of
entries onto the R0--R5 ladder and reports the highest rung triggered
--- R0 identical manifests, R1 nuisance-only, R2 runtime differences, R3
loss-form or KL-handling differences, R4 differing variant-delta sets,
R5 unauditable because a differing item is null on at least one side.
The badge formula weights the seven items equally and is outcome-blind:
\(\mathrm{badge}(s) = \mathrm{round}\bigl(100 \cdot \tfrac{1}{7} \sum_i c_i(s)\bigr)\),
where \(c_i(s)\) is the fraction of item \(i\)'s schema leaves that are
non-null (\texttt{badge()} and \texttt{item\_score()} in
\longtok{platform\_hybrid/registry/query.py}). \texttt{stackdiff} prints
a warning at R4 and above that outcome differences must not be
attributed to the shared algorithm label, and the P6 report is careful
to describe the ladder as ``a risk scale, not an effect-size scale.''
Applied to the shipped entries, \texttt{stackdiff} flags the
open-versus-closed ``DAPO'' pair as R5 from manifests alone, and across
the twenty seed entries the MIN-REPORT badge spanned 43--96; across the
28 current stack entries, \texttt{query.py\ badge} gives 43--93 with
mean 62.68. Two boundaries deserve restating. The registry reports
stated provenance and conflicts; it does not reconcile unresolved
exact-ID or model conflicts, nor does it validate nominal algorithm
comparisons. And the badge is coarse relative to the corpus --- twenty
entries collapse to ten distinct fingerprints, and twenty of
twenty-three sub-fields carry at most two values. The version of
\texttt{stackdiff} described in the P6 report did not compare the model
field, so the Base-versus-Instruct checkpoint difference behind the 17×
exhibit was invisible to it; its R5 rung meant only that a differing
item was null on one side, and it fired on 366 of the 378 pairs among
the 28 current stack entries; and it shipped without unit tests. It was
revised on 16 October 2026, and the revised tool is the one evaluated
here. It compares a fixed catalogue of 48 fields and assigns a level
only from fields known on both sides that differ, so missing information
no longer raises the verdict. R5 is now reserved for outcome-critical
fields --- execution mode, checkpoint identity and variant, reward
definition, prompt and chat template, dataset and evaluation protocol
--- and missing information is reported separately as a coverage
fraction, the worst severity that could still be hidden, and an
UNDERSPECIFIED flag when coverage is below 0.8 or an R5 field is
unknown. Across the 378 pairs of the 28 stack entries the distribution
is R0 39, R2 1, R3 10, R4 26 and R5 302; 342 pairs are UNDERSPECIFIED,
with median coverage 0.19, because 16 of the 28 entries are still
hand-entered rather than generated from a run configuration. Among the
78 pairs whose checkpoint is known and identical on both sides, the
distribution is R0 39, R2 1, R3 10, R4 26 and R5 2, so the R5 rung is
driven mainly by checkpoint differences rather than by missing data. On
the 17× exhibit the revised tool returns R5 on checkpoint identity and
variant (Qwen3-8B-Base against Qwen3-8B), reward definition, and prompt
and chat template, at coverage 0.58 --- which is the confound the
exhibit was missing. On the two ``DAPO'' records it returns R5 on
checkpoint, reward, template, dataset and evaluation metric at coverage
0.87, confirming that the pair is not comparable. The tool ships with 29
passing unit tests (\longtok{tests/test\_\allowbreak{}registry\_\allowbreak{}stackdiff.py}). It
remains a reporting check: an R5 verdict says which recorded fields
differ, not how much they move the outcome, and its predictive value for
outcome gaps is untested. The committed 11 October version, whose output
on the DAPO pair (KL coefficient 0.0 against null, R5 unauditable, exit
code 5) is quoted in earlier drafts, requires its immutable source
commit and registry snapshot to reproduce; moving git \texttt{HEAD} is
not a revision identifier. The exact historical source/dirty-diff
binding remains unresolved in this review copy.

\section{From Diagnostic to Controller
(P7)}\label{from-diagnostic-to-controller-p7}

P7 investigates whether reward-group statistics can guide rollout
allocation. The mathematical identities, stored-tensor replay and small
online experiment are distinct evidence classes. None establishes a
validated adaptive policy.

The theory is compact. Fix a prompt with latent per-rollout success
probability p and a group of G conditionally independent Bernoulli
rewards. Write \(h_G(p) = p^G + (1-p)^G\) for the probability that the
group is degenerate, and model the usable signal per prompt as
\(S(p,G) = p(1-p)(1-h_G(p))\). This product is a heuristic allocation
objective, not an identity for expected advantage magnitude. For
unnormalised centered binary advantages, \(M_G=2\hat p(1-\hat p)\) and
\(E[M_G\mid p]=2(G-1)p(1-p)/G\) already include homogeneous groups' zero
contributions. Conditioning on a mixed group divides this expectation by
\(1-h_G(p)\) rather than multiplying by it. Reward contrast also omits
score-vector magnitudes and cancellation, so it does not determine
gradient magnitude. Both factors vanish at the endpoints and peak at p =
½, so this proposed signal proxy is largest at intermediate latent
success probability and vanishes at the endpoints; it is not a measured
law of learning speed. Three consequences follow, each paired with the
measurement that could have falsified it. T1 states that the expected
ZVF indicator is exactly \(h_G(p)\). T2 states that \(h_G\) is strictly
decreasing in G for 0 \textless{} p \textless{} 1, with exponential
decay rate −ln max(p, 1−p). T3 states that with unnormalised
group-centred advantages the mean absolute advantage in a group is
exactly 2\ensuremath{\hat{p}}(1−\ensuremath{\hat{p}}).

The measured arithmetic sweep's mean ZVF declines with G (§6.4.1), but
the larger 368-record audit is not a verified independent replication
cohort (§7.1). Plugging a group's own observed success rate into the
null does not validate latent-p predictions: the observed indicator is
identically one at empirical \ensuremath{\hat{p}} = 0 or 1 and zero in the interior.
Likewise, reward-contrast identities do not establish gradient
magnitudes or learning gains. The retained scalar gradient correlation
lacks the underlying per-group gradient data needed for recomputation.

PCD is a magnitude-aware descriptive statistic, not a solution to sign
ambiguity: it too is symmetric under p ↔ 1−p.~Mean reward and the counts
of all-correct, all-wrong and mixed groups remain necessary. A proposed
allocation rule combining these signals still needs prospective testing.

The source reports one toy open-trainer seed per arm with unequal
sampling expenditure. Fixed GRPO and its centred-advantage comparison
use 120 rollouts; dynamic sampling uses 174 and adaptive G uses 186.
Their recorded held-out gains are respectively +0.500, +0.575, +0.550
and +0.575. These are small-task configuration outcomes, not
equal-budget causal estimates or replicated controller superiority. In
particular, zero ZVF among accepted dynamic-sampling batches is
purchased by rejecting already sampled candidates.

The remaining controller results are retrospective accounting or
model-based projections. Resampling an observed all-zero or all-one
group cannot produce an unseen outcome; its zero-rescue result is a
mathematical property of that plug-in procedure, not empirical evidence
that additional fresh model responses cannot restore contrast.
Beta-posterior and hindsight alternatives change those assumptions and
objectives, so their cost/restoration rankings cannot be treated as
measured policy gains. Their detailed numerical rankings are excluded
from the main results. The invalidated N10 outputs are excluded; their
implementation discrepancies belong to the audit history, and no
corrected replication is claimed here.

The historical name \texttt{Dualformer-Auto} denotes an internal
difficulty-gated group-size rule, not an implementation of the published
Dualformer method \citep{su2025dualformer}, which learns fast/slow
reasoning modes from randomized traces. Its shrink-only cost ratio must
not be combined with an escalation-only contrast-efficiency statistic.
The latter script computes \(1000R/(8F)\), where R is model-predicted
restored contrast and F counts fired prompts, assigning eight extra
completions to each G = 8 → 16 fire; a step-level fire first expands to
sixteen prompt fires. This denominator is gross positive escalation
expenditure, not a signed net rollout change. It supplies no valid
efficiency ranking for a rule whose net expenditure decreases, and the
cross-axis ranking is withdrawn.

Fresh-rollout evidence addresses a different, narrower question. The
historical V2 diagnostic (§7.4) and C1 (§7.5) directly observe
additional responses from frozen policies rather than
bootstrap-recycling eight rewards. They demonstrate that initial
homogeneity need not persist under their respective contracts, with
parser and numeric-correctness distinctions kept explicit. Their
selected cohorts, checkpoints and parsers differ from the historical
controller tensors; their rates cannot be transferred into a rescue
estimate for those runs or treated as controller benefit. A
matched-budget, multi-seed online comparison with a frozen trigger and
held-out endpoint remains unresolved.

\section{Historical V2 Frozen-Policy Fresh-Rollout
Diagnostic}\label{historical-v2-frozen-policy-fresh-rollout-diagnostic}

\textbf{Format-induced contrast is distinct from numeric contrast.} A
prospectively frozen diagnostic completed on 2 October 2026 found that
33 of 461 initially homogeneous eight-response groups became mixed after
24 additional responses: 7.16\%, with a prompt-level Wilson 95\%
interval of 5.14-9.88\%. Twenty of those first reward changes were
parser failures whose visible final number still agreed with the gold
answer. The remaining 13/461 (2.82\%) were assistant-audited
numeric-correctness contrasts: two wrong-to-correct recoveries and
eleven correct-to-wrong changes. Thus the raw 7.16\% measures variation
in this diagnostic's binary reward, not a useful learning-signal rate or
an accuracy gain.

The main cohort contained 512 GSM8K \textbf{train} prompts, each with 32
ordered responses from a frozen public Qwen/Qwen3-8B checkpoint in BF16
non-thinking mode. All 16,384 main responses completed. A disjoint
32-prompt smoke cohort was excluded from every main estimate. Each draw
was a fresh response to the same original prompt, with a unique request
seed, rather than a continuation of a previous answer. The primary
endpoint was predeclared: among prompts whose first eight binary rewards
all agreed, count those with both rewards in the combined 32. The strict
parser used exact rational matching; parser failures received reward
zero, while cap-hit outputs retained their exact-match reward and were
separately flagged. Neither kind of output was removed from the primary
denominator. Appendix B §B.12 gives the pinned revisions, decoder,
receipt chain and audit method.

The main corpus had 290/16,384 parser failures (1.77\%) and 18/16,384
cap hits (0.11\%), each below the predeclared 5\% quality threshold.
These global quality checks do not make a parser-induced reward change a
numeric error. Separate assistant reviewers reviewed all 33 initial
groups and first-changing responses in disjoint partitions, solving the
arithmetic and applying the frozen rubric; this was model-assisted
qualitative review, \textbf{not human annotation}. A coordinator
separately checked both recoveries and one bread-rise wording ambiguity.
That question's gold answer, 20 hours, assumes serial rises; it is
ambiguous rather than definitely wrong and was already excluded from the
numeric subset for a formatting failure. The two recoveries changed
eight initial answers of 1152 to the supported value
\(6(150+6\times30+6\times12)=2412\), and eight initial answers of 12 to
the supported coin total \(2+5+3.25+2+0.4+0.35=13\).

The predeclared clean-all-32 sensitivity was 12/439 (2.73\%). Because
eligibility requires all 32 outputs to be parseable and uncapped, it
selects on post-initial outcomes and cannot replace the primary
estimate. A \textbf{post-hoc exploratory} check found no later
parseable, uncapped numeric change in any of the 20 parser-first groups;
it leaves the frozen first-event audit unchanged and cannot predict
further draws.

This parser effect is \textbf{diagnostic-contract-specific}. The
repository's existing GSM8K test evaluator uses a more permissive
extractor, including numeric conclusions embedded in prose. These
strict-parser events are therefore not evidence of reward failures in
the historical evaluator or training runs. The 13 conservative numeric
witnesses show that eight equal observed rewards need not imply a fixed
numeric outcome on fresh draws for this checkpoint, task and decoder.
They do not reconstruct a historical checkpoint, demonstrate training or
controller benefit, identify a group-size optimum, or estimate held-out
capability. The earlier failed V1 engineering smoke remains separate and
unpooled.

\section{C1 Results with Reference and Unit
Boundaries}\label{c1-results-with-reference-and-unit-boundaries}

Benchmark reference errors, ambiguous questions and equivalent units
limit what this diagnostic can establish. Its frozen parser-defined
result is \textbf{11/64 questions (17.19\%; Wilson 95\% interval
9.88-28.21\%)}. Assistant-assisted reconciliation corroborates
\textbf{three numeric recovery witnesses on the same 64-question
denominator}. This 3/64 count (4.69\%) describes the support found under
a conservative review rule; it does not replace the frozen endpoint or
estimate a population recovery rate. The other eight parser events
include three convention-dependent interpretations, four materially
ambiguous cases and one match to an incorrect reference answer.
{[}S1-S4{]}

The study contains no training update. It does not measure useful
reinforcement-learning gradients, reasoning improvement, controller
superiority, held-out accuracy or general model capability. The prompts
are public GSM8K training questions and are fresh only to this campaign.
Historical V2 evidence remains separate and unpooled.
