The three corroborated witnesses are Santana presents (C00, source rank
676), Suzanne bread (C07, rank 912), and Hank reading (C38, rank 2699).
Santana's supported answer is \((4+7)-3=8\) presents; Suzanne's is
\(3\times2\times52/12=26\) loaves; Hank's is
\(5(30+60)+2\times2(30+60)=810\) minutes. Each has eight reviewed,
genuinely wrong initial final answers and at least one independently
correct fresh final answer. {[}S3{]}

Santana also shows why final-answer correctness cannot validate
reasoning. Its earliest successful fresh response, sample index 12,
returns eight through an incorrect argument that no first-half birthday
gifts were needed. Later successful indices 15 and 23 use the valid
\(11-3\) calculation. Hank's reference final value is supported even
though its reference explanation mislabels subtotals. These distinctions
remain visible in the event ledger. All sample and selected indices in
this addendum are zero-based. {[}S3{]}

The checkpoint case (C35, rank 2593) gives the opposite result.
Checkpoints at miles one and twenty-five enclose twenty-four miles
across three gaps, so the separation is eight miles. All eight initial
answers of eight are numerically correct. Four fresh outputs match the
erroneous reference value six by dividing by four. The frozen analysis
counts the event exactly as specified; semantic review does not call it
mathematical recovery. {[}S3{]}

The citations, raft and fundraiser cases have favorable ordinary
interpretations, but retain first-versus-second review disagreements.
They are described individually rather than combined with the three
corroborated witnesses into another adjusted recovery estimate. The
remaining four cases concern aggregation of walking distance, the
cake-and-ham probability event, the pet-owner denominator and Reggie's
missing initial marble count. A gold match does not resolve those
questions. {[}S3{]}

The item-level cross-tabulation below connects all 64 first-review
categories to the refined second-review categories. Each cell gives the
number of cases, with the number in the corroborated-reference-defect
union in parentheses. W = wrong final value; A = material ambiguity; I =
inconsistent premises; R = correct final value with flawed reference
explanation; S = supported; C = supported with conventional assumptions.
The two rounds used different taxonomies, so a change of category name
alone is not a disagreement. {[}S2, S4{]}

The defect union comprises fifteen wrong final values, two inconsistent
discrete-count questions with erroneous reference relations, three
correct finals with corroborated explanation defects, and two materially
ambiguous questions with explicit reference faults. Appendix G lists
their case IDs. The coin case C02 accounts for the uncorroborated W
cell, while C55 Sonia/Joss accounts for the uncorroborated R cell: its
notation defect was identified only in the second round. The fifteen
wrong-final judgments include an explicit equal-towel-weight assumption
in the towel case. The remaining second-review classifications are not
silently converted into cross-review consensus.

Thirteen first/second category disagreements are retained. The coin
question illustrates the reason: gross deposits total 225 cents and the
balance after withdrawal is 200 cents. The second review judges the
recorded 200 wrong for the requested deposits, while the first retains a
gross-versus-net target ambiguity. It is excluded from the narrower
fifteen. Ordinary mathematical conventions are not defects merely
because every physical assumption is unstated; genuinely different
quantities or plausible readings are described case by case. None of
these audit counts estimates whole-GSM8K error prevalence because the
cohort was selected for eight parser-zero outputs and then truncated.
{[}S2, S4{]}

Equivalent representations also matter. For Jackson ingredients (C36),
all eight initial values 0.24 are equivalent to the reference 24 percent
under the same conventional independent-selection probability
interpretation; formal odds would be 6:19. The scalar parser extracted
0.24 faithfully. For Gunner plugs (C62), 200 pairs equal 400 individual
plugs. Four initial responses preserve pair units, three remain
unit-ambiguous and one incorrectly calls 200 the number of individual
plugs. Neither case supports a claim of eight genuine mathematical
errors. The frozen scalar references and rewards remain unchanged.
{[}S5{]}

\subsection{Separate Prospective Parser
Validation}\label{separate-prospective-parser-validation}

The undeployed parser v3 candidate was frozen at 02:43:30 UTC on 3
October 2026, before the earliest target batch at 02:59:39 UTC. The
fixed target was the last 256 screening questions, eight responses each,
yielding 2,048 future outputs. This was a predefined future subset of an
ongoing study, not preregistration before the entire C1 campaign.
Historical candidate tuning is separate. C1's primary score remains
based on frozen parser v2. {[}S8, S9{]}

All 2,025 previously valid full parse objects were preserved. Four
previously rejected outputs at four distinct prompts became accepted:
one bold answer heading, two display-box blocks and one hash-prefixed
XML answer block. Two separately blinded, output-only assistant
annotations agreed on the same unambiguous scalar in all four changed
cases. No changed acceptance was ambiguous or incorrectly extracted
under those reviews. Nineteen outputs remained rejected. {[}S8, S9{]}

This supports only a narrow pass on the four observed extraction
changes. It does not establish semantic precision for the 2,025
unchanged outputs, validate the nineteen rejects, measure task
correctness, or estimate population false-positive rates. The responses
are clustered within prompts. Four examples do not exhaust their wrapper
classes or malformed-input behavior. The candidate remains undeployed
and is not used to recompute C1 recovery. {[}S9{]}

All source labels refer to Appendix G.



\chapter{The E1--E14 Infrastructure and Harness
Audit}\label{the-e1e14-infrastructure-and-harness-audit}

This chapter is an infrastructure and harness audit using one lightly
trained, near-base checkpoint, not a test of post-training efficacy. The
reported training budget is 40 steps, two prompts per step and G = 4: 80
prompt draws and 320 completions. Thirty-five steps meet a historical
scalar-loss threshold, but that counter establishes neither the number
of nonzero gradients nor the number of effective parameter updates
(§8.1.1). No convergence criterion, complete executed-source binding or
gradient/update-norm history survives. The fourteen lanes expose
failures of patch application, output extraction, serving, grading and
evidence retention. Their scores describe the checkpoint together with
those instruments; they cannot identify a GRPO training benefit
\citep{shao2024deepseekmath}. Here ``near-base'' describes the short
training budget and the recorded small adapter perturbation (§8.7), not
proven behavioral equivalence to the base model.

Original-contract results, exploratory protocol overrides and paired
small-scale checks have different evidential status. Item-level
decontamination is not established. The repository does not preserve
every cited artifact, and private recovery does not make a result
publicly reproducible. Appendix D records the lane detail and unresolved
sources.

Two reporting rules govern everything below, and they are worth stating
before any number appears. First, original-contract results and
replacement-scope results are never pooled and never averaged across
suites; a replacement scope is a different suite, a different interface
and often a different grader, and combining such figures would produce a
number with no referent. Second, rates computed from retained integer
counts are displayed as exact numerator/denominator with rounded
percentages, while source-only scalar scores retain their recorded
precision; and no comparison against a baseline, a sibling suite, or the
untrained base model is asserted for the campaign lanes of Sections
8.2--8.6. Those lanes have no matched base-model arm. The one paired
baseline comparison in this chapter is the small-scale
trained-versus-base rerun of Section 8.7 (Table 8.6): 14 lanes, 5 to 151
identical items each, one vLLM engine for both arms. Among the
comparisons with an applicable test or interval, none provides
conventional evidence of a nonzero effect; E4 has neither a test nor an
interval and remains descriptive. This chapter claims no improvement
over the base model. Where a lane's record itself contains an unresolved
discrepancy, the discrepancy is stated.

The consolidated ledger of record for the campaign is
\longtok{outputs/E1\_\allowbreak{}E14\_\allowbreak{}FINAL\_\allowbreak{}RESULTS\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-19.md}, which
supersedes the working tables of 2026-09-05 and 2026-09-12; the per-lane
terminal-state ledger,
\texttt{withheld\ historical\ campaign\ chronology}, was recovered from
the original Mac checkout on 3 October 2026 with updates through 21
September, while remaining absent from the frozen repository; and
selected arithmetic of the ledger was historically re-verified by
deterministic code checks recorded in
\longtok{outputs/verification/LEDGER\_\allowbreak{}CODE\_\allowbreak{}CHECK\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-19.json},
which reports eleven of eleven named checks passing with zero failures
(Appendix B lists their scope; they do not cover every figure in this
chapter).

\textbf{Evidence availability (private recovery audit, 3 October 2026).}
Six original files covering all five previously missing basenames were
recovered from the Mac checkout: the E10 native receipt, E8 diagnostics,
training-identity verification, E14 terminal note and two distinct
\texttt{Pending\_Experiments.md} snapshots. All remain absent from the
frozen repository, but their exact UTF-8 bytes and SHA-256 hashes are
preserved in the withheld source archive described in Appendix B §B.11.
A separately retained source audit records bounded read-only checks of
underlying E8/E10/E14 artifacts on the Mac: 29 exact reference-path hash
matches, 1,979 E8 archive/local member checks, 525 E10 archive-member
checks and E14 stored-disposition inspection. Exported E8 grade records
and E10 selected trace records permitted independent tally checks in the
retained private evidence audit; those underlying rows are not included
in this public document package; E14 exports aggregate traversal results
and two exclusion records, not all underlying rows. These
retained-source reports support stored evidence and arithmetic within
that scope, without rerunning models or scorers, rejudging correctness,
or reconstructing the missing training logs. It closes the author-side
recovery search for these six files only; it does not close the public
reproducibility gap or make the historical eleven-check ledger
independently runnable from this checkout. E14's recovered note contains
a denominator inconsistency, and the recovered finish ledger does not
substantiate the later E4 rerun; both are disclosed below.

\textbf{Benchmark identities and citation scope.} The original contracts
are SWE-bench Pro \citep{deng2025swebenchpro}, FrontierSWE
\citep{proximal2026frontierswe}, SDAB \citep{emulated2026sdab},
BankerToolBench \citep{lau2026bankertoolbench}, APEX-Agents
\citep{vidgen2026apexagents}, WebBench \citep{halluminatewebbench},
BinaryAudit \citep{quesma2026binaryaudit}, LifeSciBench
\citep{liu2026lifescibench}, MLE-bench \citep{chan2025mlebench},
AgentHarm \citep{andriushchenko2025agentharm}, VerilogEval
\citep{liu2023verilogeval}, App-Bench \citep{afterquery2025appbench},
OpenReward games \citep{openrewardplatform} and FrontierMath
\citep{glazer2024frontiermath}, respectively. Project/site references
are used where no archival paper was authenticated. These citations
establish identity, not access to a lane's contracted private scope or
verification of its historical execution.

The executed replacement scopes discussed here are SWE-bench
Multilingual \citep{khandpurswemultilingual}, CORE-Bench
\citep{siegel2024corebench}, tau3's banking\_knowledge component
associated with tau-Knowledge \citep{shi2026tauknowledge}, WebArena
\citep{zhou2024webarena}, LAB-Bench \citep{laurent2024labbench},
ML-Dev-Bench \citep{padigela2025mldevbench}, AgentDojo
\citep{debenedetti2024agentdojo}, BALROG \citep{paglieri2025balrog} and
Omni-MATH \citep{gao2024omnimath}. They retain separate contracts and
denominators (Appendix C). The 34 ML-Dev-Bench configurations are the
pinned campaign scope, not the paper's 30-task benchmark; public
identity checks do not repair that scope difference.

\section{The evaluated actor and the one-actor
design}\label{the-evaluated-actor-and-the-one-actor-design}

A single actor was evaluated across the whole campaign. The base model
is \texttt{Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B} at revision
\longtok{995ad96eacd98c81ed38be0c5b274b04031597b0}, and the trained
parameter set is the LoRA adapter \texttt{campaign-adapter} at commit
\texttt{withheld-adapter-revision}, served in bfloat16 under the
identifier \texttt{campaign-actor-bf16}. The public package does not
contain the original execution route or operational repository access
identities. It therefore cannot reproduce the original sampler execution
from these materials alone; no current provider-retention or
account-status claim is made here.

The campaign retained one nominal actor rather than a controlled
training comparison. The retained record does not establish a
convergence-based checkpoint-selection rationale for extending this
40-step run across fourteen suites. The principal campaign records one
nominal trained checkpoint; the recovered training-identity record lists
only API-Bank-RLVR and SWE-Gym (§8.1.1). The underlying suite families
are API-Bank \citep{li2023apibank} and SWE-Gym \citep{pan2025swegym};
those references do not authenticate the historical training
transformations or consumed rows. This does not exclude duplicated
content, pretraining exposure or evaluation-informed scaffold changes,
while the recovered E10 receipt explicitly reports
\longtok{TRAINING\_\allowbreak{}INVENTORY\_\allowbreak{}ABSENT} and makes no held-out claim. The
retained portfolio contract includes
\texttt{suite\_registry.agentdojo\_train} with role \texttt{train} and
split \texttt{training\ and\ development\ tasks}: it is a planned
curriculum entry, not a consumed-data log. The reason a particular E10
contract copied that declaration is not preserved; neither that field
nor the recovered identity note proves content-level decontamination.
Differences between suites reflect the nominal checkpoint together with
lane-specific serving conditions, interfaces, observation spaces, tool
conventions and graders, and they are not evidence about what a
specialised actor could achieve. The campaign also cannot support any
statement about seed variance. Inference conditions were frozen per lane
and recorded in that lane's own receipt (§8.1.2).

\subsection{How the actor was trained}\label{how-the-actor-was-trained}

The adapter is attributed to a single Tinker training run (W\&B run
\texttt{withheld-id}). The recovered
\longtok{training\_\allowbreak{}identity\_\allowbreak{}verified.json} records that run ID,
aggregate dataset revision, start time, base Git commit and hashes of
\texttt{config.yaml}, \texttt{wandb-metadata.json} and
\texttt{output.log}. Its status is
\longtok{TRAINING\_\allowbreak{}CONFIG\_\allowbreak{}VERIFIED\_\allowbreak{}NOT\_\allowbreak{}CONTENT\_\allowbreak{}DECONTAMINATION}, and
it states that the dirty source diff was not uploaded. The underlying
W\&B files and local run receipt have not been recovered in this
package, so their bytes cannot yet be matched afresh to those recorded
hashes. Appendix D separates these surviving identity fields from the
still-reported configuration details in Table 8.1.

\textbf{Reported training record of the E1--E14 actor.} The
identity-verification artifact is recovered; underlying local run files
remain unavailable in this package. Current-code evidence is labelled
separately.

\longtok{config.yaml} \\
LoRA & rank 32, alpha 32, all linear layers & \longtok{config.yaml};
adapter config \\
Decoding during training & prompt 1,536 and response 384 tokens;
temperature 0.7, top-p 0.95; seed 809 & \longtok{config.yaml} \\
Reward trace & mean 0.025 over the first 5 steps and 0.2425 over the
last 10; reward zero on 22 of 40 steps; 35 of 40 steps satisfying the
historical scalar-loss threshold & run receipt; \longtok{output.log} \\
Training time & started 2026-08-09; runtime 1,934 s & historical
training metadata; underlying files remain unavailable \\
Checkpoints & Scientific schedule reported at step 0, step 20, step 40
and final; the final adapter follows step 40 without an additional
optimizer step & Historical scientific training record;
execution-specific repository paths omitted \\
Execution provenance & Exact private execution identities and access
paths are withheld; the public package cannot replay the original
execution route & Public-source limitation, Appendix I \\
\end{longtable}

Four items remain unavailable or were never created --- the exact
training code, which 80 training prompts were drawn, the Tinker sampler
weights, and any held-out training-time evaluation --- and the run
departs from the planned Pavlov protocol; both are recorded in Appendix
D so that no reader fills them in.

What this means for the chapter is direct. The actor received 40 steps
of sparse-reward, critic-free policy-gradient training, with 35 steps
satisfying its historical scalar-loss threshold. This counter does not
establish how many policy gradients or parameter updates were zero.
Future telemetry must distinguish all-advantages-zero, policy-gradient
norm and parameter-update norm. The fourteen suite results are
properties of this checkpoint, not evidence about what GRPO-style
training contributes. The campaign is reported as an infrastructure and
evidence audit; its governance failures are part of the finding. The
only trained-versus-base evidence is the small-sample paired comparison
of Table 8.6.

\subsection{Serving conditions per
lane}\label{serving-conditions-per-lane}

The E4 investigation (§8.5) identified a serving bridge that sends no
explicit stop sequences. The source-level defect is independently
inspectable, but the historically reported 100-trial zero is not
accepted as a verified empirical result. The same bridge,
\longtok{zvf-program/flagship/modal\_\allowbreak{}tinker\_\allowbreak{}openai\_\allowbreak{}bridge.py}, served
several other lanes, so Table 8.2 records for every lane how the actor
was served and whether that defect could apply. Its
\texttt{/v1/responses} route hard-codes \texttt{stop=None} and thinking
on. Its \texttt{/v1/chat/completions} route sends stop sequences only if
the client supplies them, and the agent clients used here (opencode,
mini-swe-agent and the APEX client) do not. The historically reported
deployed bridge hash (\texttt{f59c08b9…}) matches neither retained
committed version, so current-code inspection cannot authenticate the
exact deployed bytes.

lane.}

The recovered E8 diagnostic now supports its aggregate failure counts,
and the recovered E10 receipt supports its declared inference limits.
Other entries still depend on unrecovered raw responses or finish-era
sources, so recovery of these two receipts does not verify every serving
and failure count in the table. Two consequences follow. First, the
lanes served through the bridge (E2, E4, E5, E7 and the E9 streaming
arm) may be exposed to the E4 mechanism, but exposure does not
demonstrate that it caused their failures; the E4 full-run account
remains unverified. Second, the reported format and length failures mean
that E8, E11 and E14 headline figures are end-to-end harness scores;
§8.2 distinguishes their sensitivity figures and missing evidence. E11
has substantial extraction failures under a tight generation cap, E14
has a large capped/uncapped accuracy difference, and E1 has widespread
patch-application failures. These are end-to-end protocol outcomes; the
evidence does not isolate the causal contribution of budget in E11 or
E14 (§8.2, §8.3). A note on the scope of the stop-sequence mechanism is
in Appendix D.

\section{Suites with strictly complete
scopes}\label{suites-with-strictly-complete-scopes}

The historical ledger and its check log report three strictly complete
scopes: E8, E10 and E11. E8 and E10 are replacement scopes. E11 is an
original-contract full-suite result that also meets the
strict-completeness check, because the suite evaluated is VerilogEval
itself; the ledger lists it both among its replacement scopes and among
its original-contract results, but it is one run and one result. A
fourth, E14, is recorded terminal-complete under its native protocol in
the recovered terminal note, subject to its denominator inconsistency
and the raw-output limits below. The six replacement scopes completed on
27 September postdate that check and are reported separately in §8.4 (Figures~\ref{fig:e8} and~\ref{fig:e11}).

Accuracy is strongly category-dependent, so no pooled cross-category average is drawn or claimed. CloningScenarios is a historically reported zero (0/33). The original diagnostic file was recovered and byte-verified on 3 October (Appendix B); its aggregate counts agree with these bars.}

\fig{fig_e11_decomposition}

{VerilogEval decomposition: two native framings of 156 tasks, 67/156 code-completion and 62/156 spec-to-RTL, summing to $129/312 = 41.35\%$ pass@1. The dashed top bar is the sum of the two rows, not a third measurement. Graded by the suite's native harness (receipt \longtok{e11\_\allowbreak{}full\_\allowbreak{}receipt.json}); the $129/311 = 41.48\%$ value that excludes \longtok{Prob099\_\allowbreak{}m2014\_\allowbreak{}q6c} is a non-canonical sensitivity only.}
{fig:e11}




\textbf{E8 --- LAB-Bench, public split.} The recovered
\longtok{outputs/public\_\allowbreak{}portfolio\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-05/labbench\_\allowbreak{}final\_\allowbreak{}diagnostics.json}
records all 1,967 expected items evaluated across eight categories, with
450 correct and 1,517 incorrect responses. Those counts give 450/1967 =
0.2288 (Wilson 95\% CI {[}0.211, 0.248{]}, unit = question, n = 1,967).
It also records 1,259 unparsed responses
(\longtok{native\_\allowbreak{}answer\_\allowbreak{}parse\_\allowbreak{}none}) and 1,086 length-truncated
finishes. The supplementary validation found the native receipt at its
recorded hash and checked 1,979 archive members against both declared
and local hashes. Its 1,967 unique exported grade records independently
corroborate the correct/incorrect totals, category counts and 1,086
length finishes. The 1,259 parse-none count remains a field of the
byte-preserved diagnostic: the exported grade records contain no
parsed-answer field for a separate recount. The response-level
intersection of parse-none and length-truncated outputs is
\textbf{unavailable}: retained compact grade rows contain finish reasons
but no parsed-answer membership. From the two reported margins alone,
the overlap can only be bounded between max(0, 1,259 + 1,086 − 1,967) =
378 and min(1,259, 1,086) = 1,086 responses. These are arithmetic
bounds, not an observed cross-tabulation, and even a measured overlap
would not by itself identify failures caused by the token cap. The
reported score is an end-to-end harness score with substantial
formatting and length limitations. The conditional descriptive
sensitivity remains 450/708 = 0.636 (Wilson 95\% CI {[}0.600, 0.670{]}),
not the headline or a direct measure of domain knowledge; its
response-level classification has not been independently regraded.
Per-category figures and evidence limits are in Appendix D.

\textbf{E10 --- AgentDojo, benign utility scope.} The recovered
\longtok{outputs/public\_\allowbreak{}portfolio\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-05/agentdojo\_\allowbreak{}native\_\allowbreak{}receipt.json}
records status \longtok{COMPLETE\_\allowbreak{}NATIVE\_\allowbreak{}BENIGN\_\allowbreak{}UTILITY}, 97 completed
episodes, 88 utility passes, AgentDojo v1.2.2 and decontamination status
\longtok{TRAINING\_\allowbreak{}INVENTORY\_\allowbreak{}ABSENT}. Its score is 88/97 = 0.9072
(Wilson 95\% CI {[}0.833, 0.950{]}, unit = episode, n = 97). The receipt
binds the benchmark version but contains no upstream commit field; the
previously attributed commit
\longtok{089ed468cf3ed0322acc66b0211f26d9d90dbf60} is therefore not
verified by this receipt. Its scope is native default benign utility
only: \texttt{security\_evaluation} and \texttt{heldout\_claim} are
false, while \texttt{attack} and \texttt{defense} are null. Completion
and utility are distinct: 97 completed episodes do not mean 97 correct
episodes. The supplementary validation checks 525 archived member pins
and maps all 97 selected episodes to unique exact-hash traces (94
original and 3 explicit continuation), whose stored utility fields
independently tally to 88 passes and zero handled errors. It excludes
extra historical partial traces from that mapping. This is stored-trace
corroboration, not a rerun of AgentDojo or an independent judgement of
task correctness. The earlier 94-episode attempt and reported collector
repair retain their separate missing-source limitations in Appendix D.

\textbf{E11 --- VerilogEval, two native framings.} All 312 pinned prompt
instances were evaluated under the upstream NVlabs/verilog-eval harness,
but they represent 156 underlying designs, each appearing in
code-completion (\texttt{code-complete-iccad2023}) and
specification-to-RTL framing. The descriptive result remains 129 passes
of 312 = 0.4135, decomposed into 67/156 = 0.4295 for code-completion and
62/156 = 0.3974 for specification-to-RTL. Because paired framings share
a design, a Wilson interval treating all 312 outcomes as independent is
inappropriate. Resampling the 156 design pairs together gives a 95\%
percentile cluster-bootstrap interval of {[}0.343, 0.487{]} (200,000
replicates, seed 20261002). This describes design-composition
uncertainty under an exchangeable-design assumption for this checkpoint
and these fixed sampled generations; it is not training-seed,
decoding-seed or protocol uncertainty. The observed full
pinned-benchmark fraction itself remains descriptive. The surviving
receipt records 150 extraction failures included in the denominator, so
129/312 is an end-to-end harness score: 150 of its 183 failures are
extraction failures. A generation-budget mechanism is plausible but not
identified (corrected; see Table E.1). The run used a 1,024-token cap
with the chat template's default thinking mode on, and the receipt's
312,622 response tokens give a mean of about 1,002 per sample. That mean
shows near-cap lengths; without per-response text or finish reasons it
cannot establish how many outputs reached the cap or where their
reasoning blocks ended. On the 50 items of the 26 September small-scale
rerun (§8.7), the receipt's verdicts give 23/50, while the same trained
adapter with thinking off and a 4,096-token cap scores 33/50 with no
extraction failures (11 items pass only in the second run and 1 only in
the first; exact McNemar p ≈ 0.006). That comparison also changes engine
and temperature (managed sampler at 0.2 against vLLM at 0) and covers 50
of 312 prompts, so it shows an improvement under the combined protocol
changes, not an isolated budget effect or a corrected score. The
extractor also accepted a module found inside an unterminated reasoning
block, and per-sample response text was not kept, so some of the 162
extracted candidates, and possibly some passes, may be drafts from the
reasoning rather than final answers. The parseable-only descriptive
sensitivity is 129/162 = 0.796, hard to interpret for that reason; no
interval is given because the receipt's aggregate extraction count does
not provide the per-design extraction indicators needed for a clustered
conditional interval. One prompt,
\longtok{verilog\_eval/spec-to-rtl/Prob099\_\allowbreak{}m2014\_\allowbreak{}q6c}, fails against
its own test bench under both simulators; excluding it yields 129/311 =
0.4148, but no immutable justification for the exclusion was captured,
so the raw 129/312 is canonical and 129/311 is a noncanonical
sensitivity only. Pair counts, the bootstrap method, harness revisions,
verdict rule and amendment are in Appendix D.

\textbf{E14 --- Omni-MATH replacement scope, receipt-recorded
terminal-complete.} The surviving ledger and historical arithmetic check
record 4,426 accepted rows of 4,428 and native judged-row accuracy
2,271/4,426 = 51.31 per cent (recorded decimal 0.5131043831902395).
Retaining 2,271 passes but counting both unjudged rows as failures gives
the conservative all-disposition diagnostic 2,271/4,428 = 51.29 per cent
(Wilson 95\% CI {[}0.498, 0.528{]}), 0.02 percentage points below the
judged-row rate. The recovered
\longtok{outputs/PES\_\allowbreak{}Phase2\_\allowbreak{}Review\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-12/finish/e14\_\allowbreak{}terminal\_\allowbreak{}note\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-19.json}
records truncated judge outputs before an Equivalence Judgement section,
two recorded skip reasons, no re-judge and terminal completion at 4,428
dispositions. The note nevertheless labels \texttt{2271/4428} as
\texttt{0.5131043831902395}, a denominator inconsistency: that decimal
equals 2,271/4,426, not 2,271/4,428 (corrected; see Table E.1). The
original note is preserved unchanged, and this thesis retains both
clearly labelled arithmetic conventions. The supplementary Mac-side
traversal found the stored disposition, judged-row and
new-judge-response files with 4,428 valid JSON rows apiece. It reports
4,426 accepted dispositions, 2,271 correct and two skipped, and exports
both exclusion records with missing Equivalence Judgement headings. It
also records the native score's explicit 4,426 denominator and the
separate all-disposition diagnostic. The full E14 rows are not exported
here, so the cloud check verifies the exported arithmetic and exclusions
rather than independently recounting all 4,428 rows or rejudging any
mathematics. The 2,048-token actor cap is an important protocol
limitation, with a strong observational association between cap hits and
errors (corrected reading; see Table E.1). Recounting the stored
dispositions, the 2,982 responses that hit the cap are correct in 1,029
cases (34.5\%), against 1,242 of the 1,446 uncapped responses (85.9\%).
The split is confounded by difficulty, since harder problems both run
longer and fail more, so it does not identify what a larger budget would
score; neither the native judged-row score nor the all-disposition
diagnostic should be read as the actor's Omni-MATH capability
independent of its generation budget.

\section{Original-contract results}\label{original-contract-results}

Seven lanes have an original-contract figure, reported separately from
any replacement scope with its coverage caveats; two are full-suite
results. Lane detail is in Appendix D.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{E1 --- SWE-bench Pro.} 2 of 731 tasks resolved, a pass@1 of
  2/731 = 0.00274, or 0.274 per cent (Wilson 95\% CI {[}0.08\%,
  0.99\%{]}, unit = task), over the complete 731-row test split of
  \texttt{ScaleAI/SWE-bench\_Pro}. The fourteen generation failures and
  four lost artifacts stay in the denominator. The 713 ``native
  evaluations'' are not 713 tests of a candidate fix (corrected reading;
  see Table E.1). Parsed with \texttt{git\ apply}, 593 of the 713
  submitted patches are rejected as corrupt, because their \texttt{@@}
  hunk headers state line counts that do not match the hunk bodies; the
  official entry script runs a plain \texttt{git\ apply} and then the
  tests, so for those 593 the tests ran against the unpatched
  repository. With \texttt{git\ apply\ -\/-recount}, 711 of 713 parse.
  Both resolved instances are among the 120 patches that parse. The
  2/731 is therefore an end-to-end issue-resolution score with a major
  patch-format limitation. It does not isolate semantic problem-solving
  ability. No semantic test-harness rerun of the 591 additionally
  parseable patches is present in the retained evidence or performed for
  this revision, so their post-repair pass@1 remains unknown.
\item
  \textbf{E2 --- FrontierSWE.} 1 of 17 tasks evaluated,
  replay-normalised score 0.8628; partial, because revision-bound
  authorisation for the remaining sixteen was never obtained.
\item
  \textbf{E4 --- BankerToolBench.} 1 of 100 tasks evaluated, recovery
  metric 0.3115 --- a verifier recovery metric on a single task, not the
  simple fraction 37/128 and not a suite score; the pass3/pass16 archive
  is missing.
\item
  \textbf{E5 --- APEX-Agents.} 7 of 480 tasks natively scored. The
  prefix mean of 0.050505 treats unscored attempts as zero and is not a
  native 480-task score; the seven scored tasks average 0.079365.
\item
  \textbf{E7 --- BinaryAudit.} One of 46 attempts recorded, verifier
  reward 0.0 after an agent error, no grade.
\item
  \textbf{E9 --- MLE-bench.} 40 of 75 competitions carry native grades,
  a coverage of 53.33 per cent, and the suite score is null; a separate
  merged-vLLM arm holds one valid grade (H\&M, 0.02132) and is not
  combined.
\item
  \textbf{E11 --- VerilogEval} is reported in §8.2; it is the second
  original-contract full-suite result, alongside E1, and not an
  additional one.
\end{itemize}

No lane here has a matched base-model run, so none licenses a claim that
the actor outperforms its untrained counterpart, a predecessor model, or
a published number.

\section{Exploratory protocol-override replacement runs, 27 September
2026}\label{exploratory-protocol-override-replacement-runs-27-september-2026}

At the 19 September ledger of record, six replacement scopes were
started but incomplete: E1 (SWE-bench Multilingual, 35 of 300 graded),
E2 (CORE-Bench, 45 capsule setups, no grades), E5 (Tau3, 20 of 97
cleaned) and E13 (BALROG, 13 of 255 episodes) were launch-pending, and
E6 (WebArena, 0 of 812) and E9 (MLDevBench, 0 of 34) were
\texttt{PENDING\_QUOTA} because required execution capacity was
unavailable. The six later receipts account for their attempted
replacement scopes on 27 September, subject to the missing grades and
split contracts below.

The later launch used a lead-issued protocol override rather than
independent approval: the candidate was both experiment lead and
override author. These are \textbf{exploratory protocol-override
replacement runs}, outside the original prospective fail-closed
acceptance gates, not canonical completions of the original contracts.
They retain receipts and declared deviations, so ``ungoverned'' would
overstate the absence of all controls; nevertheless, they do not
validate the promised independent-approval process. The public
derivative omits operational authorization records and account limits,
and Table 8.4 preserves the scientific departures.

\Needspace{10\baselineskip}\addcontentsline{lot}{table}{\protect\numberline{8.3}Exploratory protocol-override replacement results, 27 September 2026}\textbf{Table
8.3.} Exploratory protocol-override replacement results, 27 September

multiple attempts, under the stated lane contract and native grader.
Attempt history and native-verdict coverage are separate from task
accounting. Errors, timeouts and ungraded items count as failures in the
denominator. Graded counts items with a native verdict. Intervals are
Wilson 95\% for rates; E13's uses BALROG's standard error, and its score
is the mean of six per-environment means.

The six figures are on six different benchmarks with six different
metrics, and they are not averaged, pooled with the same lane's
original-contract result, or compared with a base model. Five lanes (E2,
E5, E6, E9, E13) have outcomes accounted for over their replacement
scope, with incomplete native grading in E6. E6 retains 108 unknown
verdicts; for these fixed outputs its missing-judgment bound is 90/812
to 198/812, between 0.111 and 0.244, not a confidence interval or a
bound on new attempts. E1 has two separately reported scores, 1/190 and
4/110 (the earlier waves, served at a different context), that together
cover all 300 tasks but are not combined. E5's pass\^{}1 over the 72
tasks without an infrastructure error, 8/72 = 0.111, is a sensitivity
figure only, and E13's 26.1 per cent is not compared with the 26-episode
small-scale E13 rows of Tables 8.5 and 8.6. Per-lane narratives are in
Appendix D.

\section{E4: verifiable bridge behavior and an unresolved run
history}\label{e4-verifiable-bridge-behavior-and-an-unresolved-run-history}

The retained bridge code provides inspectable infrastructure evidence.
In \longtok{zvf-program/flagship/modal\_\allowbreak{}tinker\_\allowbreak{}openai\_\allowbreak{}bridge.py}, the
\texttt{/v1/responses} handler calls \texttt{\_complete} with
\texttt{stop=None}; the sampler converts this to an empty stop list and
decodes with \longtok{skip\_\allowbreak{}special\_\allowbreak{}tokens=True}. No explicit
end-of-turn stop is supplied by that path. This creates a plausible
route to turn-boundary errors, but EOS behavior and the exact deployed
version still matter. The retained protocol tests also explicitly
confirm that prior function-call items are omitted by the
responses-to-chat converter. Re-running
\texttt{PYTHONPATH=\allowbreak{}zvf-\allowbreak{}program\ python\ -\allowbreak{}m\ unittest\ flagship.test\_\allowbreak{}\allowbreak{}tinker\_\allowbreak{}\allowbreak{}openai\_\allowbreak{}\allowbreak{}bridge\_\allowbreak{}\allowbreak{}protocol}
on 18 October passed all 18 offline tests. These tests exercise
conversion and response formatting; they do not reproduce generation,
prove the effect of a missing stop, or authenticate a historical
deployment.

The historical full-rerun account is quarantined as \textbf{unverified}.
\longtok{outputs/e1\_\allowbreak{}e14\_\allowbreak{}small\_\allowbreak{}scale\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-26/E4/result.json}, field
\longtok{prior\_\allowbreak{}base\_\allowbreak{}run}, attributes 100 trials and mean reward 0.0 to
21 September. The recovered pending-state ledger says the rerun was not
launched at its snapshot updated through the same date. With no precise
launch timestamp or raw job receipt, these accounts cannot be ordered or
reconciled. A later secondary summary cannot establish that execution
occurred. Consequently, the earlier step-count histogram and trajectory
counts are removed from the empirical presentation; the reported
aggregate zero is not used in a capability estimate or causal
comparison. A filesystem creation timestamp from the original machine is
not available, and a checkout timestamp would not establish execution
time.

The independently retained six-task pilot reports a positive verifier
reward of 0.4087 on \texttt{btb-19b3361c} through a different shim
(§8.7). It establishes that pilot outcome only. It cannot validate the
missing full run or isolate stop-token effects because serving path,
tasks and caps differ. No verified full-suite BankerToolBench capability
estimate exists for this actor family. Appendix D §D.7 records the exact
source conflict and the evidence required to resolve it.

\section{Externally blocked lanes and terminal
states}\label{externally-blocked-lanes-and-terminal-states}

Seven original benchmark contracts remain outside the completed
evaluation scope: SDAB, BinaryAudit, LifeSciBench, AgentHarm, AppBench,
OpenReward and FrontierMath. Their required benchmark payload or hosted
evaluation scope is not available in this public package. Six are
recorded as externally closed; OpenReward proceeds only through the
separate BALROG replacement scope. These statuses are scope limits and
not graded model failures. Private access correspondence, operational
requests and account state are omitted. The following small-scale reruns
keep original and substitute benchmarks separate (Figure~\ref{fig:taxonomy}).


\fig{fig_terminal_taxonomy}
{Terminal-state taxonomy used to close every lane}
{Terminal-state taxonomy used to close every lane. Each state has a defined meaning and a named reopen condition where one exists. The seven states are the ledger's full vocabulary; 17 lane-states cover 14 lanes because E8, E10 and E14 carry separate original and replacement rows. States classify lanes, not results: an unrecognised state is recorded as blocked rather than absorbed into a percentage, and original-contract and replacement-scope numbers are never pooled. The completion gate's \texttt{PARTIAL\_EXACT}/\texttt{PARTIAL\_RECOVERY} labels are a separate evidence-class vocabulary (Chapter~5). Lane assignments are those of 2026-09-19; the six lanes in the three pending leaves were all run and scored on 2026-09-27 (Chapter~8).}
{fig:taxonomy}



\section{Small-scale reruns of all fourteen
lanes}\label{small-scale-reruns-of-all-fourteen-lanes}

This section adds a separate, smaller set of runs, made on 26 September
2026, to give every one of the fourteen lanes a measured number under
one fixed protocol, including the lanes that never produced a score.
None of these figures is pooled with, averaged against, or substituted
for a result reported earlier in this chapter.

The protocol fixes one actor family, a fixed item-selection seed
(20260926), temperature 0 and a non-thinking chat template, and scores
each lane with its native grader where one could be run. Lanes whose
original benchmark is externally blocked were run on a public
substitute, and each substitute's gap from the original suite is
recorded in its lane file. Items that errored or timed out are counted
as failures in the denominator.

Two arms were run. The first is the base model,
\texttt{Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B} with no adapter, sampled on Tinker (Table
8.5). The second is a paired comparison of the trained actor against the
same base model, both served by one vLLM configuration on Modal, on
identical items (Table 8.6). The pairing exists because the adapter's
effect on individual token probabilities was measured to be about the
same size as the numerical difference between the two serving engines on
the same base weights, so a trained-on-vLLM against base-on-Tinker
comparison would confound the adapter with the engine, and none is made.

For E10, let \(r_i\) denote the recorded refusal indicator, \(s_i\) the
recorded task score, and H and B be the 30 retained \texttt{harmful/}
and 30 \texttt{benign/} rows in \texttt{E10/paired.json}. The displayed
refusal rate is \(\sum_{i\in H} r_i/30\) (21/30 trained, 20/30 base);
harm score is \(\sum_{i\in H} s_i/30\); benign score is
\(\sum_{i\in B} s_i/30\). The continuous scores measure task completion,
not the complement of refusal. Their retained means reproduce 0.2467
versus 0.2362 and 0.7799 versus 0.7944. The upstream AgentHarm rubric
averages a task-specific set of grading-function checks over tool-use
behavior, with semantic judging for some checks and refusal recorded
separately. The public paired file does not preserve each check, scorer
revision or judge configuration, so that rubric identity cannot
authenticate the exact historical grading implementation; the
reproducible result here is the aggregation of the retained scores, not
a fresh semantic regrade (Appendix D §D.9).

These are small samples. Most lanes score between five and eighty items,
so a single item moves a lane's rate by several percentage points and
the confidence intervals are wide. In Table 8.6, b and c count items
solved only by the trained actor and only by the base model
respectively, and the CI is a paired bootstrap interval on the
difference. Four lane-specific caveats matter. For E12 the 151 rubric
items come from only six generated applications, so its item-level
McNemar p-value treats clustered observations as independent; the
corresponding item-level Wilson interval is also omitted from Table 8.5;
the exact sign-flip test over the six equally weighted per-app
differences gives p = 0.25, whereas the displayed −0.033 is rubric-item
weighted; that p-value is not assigned to the displayed item-weighted
effect. The sign-flip test requires justified within-application label
exchangeability or symmetry of paired differences, and the grader is an
LLM judge that is the base model itself, so the E12 rates have a direct
self-evaluation bias risk that pairing does not remove. No independent
judge regrade is available; the direction and size of that bias are
unknown, and this row cannot support a model-quality ranking. For E4,
only \texttt{btb-19b3361c} scores above zero in either arm (trained
0.397, base 0.557; 0.409 in Table 8.5), so the table omits an interval:
an empirical bootstrap is computable but has little support for
inference beyond these six tasks. For E13, the 1.875 difference is the
mean of per-environment differences computed from unrounded per-item
values (recomputed 1.8752); the source rounds the two arm means to two
decimals, so subtracting 25.51 − 23.63 loses precision. For E9, the one
competition that earned a medal in the Tinker run (nomad2018) has a
46.8k-token prompt that exceeds the 32,768-token vLLM limit and fails
identically in both vLLM arms, so the vLLM medal rates are not
comparable with Table 8.5. The paired differences are not combined
across lanes, and none of the comparisons with an applicable test or
interval provides conventional evidence of a nonzero effect; E4 is
descriptive only, and the reported tests have different units,
assumptions and support. At these sizes that is an inconclusive result,
not evidence of no effect: six discordances all in one direction suffice
for a two-sided exact McNemar p \textless{} 0.05, but unanimity is not
necessary: eight in one direction and one in the other give p =
0.0390625. No equivalence test was run. Fuller E12 detail and the
principal per-lane caveats from each result file are in Appendix D.

\section{Summary of the campaign at the point of
writing}\label{summary-of-the-campaign-at-the-point-of-writing}

By the 19 September ledger the campaign had established two full-suite
results and one complete replacement scope with a score: E1 at 2/731 =
0.274 per cent on SWE-bench Pro, E11 at 129/312 = 41.35 per cent pass@1
(design-cluster bootstrap 95\% interval {[}34.3, 48.7{]}, n = 156
designs) across its two framings, and E8 at 450/1967 = 0.2288 ({[}0.211,
0.248{]}) on the LAB-Bench public split, alongside a complete
benign-utility evaluation of E10 at 88 of 97 episodes (0.907, {[}0.833,
0.950{]}), a terminal-complete E14 replacement scope with native
judged-row accuracy 2,271/4,426 = 51.31 per cent and all-disposition
diagnostic 2,271/4,428 = 51.29 per cent, and five original-contract
partials (E2, E4, E5, E7, E9), none of which is a suite score. Three
scores have substantial protocol limitations: 593 of E1's 713 submitted
patches were corrupt for \texttt{git\ apply}; E11 has 150 extraction
failures under a 1,024-token cap with thinking on; and E14's capped
responses are correct at 34.5\% against 85.9\% uncapped. The latter
split is confounded by problem difficulty, and the E11 comparison
changes several conditions together, so their causal budget effects
remain unidentified (§8.2, §8.3). On 27 September six exploratory
replacement scopes were attempted under a self-issued override outside
the original prospective gates, with deviations recorded per lane
(Tables 8.3 and 8.4). Seven original contracts remain externally
blocked, and no lane now waits on a launch or a cloud quota. The
campaign did not establish a multi-seed estimate, a held-out security
result, a BankerToolBench suite score, a graded result for the 108
WebArena tasks that need an LLM judge, or a baseline comparison for the
campaign lanes themselves; the only trained-versus-base comparison,
Table 8.6, provides no conventional evidence of a nonzero effect among
tested comparisons, while E4 remains descriptive without a test or
interval. The E8 diagnostic, E10 native receipt, training-identity
verification, E14 terminal note and both pending-state snapshots are
recovered in the private package while remaining absent from the frozen
repository. Their contents were inspected in the retained private audit,
but are not independently inspectable from this public package; reported
stored-payload checks support E8/E10 counts and the E14 denominator
distinction; training-source, E4 raw-evidence and semantic-reproduction
limits remain as in §8.2 and Appendix D. The available evidence chains,
reproduction paths and gaps are given in Appendix B; the next chapter
draws the cross-study synthesis and conclusions (Figure~\ref{fig:lanes}).

Their receipts retain evidence, but these runs did not satisfy the original prospective acceptance path. A complete coverage label does not establish complete grading, execution-source provenance or training benefit. Original contracts and replacements are never pooled.}
{fig:lanes}

\part{Synthesis}


\chapter{Cross-Study Synthesis, Threats to Validity, Conclusions and
Future
Work}\label{cross-study-synthesis-threats-to-validity-conclusions-and-future-work}

\section{Cross-Study Synthesis}\label{cross-study-synthesis}

This report presents two bodies of evidence with different instruments.
The P1--P7 corpus examines short-horizon GRPO-style post-training and
produces a diagnostic layer, machine-readable registry and controller
audit. The E1--E14 campaign is an infrastructure and harness audit of
one lightly trained checkpoint, with incomplete execution provenance and
later exploratory protocol overrides. It does not test training
efficacy. The common methodological commitment is attribution: separate
measured outcomes from reconstructions, preserve denominators and nulls,
and narrow a claim whenever the data cannot isolate its cause.

The retained contribution is strongest where the evidence can be
recomputed without inferring an unobserved training process. Three
stored Qwen3-8B reward tensors reproduce ZVF values 0.130, 0.190 and
0.155, pooled to 0.1583. Exact ties, sample-variance thresholds and
population-variance conventions are kept distinct. The binomial identity
explains why both all-correct and all-wrong groups can be homogeneous;
fitted prompt-difficulty heterogeneity is compatible with the observed
excess over a homogeneous null, without identifying dependence or
useful-gradient loss. ZVF is therefore an instrument for a specified
reward tensor, not a new diagnostic or a validated predictor of training
benefit (§6.3) \citep{he2026avspo, nie2026starvation}.

Several earlier headline interpretations do not survive source
inspection. P1's heterogeneous scale anchors include interrupted traces
and confounded recipes; their fits cannot establish either a scaling law
or a scaling null. The nine-method mitigation panel is synthetic, and
the risk-index panel combines simulated rows with imputed collapse
features; neither validates an intervention or predictor. The arithmetic
PPO/GRPO comparison uses different evaluation items and unequal overlap
with training, so its difference and equivalence tests do not estimate
an isolated algorithm effect. Larger-budget group-size points are
reconstructions rather than trained arms. These exclusions are not
negative experimental results (§§6.2--6.4).

The capped P4 records describe observed lengths but do not test
unconstrained verbosity. A 200-token cap leaves little room for
expansion; the raised-cap extension also begins close to its limit and
lacks a held-out evaluation or immutable executed-source binding.
Separately, nominally unchanged pre-training evaluations disagree on
seven of 200 items, so the historical pre/post tests do not isolate
learning from evaluation variability. The late-efficiency statistic is
exploratory training-metric evidence. Correcting its test does not
convert it into a held-out capability result (§6.5).

The separately contracted v2 unsaturated comparison is the principal
interim algorithm comparison, with its numerical snapshot preserved in
Table 6.2. It remains provisional because planned PPO seeds and the
exposure-matched arm are incomplete. Joint changes to critic
initialization, generation cap, scoring and evaluation scope prevent
attributing the v1--v2 difference to a single repair. The five-arm
variant audit likewise supplies no multiplicity-adjusted rejection or
established equivalence. A completed, adequately powered estimator
comparison remains open.

P5 and P6 contribute a reporting specification and implemented checks.
The nominal 17× training-reward contrast changes checkpoint, reward,
template and other settings; it cannot attribute an effect to backend.
The retained 98-cell mega subset is sampling-only, not 98 training runs,
and cannot validate claims about training variance or outcome
prediction. The 368 service records include failed/crashed records and
lack a complete training/simulation classification; the historical
790-run total has no recovered exact membership. These counts are
distinct provenance populations (§7.1). The registry distinguishes
missing declarations from explicit absence, exposes method-label
ambiguity and implements a 48-field comparison. Across its 28 current
stack entries, 342 of 378 pairs remain underspecified. This is useful
reporting infrastructure, without validation of predictive sufficiency,
execution honesty or causal risk (§7.2).

P7's controller audit supplies a design warning rather than an
allocation recommendation. In the counterfactual trace, 1,723 of 1,867
escalation events occur on observed all-correct groups. That share
largely follows from the reward level, and no controller executed in
that replay. Plugging observed success rates of zero or one into a
Bernoulli model forces zero predicted rescue; it does not establish
latent certainty. Historical N10 scripts do not implement one frozen
controller and are excluded as replications. The shrink-only rule's
gross-added-rollout efficiency is undefined when its denominator is
zero; avoided-rollout efficiency is a different endpoint. Neither the
heuristic nor the toy gradient correlation establishes a useful-update
or held-out benefit (§7.3; Appendix E).

Fresh frozen-policy sampling supplies a narrower empirical result.
Historical V2 observes 33/461 initially homogeneous groups becoming
reward-mixed, but 20 first changes are format-induced parser failures.
The 13 reviewed numeric contrasts comprise two wrong-to-correct
recoveries and eleven correct-to-wrong changes. C1 retains its separate
11/64 parser-defined endpoint; assistant-assisted reconciliation
supports three numerical recovery witnesses and a 22-case union of
specified reference defects. The first-review/second-review cross-tab
accounts for all 64 cases and thirteen category disagreements
(§§7.4--7.5; Appendix G). Neither study measures gradients, training
improvement or a controller effect, and neither is pooled with the
other.

The synthetic card-fraud study P8 remains outside the RL contribution
(Appendix F). Its tree and SFT language-model AUCs use different
held-out subsets and do not establish a matched performance ranking,
deployment calibration or production economics.

The E1--E14 campaign supplies a bounded harness audit rather than a
broad post-training result. The reported actor budget is 40 steps, 80
prompt draws and 320 completions; the historical scalar-loss threshold
was met on 35 steps, without enough telemetry to count zero gradients or
parameter updates. Exact training source, consumed prompts and
convergence evidence remain missing. The public base-model identity is
verified (Appendix B §B.14), but public model availability does not
authenticate historical execution. Private recovery supports selected
receipt fields and arithmetic without making those receipts
independently available in the public package.

Under these limitations, the headline campaign outcomes remain LAB-Bench
450/1,967, AgentDojo benign utility 88/97, VerilogEval 129/312 across
156 paired designs, SWE-bench Pro 2/731, and Omni-MATH native judged-row
accuracy 2,271/4,426 (51.31\%), with a separate all-disposition
diagnostic of 2,271/4,428 (51.29\%). The denominator-label correction is
explained in §8.2. These are end-to-end protocol scores. Their failure
analyses expose 593 malformed SWE-bench Pro patches and 150 VerilogEval
extraction failures; cap-related observational differences do not
isolate a causal budget effect. Six later replacement scopes were
attempted under exploratory self-issued protocol overrides, outside the
original prospective gates (Tables 8.3--8.4). They are neither
original-contract completions nor evidence that independent governance
succeeded. Appendix C preserves the scope and status of each lane.

In the same-engine paired pilot (Table 8.6), comparisons with an
applicable test or interval provide no conventional evidence of a
nonzero effect. The E4 paired scores remain descriptive, without a test
or interval; the pilot's small samples and differing inferential units
do not establish equivalence. E12 adds a direct self-evaluation bias
risk because its judge is the base-model family being compared; no
independent regrade is available. E4's reported full-rerun zero is
excluded from empirical conclusions because its secondary date conflicts
with the pending-state ledger and the primary job receipt is missing.
The retained bridge source documents omitted explicit stops and lossy
history conversion; source inspection and offline protocol tests do not
recover that historical run or attribute a benchmark failure to the
defect (§8.5).

The historical ledger is useful as a record of decisions and unresolved
conflicts, not a certificate of universal reproducibility. Its eleven
named checks have a bounded historical scope; missing public
dependencies prevent treating their recorded success as a fresh
checkout-level validation. The 27 September self-issued override and
unreconciled E4 history are governance limitations in the result, not
exceptions to hide.

\section{Rigour and Threats to
Validity}\label{rigour-and-threats-to-validity}

\textbf{What rigour was exercised.} Diagnostics are recomputable from
stored raw tensors where tensor-instrumented runners persisted them, and
selected structural claims were historically checked by eleven named
deterministic checks on 2026-09-19, recorded as passing, covering exact
divisions, receipt presence, sealed-artifact hash equality and
lost-source absence probes. Held-out \emph{n} is reported alongside
every gain, and failed runs are counted rather than hidden: failed
attempts remain part of the stated limitations.

Table 9.1 lists the threats discussed below, grouped as internal,
construct and external, with the mitigation applied to each. Most
mitigations narrow what is claimed rather than repair the evidence.

\textbf{Threats to validity and the mitigation applied to each.}

Every claim is scoped to the observed horizon; no asymptotic or
