\textbf{Statistical power: single-seed Tinker experiments.} Most Tinker
configurations ran once, because cost precluded repetition, and those
single-seed runs carry no variance estimates or significance tests. The
exceptions are stated where they occur. They are the three-seed-per-arm
managed head-to-head (Chapter 7 §7.1), the five-seed Qwen3-8B held-out
control (p = 0.26, Chapter 6 §6.5.4) and the Qwen3-8B scale extension.
Henderson et al.~(2018) \citep{henderson2018deep} motivate reporting
variability across random seeds; no universal seed-count threshold
guarantees power. A follow-up needs a prespecified effect size and power
rationale. The seed counts are: one seed for most Tinker runs and all P1
anchors; three for the P3 sweep, the P4 GSM8K-CoT and scale-extension
arms and the managed head-to-head; five for the same-stack PPO/GRPO
contrast, the P4 arithmetic arms and the Qwen3-8B pre/post control; and
eight for the variant audit (P11). The limitations document says the
ten-seed bar was met for the TRL baseline and the GSM8K evaluation and
then that the evaluation was cut to five seeds; the run records in
Appendix A show five seeds for both the TRL Modal campaign (Table A.3)
and the GSM8K pre/post evaluation. The 30B MoE scaling analysis uses
three seeds; the bootstrap confidence intervals assume independent
observations, so any positive correlation from shared initialisation or
data ordering would make the reported intervals too narrow, and the true
intervals wider; and secondary analyses carry uncorrected p-values. An
open runner can improve inspectability, but does not by itself certify
an executed configuration or remove leakage and measurement error;
inferential support is assessed comparison by comparison, while
single-seed Tinker figures remain descriptive.

\textbf{Horizons of 30--50 steps.} The Tinker runs are early-training
snapshots; long-horizon effects such as reward hacking, catastrophic
forgetting and late-stage policy collapse are unlikely to manifest, and
the source conclusion is direct: ``Asymptotic claims are unsupported''.
Short horizons are only one limitation. P1 lacks a common-budget,
replicated pre/post design, so it supplies no scaling null; P4's
observed compression under a 200-token cap does not identify
unconstrained length bias.

\textbf{Closed Tinker internals.} The Tinker SDK exposes a custom loss
path, a limited optimiser surface and standard LoRA hyperparameters, but
not the exact server-side GRPO loss formulation, the reward
normalisation or baseline-subtraction scheme, minibatch or
gradient-accumulation strategy, hardware configuration, or system-level
telemetry. This applies unevenly: the inspected custom-loss Tinker
reference loops define their loss in client code (REINFORCE with a
group-mean baseline, Table 4.2), while the actor's missing original
execution source limits verification of its historical loss path
(Appendix D), whereas the runners that use Tinker's built-in
\texttt{importance\_sampling} loss leave its server-side details
unrecorded. The consequence is best quoted: Tinker results measure ``the
\textbf{platform's} implementation of GRPO, not an abstract algorithmic
specification''. The illustrative case in the limitations document
compares a pooled last-10 training reward of 99.9\% for Tinker GRPO with
73.4\% for TRL GRPO on GSM8K. Those figures are training rewards, not
held-out accuracy, and they pool Tinker runs on 4B--235B models against
TRL on Qwen2.5-0.5B, so model size and implementation are confounded;
the source itself attributes part of the gap to the model difference.
The only same-model pair, Qwen3-8B, used the Base checkpoint on Tinker
and the Instruct checkpoint on TRL (Table A.2), so it does not remove
the confound either. The threat propagates into the campaign, whose
actor was trained on Tinker. The SDK is also a moving target: Phase 1
pinned \texttt{tinker==0.16.1}, while the September 2026 bridge required
\texttt{tinker==0.30.0} after the older SDK produced a
no-longer-supported error.

\textbf{Per-suite native denominators are not comparable.} Every E1--E14
result is expressed on its own suite's denominator, and those
denominators differ in size, in what they count and in what they
exclude. SWE-bench Pro is 2/731, whose denominator retains 14 generation
failures and 4 artifact losses alongside 713 native evaluations, 593 of
which ran on the unpatched base because the submitted patch was corrupt
for \texttt{git\ apply} (§8.3); APEX-Agents is 7/480; E9 MLE-bench is
40/75 competitions natively graded, stated as 53.33\% coverage with a
null suite score, because coverage and score are different quantities;
E1's replacement SWE-bench Multilingual is 1/190 on one contract and
4/110 on another, never summed to a 300-task figure; E6 WebArena is
90/812 with 108 tasks ungraded, so its denominator counts items that
were never graded; E13 BALROG is a mean of six per-environment means
rather than a rate over episodes; and E14's native judged-row accuracy
is 2,271/4,426 (51.31\%) and its separate all-disposition diagnostic is
2,271/4,428 (51.29\%), with the denominator erratum explained in §8.2. A
41.35\% on one denominator and a 0.274\% on another do not license a
comparison, an average or a ranking, and none is offered; the rule holds
within lanes too, and the ledger states it flatly: ``N/A means
unverified; no pooled results''.

\textbf{No cross-suite aggregate.} No mean, median or composite index
over suites appears anywhere in this report or in the campaign materials
it draws on: an aggregate over native denominators would be a number
with no referent, mixing suites that measure different capabilities at
different difficulties under different scoring rules. The cost is that
the campaign has no single headline; the three strictly complete scopes,
the terminal-complete Omni-MATH scope, the six exploratory
protocol-override replacement scopes attempted on 27 September and the
two original-contract scores are the most the record supports.

\textbf{The E10 benign-utility scope says nothing about adversarial
robustness.} The E10 lane's completed evidence is a benign-utility
evaluation of 97/97 completed tasks with utility 88/97 = 0.907 under
AgentDojo's benign scope; its original contract, private AgentHarm
tasks, is externally blocked and carries a closure record with a named
reopen condition. The completeness result therefore carries no
adversarial-robustness measurement, and this report makes no such claim
for the trained actor. The lane has a dual-use dimension, though the
limitations analysis finds the marginal uplift of the released artefacts
small, because the base model's capability is overwhelmingly
pre-existing: the pre-RL Qwen3-8B-Instruct checkpoint already scores
82.0\% on held-out GSM8K, and full GRPO adds only +1.3 points to 83.3\%,
not significant at p = 0.26. The 82.0\% is the same Instruct checkpoint
before RL, not a separate base model.

\textbf{The retained E11 result came through the original sampler.} The
129/312 full-run receipt attributes its samples to the managed Tinker
sampler rather than a locally served copy. Private execution and
repository access identities are omitted, so this public package cannot
replay the original route. A different serving stack would be a protocol
change with unmeasured same-weights, different-sampler divergence. The
separate four-task step-40 pilot has different source and
decontamination limitations and is not substituted for the full run.

\textbf{Known evidence loss in the finish-era recovery source.} The
record is explicit: the historical source-loss incident recorded in
\texttt{withheld\ historical\ source-loss\ chronology} removed the
execution sources of the finish-era recovery and build code --- the E1
wave-10 controller, the E9 restored dependencies and the E6 validator
--- while sealed requests, validation receipts and test logs survive
with matching hashes, and the replacement lanes' native runners survive
under \texttt{zvf-program/flagship/}. The lost-source policy was applied
rather than waived: a replacement constrained by the retained regression
fixtures (full historical behavioral equivalence is unverified) was
written into a tracked path under \texttt{zvf-program/e1\_wave10/},
where it verifies 10/10 targeted regressions offline
(\longtok{test\_\allowbreak{}process\_\allowbreak{}group.py} 4/4,
\longtok{test\_\allowbreak{}cleanup\_\allowbreak{}regressions.py} 6/6). Two consequences follow: a
sealed request without its execution source is not a rerun, so the E9
MLDevBench runtime recipe was never reconstructed (the 27 September run
built a fresh environment on Google Cloud instead); and the policy
response --- execution sources henceforth live under tracked paths ---
cannot restore what was already lost. A related item: the Jev (TypeSafe)
judgment harness lost reliability from roughly 13:10Z on 2026-09-19,
both blocker-classification batteries returning degenerate distributions
and a controlled yes-probe falling from about 1.0 to 0.3; the receipts
are retained as incident evidence and the batteries must be re-run once
the health check passes, so the ledger's first-uncleared-blocker column
stands on document evidence rather than judgment output. The retained
incident account does not identify a cause: no version-pinned
before/after API payloads, bridge diff or prompt comparison survives
here to distinguish an external model update from a local bridge/prompt
regression. The approximate 13:10Z onset is an incident-report
timestamp, not proof of either mechanism.

\textbf{Scope and confound inventory.} Several Phase-1 threats remain
live. Many early runs logged train-set rewards only, so time-series
comparisons spanning old and new runs must account for the
instrumentation change. Initial phases used LoRA at ranks 8--64
exclusively; historical baseline comparisons use default hyperparameters
from the Tinker cookbook and the TRL documentation; and no full
learning-rate × rank × batch × group-size grid was swept. Tinker, Modal,
Colab and the PES A100 node use different accelerators, memory
hierarchies and training stacks, so observed differences in reward
dynamics are not purely algorithmic, and the work was not
pre-registered. The historical model-scale roster is heterogeneous,
includes interrupted and single-seed traces, and supplies no reliable
extrapolation interval (Chapter 6 §6.2); benchmark coverage is narrow
and biased toward verifiable rewards such as exact match, unit tests and
schema validity, with no human preference data, so findings should not
be extended to reward-model-based RLHF; and the work was carried out at
one institution in Bengaluru, so benchmark choices and evaluation design
reflect that context.

The resource accounting is itself a threat. The report estimates roughly
60 A100 GPU-hours of in-kind PES University compute and projects
environmental use of about 776 kWh and about 296 kg CO₂-eq; the dominant
uncertainty is the Tinker GPU-hour count, inferred because the platform
exposes no hardware telemetry, giving a pessimistic Tinker contribution
of about 540 kg against an optimistic one of about 130 kg. The
mitigation offered is reproducibility by artefact.

\section{Conclusions}\label{conclusions}

This report set out to build a multi-framework training harness for
GRPO-style post-training of large language models and to study that
post-training under a single methodological rule: hold the stack fixed,
change one factor, report what happens. The harness dispatches one
configuration to several back-ends, but only Tinker and TRL produced
completed runs, on different base checkpoints, so it is not a completed
multi-framework benchmark. The four research questions of §1.4 have the
following answers.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{RQ1 (ZVF).} Yes, it is recomputable: the three stored Qwen3-8B
  tensors reproduce 0.130, 0.190 and 0.155 exactly. Its predictive
  validity remains unestablished: the historical outcome and collapse
  correlations mix synthetic and measured records, and the outcome is
  not a common held-out measure (§6.3; Table 1.1). Its magnitude is
  regime-dependent, from 0.16 to 0.84 across selected measured cohort
  means.
\item
  \textbf{RQ2 (identifiable algorithm and group-size contrasts).} Partly
  answered. The arithmetic estimator comparison is excluded because
  leakage, unequal exposure and broken item pairing prevent isolated
  attribution; a ceiling is an additional limitation, not its only
  defect. Capped length records do not identify unconstrained length
  bias, and inconsistent unchanged-base evaluations limit historical
  pre/post claims. The unsaturated v2 and five-arm variant comparisons
  retain the scoped limits below. The separately contracted same-stack
  rerun on the unsaturated Qwen2.5-1.5B GSM8K-CoT configuration supplies
  an interim comparison. Its first version (v1: 200-token cap, cold
  critic, 200 test items, five seeds) showed GRPO at G = 8 exceeding PPO
  by +5.0 percentage points (+0.050; 95\% CI {[}+1.5, +8.5{]} points),
  but its PPO arm finished below its starting accuracy (0.195 against
  0.200) and the sign-flip p after Holm adjustment was 0.19. The
  corrected v2 rerun (512-token cap, warm-started critic, full
  1,319-item test split, independent re-grade agreeing on every item)
  supersedes it: GRPO G8 − PPO is +0.76 percentage points (+0.0076; 95\%
  CI {[}−0.34, +1.85{]} points) over seven paired seeds, an interim TOST
  calculation at ±2 points (±0.02) (p = 0.016), not a final equivalence
  conclusion; G8 − G2 is +0.79 points (+0.0079; {[}+0.02, +1.56{]}) over
  ten seeds, not significant after Holm adjustment (p = 0.14); and G2 −
  PPO is +0.04 points (+0.0004), from a base accuracy of 0.654. Under
  the revised protocol the interim difference test does not detect a
  GRPO advantage over PPO. Critic training, generation cap, scoring and
  evaluation scope changed together, so the v1--v2 comparison does not
  isolate any one repair. The figures are provisional while three PPO
  seeds and the pre-specified exposure-matched PPO arm complete (§6.5.5,
  Table 6.2).
\item
  \textbf{RQ3 (manifest-level flip risk).} Yes, as a reporting check.
  The revised \texttt{stackdiff} flags both exhibits from their records
  alone --- the 17× pair at R5 on checkpoint, reward and template
  (coverage 0.58) and the ``DAPO'' pair at R5 on checkpoint, reward,
  template, dataset and evaluation metric (coverage 0.87) --- and
  reports missing information separately; 342 of the 378 pairs are
  underspecified because 16 entries are hand-entered (§7.2). Whether its
  flags predict outcome differences was not tested.
\item
  \textbf{RQ4 (governance-first evaluation).} Partly answered as an
  audit: the ledger separates scopes and records failures, but missing
  public receipts, the E4 chronology conflict and self-issued protocol
  overrides limit reproducibility and prospective governance. It does
  not yield a post-training delta: the actor had 40 reported training
  steps, and the paired Table 8.6 comparisons provide no conventional
  evidence of a nonzero effect where a test or interval is reported,
  while E4 remains descriptive without either.
\end{itemize}

The analytical contribution is a verified diagnostic calculation, a
reporting specification and a set of explicit evidence boundaries. ZVF
is recomputable but predictive validity is unestablished; the
homogeneous-group identity explains structure without validating an
allocation policy. The surviving short-horizon contrasts do not
establish a general algorithm ranking, group-size optimum or uncensored
length-bias result. Interrupted scaling fits, synthetic/imputed
validation panels and evaluation-contaminated estimator comparisons are
excluded from scientific support, rather than retained as nulls. The
reporting standard and registry remain implemented artifacts, whose
utility for predicting independent outcomes still needs testing. The
novelty claim is bounded by the reviewed literature, not proof that no
equivalent specification exists (§7.1).

The E1--E14 campaign contributes infrastructure and harness findings
with explicit governance limitations: the actor received 40 steps of
critic-free policy-gradient training (§8.1.1), so its scores describe a
checkpoint rather than a training method. Its historical ledger reports
results on three strictly complete scopes (two replacements and one
original contract), with Omni-MATH terminal-complete; full-coverage
original-contract scores on two (E1 and E11); and explicit lane states
and remaining gates (Table C.3). Original-contract and replacement-scope
numbers are never pooled, no cross-suite aggregate is computed, and no
improvement over the base model is claimed for this campaign: the paired
Table 8.6 comparisons provide no conventional evidence of a nonzero
effect where a test or interval is reported, while E4 remains
descriptive without either. The controlled Chapter 6 gains are separate,
stack-specific findings. VerilogEval's 129/312 = 41.35\% pass@1 at full
coverage is an end-to-end harness score. Of its 183 failures, 150 are
extraction failures, plausibly related to the 1,024-token cap with
thinking enabled; missing per-sample finish reasons prevent a definitive
causal attribution. On a 50-item subset the same adapter scores 33/50
with thinking off at 4,096 tokens against 23/50 in this run, but engine
and temperature also changed. That comparison does not isolate the cap's
effect or replace the full-run score. Operational source bindings remain
outside this public derivative.

The report's overall conclusion is about method rather than algorithms.
With these instruments, some measurements are directly inspectable while
several algorithmic questions remain unidentified or underpowered; the
stack is a material part of the result, so a label without its stack is
not a reproducible experimental specification; and the most durable
artefacts produced here are the ones that make other people's results
checkable --- the ZVF/PCD diagnostic layer, the eight-item minimum
reportable stack (seven manifest fields and one evaluation item), the
registry and its CLI, and a campaign ledger with named receipts and
gates, with recovered primary receipts preserved separately from the
frozen checkout and remaining raw-evidence and provenance limits stated
explicitly (Appendix B §B.11).

\subsection{C1 adds numerical witnesses and exposes reference
limits}\label{c1-adds-numerical-witnesses-and-exposes-reference-limits}

The C1 extension makes the evidence boundary more precise. Its frozen
score is 11/64 on a deterministic first-64 subset, with a question-level
Wilson 95\% working-model interval of 9.88-28.21\%. Assistant-assisted
reconciliation corroborates three numerical recovery witnesses on that
unchanged denominator. Three further events retain convention-dependent
interpretations, four are materially ambiguous and one matches an
incorrect checkpoint reference after eight correct initial answers. The
frozen score is preserved, not retrospectively repaired. Selection on
parser zeros can enrich the reference faults and representation
mismatches that prevent a direct mathematical-recovery interpretation
(Appendix G, sources S1-S5).

These three witnesses show that eight observed wrong numerical answers
do not force future numerical answers to remain wrong for this frozen
checkpoint, task and decoder. They do not validate the reasoning in a
successful response: Santana's earliest successful number was reached by
an incorrect argument. They do not measure a gradient, training update,
held-out capability or controller benefit. Reference auditing and
unit-aware evaluation therefore belong in the measurement design before
a future intervention is interpreted. The full 64-case audit retains
thirteen category disagreements and provides no whole-benchmark
error-prevalence estimate. Historical V2 remains separate and unpooled.

The separately frozen parser-v3 candidate passes only its narrow
four-change extraction check in 2,048 prospectively selected future
outputs. Preservation of 2,025 old-valid objects and agreement on four
new scalar extractions do not establish task correctness or broad parser
precision. The candidate remains undeployed; the C1 endpoint stays on
parser v2 (Appendix G, sources S8-S9).

\section{Future Work}\label{future-work}

The work leaves three kinds of open question: evaluation lanes whose
measurement is incomplete, analytical claims that the present evidence
could not settle, and methodological tools that have been built but not
yet tested at scale.

\textbf{Closing the remaining evaluation gaps.} The six replacement
lanes that were launch-pending or quota-blocked on 21 September were
attempted under exploratory protocol overrides on 27 September 2026 with
the reported lane outcomes (Chapter 8 §8.4). Two measurement gaps remain
in them. The 108 WebArena tasks that need an LLM judge are ungraded
because the judge was unavailable, and the native judge model has since
been retired, so a regrade would record a judge substitution. SWE-bench
Multilingual has been attempted on all 300 tasks, but under two serving
contracts, so a single 300-task figure would need the first 110 tasks
rerun under the 27 September contract (32,768-token context and the
re-implemented source collector). Six original-contract scopes are
recorded \texttt{CLOSED\_EXTERNAL} with written closure records: E3
(SDAB private bundle), E7 (BinaryAudit private payload), E8-original
(LifeSciBench package), E10-original (AgentHarm private tasks), E12
(AppBench deployment) and E14-original (FrontierMath hosted evaluation).
A seventh original contract, E13's OpenReward held-out games, is also
blocked on external access but has no closure record, because the lane
continues through its BALROG replacement scope (Table C.3). A new
evaluation would require the unavailable benchmark scope. A full
BankerToolBench score for E4 would be a new-runtime experiment; the
recovered pending-state ledger recorded a grader-only lower bound of
approximately an undisclosed operational estimate before launch. The
later 100-trial zero-mean account in
\longtok{outputs/e1\_\allowbreak{}e14\_\allowbreak{}small\_\allowbreak{}scale\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-26/E4/result.json}
(\longtok{prior\_\allowbreak{}base\_\allowbreak{}run}) is unverified and quarantined pending a
primary execution receipt, exact timestamp and matching trajectories
(§8.5).

\textbf{Turning the campaign into a post-training measurement.} A future
study needs a source-bound training implementation, exact prompt draws,
checkpoint hashes, policy-gradient and parameter-update norms, reward/KL
trajectories, and a prespecified adequacy or stopping criterion before
expensive suite evaluation. The paired same-engine pilot (§8.7) provides
a starting design, not validation of the present actor. Full native
scopes, justified sample sizes and multiple independent training seeds
would be needed to estimate post-training deltas. E12 requires a blinded
independent judge or independently adjudicated rubric; E10 requires
preserved task-level rubric components and scorer/judge versions. E1's
591 newly parseable patches require semantic test execution under an
explicitly post-hoc repair contract before any repaired pass@1 can be
claimed. None of these new experiments or regrades was performed for
this source-only revision.

\textbf{Settling the analytical questions.} Five Phase-1 directions
remain open, each answering a threat identified above:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  adequately powered evaluation, moving from the Phase-1 probes'
  held-out \emph{n} of 8--20 (the multi-seed controlled comparisons
  already use 200--500 items, Chapter 1 §1.5) to the full GSM8K test
  split or a large stratified subset at five or more seeds;
\item
  a token-budget-optimal curriculum with staleness bounds, tested
  multi-seed;
\item
  testing whether the mid-to-late band in the per-layer gradient profile
  supports a FLOP-saving freeze schedule, even though step-1
  predictability did not survive scaling;
\item
  hardening the Phase-1 method-trace classifier on grouped run-level and
  temporal splits rather than row-wise cross-validation;
\item
  consolidating the diagnostic findings and the reporting standard for
  publication.
\end{itemize}

Three more follow from the Chapter 6 results. The first is a
length-confounded regime, with the completion cap lifted and longer
horizons, in which a Dr.~GRPO length advantage would be expected to
appear if it exists. The second is the head-to-head protocol between ZVF
and the pairwise-contrast density, to test whether PCD meets its target
association. The third completes the declared v2 seeds and
exposure-matched arm before interpreting final estimator or group-size
differences. Any subsequent critic-quality ablation must change one
factor on a bound execution stack, with unchanged-base repeat checks and
enough independent seeds to assess the prespecified margin.

\textbf{Testing the methodological tools.} Three extensions come from
the Semester-4 work:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  a pre-registered controller bakeoff against static G = 16 and naive
  boundary heuristics, the test P7 declines to claim in advance of;
\item
  a cross-framework divergence study that runs an identical task,
  reward, decoding and seed configuration across TRL, veRL, OpenRLHF and
  Tinker, turning P5's attribution argument from an audit of existing
  runs into a designed experiment;
\item
  dogfooding the registry schema on the campaign's own runs, so that
  every future benchmark result ships with a verifiable provenance
  record.
\end{itemize}

Two commitments carry forward, because the threats above are structural
rather than incidental. The first is the never-pool rule:
original-contract and replacement-scope numbers stay separate, and
per-suite native denominators are never averaged. The second is that
failures are reported with their mechanism, as the E11 sampler path, the
E4 chronology conflict, the lost execution sources and the seed-count
discrepancy are here.

\chapter{Historical Run Registry and Coverage
Reconciliation}\label{historical-run-registry-and-coverage-reconciliation}

This appendix records the historical run inventories used by the report
and the later reconciliation of those inventories. It is not an
exhaustive current account history. Provider logging identities,
independent scientific executions, checkpoint revisions, and
claim-to-run links are different units and must not be added as though
they were interchangeable. Run identifier, library and platform, base
model, algorithm, group size, dataset, seed, steps, and terminal status
are retained where a primary source records them; unknown fields remain
unknown. A claim without supporting primary evidence must be qualified
or withdrawn rather than inferred from an inventory count.

Historical inventories differ in scope and counting units.
Provider-ledger entries, opening-study records, claim links and
independent scientific trials are not interchangeable. This public
derivative omits all account-wide totals, project breakdowns and exact
provider identities. Only scientific study configurations, denominators,
outcomes and limitations are retained; it makes no complete-coverage
claim.

scope}\label{historical-boundary-and-source-scope}

\texttt{capstone-final-2026-04-25}, commit \texttt{21a99ef7} dated 23

history, not provider-account ownership or activity counts. The study
tables below retain scientific configurations, seeds, steps, outcomes
and evidence tiers. Operational identifiers are withheld; locally named
scientific study units remain for interpretation.

\section{Semester-3 shared benchmark
runs}\label{semester-3-shared-benchmark-runs}

The Semester-3 contribution was the multi-framework benchmark
infrastructure: a common harness, a fixed evaluation configuration, and
integrations for several independent RL post-training libraries. These
runs are the shared baseline measurements, and they are the ones for
which the registry is \emph{most} complete, because each was produced
under a declared configuration.

\textbf{Cross-framework comparison at the fixed benchmark
configuration.} Configuration for all four rows: model
\texttt{Qwen/Qwen3-8B} (Tinker row uses \texttt{Qwen/Qwen3-8B-Base}),
algorithm GRPO, group size G = 8, learning rate 1e-5, dataset
GSM8K{[}:500{]}, 30 steps, seed 42. Metric is \texttt{last10\_avg}, the
mean of the final ten reported training rewards. Source:
\longtok{platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/framework\_comparison.json},
generated by \longtok{aggregate\_\allowbreak{}framework\_\allowbreak{}comparison.py}.

The two \texttt{dryrun} rows are a documented threat to validity, not a
result: the generator falls back to a deterministic seeded trace from
\texttt{verl.VERLTrainer} / \texttt{OpenRLHFTrainer} when the framework
is absent from the sandbox, and the JSON carries the note ``Seeded
deterministic fallback \ldots{} (verl not installed in sandbox)''. Any
comparison reading them as measurements of verl or OpenRLHF is invalid.
The Tinker row also initialises from the \emph{base} checkpoint while
the open-framework rows use the instruction-tuned one, so even the two
real rows are not a clean framework contrast.

arithmetic/math. Only the five TRL rows record a model
(\texttt{Qwen/Qwen2.5-0.5B}), a step count (125 training steps) and a
GPU (NVIDIA L4). The fifteen PPO rows record final accuracy, wall time
and a learning curve indexed by environment steps (to about 100,000),
but no model, optimizer-step count or GPU; those fields are shown as
``not recorded''. Group size is not recorded in the artefact and is
therefore shown as ``---''.

\section{Semester-4 P1--P8 study runs}\label{semester-4-p1p8-study-runs}

The P1--P8 series is the individual Semester-4 contribution, and its
runs are indexed by \emph{claim} rather than by project. The audit
ledger at \longtok{platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/claim\_\allowbreak{}to\_\allowbreak{}run/}
maps each published claim to its supporting run identifiers, provider
references retained in the non-public source records, whether the model
named in the paper matches the model actually trained, the seed and step
counts, the held-out metric, and an evidence tier. The retained claim
map has eighteen scientific claim rows. Private link and
provider-inventory counts are omitted; the table retains the scientific
evidence tiers and study outcomes.

\textbf{P1--P8 claim-to-run ledger.} Tier vocabulary: A = ≥5 seeds and
≥100 steps; B = 3--4 seeds and 50--99 steps; C = completed/descriptive;
R = deterministic resource or schema claim; X = unlinked, contradicted,
or provenance-conflicted.

Three facts here are load-bearing. First, \textbf{no empirical headline
row in the P1--P8 ledger reaches Tier A or B} under the
statistical-rigor thresholds; the rectified P1--P8 tally is C = 12, R =
2, X = 4. P11 adds an eight-seed matched block but does not meet the
stated Tier A step threshold, as clarified below. Second, the four Tier
X rows are X for specific, checkable reasons: \textbf{P3-C2} rests on G
= 32 \emph{sampling} cells with zero optimizer steps, so no matched G =
4/G = 32 training replication exists; \textbf{P4-C1}'s abstract names
Qwen3-8B while every mapped run is \texttt{Qwen/Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct};
\textbf{P5-C1} compares \texttt{Qwen/Qwen3-8B-Base} against
\texttt{Qwen/Qwen3-8B}, so the base checkpoint changes together with the
backend and the pair must not be cited as a backend-only causal effect;
and \textbf{P8-C3} is a proposed architecture with no execution, hence
zero linked runs. Third, some cross-provider model-identity conflicts
remain unresolved. Their private identity reconciliation is outside this
public package; the affected scientific model-attribution claims remain
contested.

Two column caveats travel with the table. The dataset cell for P7-C1 is
\texttt{—} because the ledger's only dataset-adjacent field for those
368 runs is a prompt/tokenizer family (\texttt{llama3},
\texttt{qwen3\_instruct}, \longtok{gpt\_\allowbreak{}oss\_\allowbreak{}low\_\allowbreak{}reasoning},
\longtok{nemotron3\_\allowbreak{}disable\_\allowbreak{}thinking}), not a dataset name. And the P3
sweep here is \textbf{not} the Semester-3 sweep of Table A.4: it is a
different project (\texttt{study\ logging\ group}), a different model
(Qwen3.5-4B), and it reports deltas of 0.0000 rather than held-out
accuracies near 0.99. The two share a group-size grid and nothing else.
Chapters 4 and 6 cite the Table A.4 sweep (Qwen2.5-0.5B) whenever they
give the 0.838 → 0.631 ZVF decline or the 0.98--0.99 held-out figures.
They do not cite P3-C1.

The extension ledger added in August 2026 carries five rows besides P11:
P10-C1 is Tier R (an algebraic identity, not an empirical result),
P10-C2 and P12-C2 are Tier X (placeholder figures and unlinked
prospective claims, zero runs), and P12-C1 is Tier R as a lemma and Tier
X as an empirical headline.

\textbf{P11} is an eight-seed, matched-stack block of 40 arm--seed units
at
\longtok{local:zvf-program/audit/results/full/\{grpo,dapo,gspo,drgrpo,aero\}-seed-\{11,23,37,53,71,89,107,131\}.json},
five arms × eight seeds on one stack (Qwen3-8B, GSM8K, 30 steps, Colab
A100, held-out n = 500). Its headline contrast, DAPO versus GRPO, is Δ =
+0.001, percentile-bootstrap CI {[}−0.0045, +0.00675{]} (paired-t 95\%
CI {[}−0.0063, +0.0083{]}, n = 8 seeds; the bootstrap undercovers at
this n), MDE₈₀ = 0.01012, all raw p \textgreater{} 0.21, with
Benjamini--Hochberg rejecting nothing. The mechanism is visible in the
diagnostic: DAPO's mean ZVF is 0.000 against GRPO's 0.693, bought with
3.61× the rollouts (1,734 versus 480) and 1.44× the wall clock. This
eight-paired-seed contrast has no multiplicity-adjusted rejection and
does not establish equivalence. Every retained P11 final manifest
specifies \texttt{max\_steps=30}; the 40 units are 30-step runs.
Accordingly this block does \textbf{not} satisfy the stated Tier A
requirement of at least 100 steps per run and remains Tier C under the
vocabulary above. Pooling steps across arms or seeds does not satisfy
that per-run requirement. This corrects the evidence tier, not the
recorded rewards, seed counts, interval, or null result.

\subsection{Opening and null-result
runs}\label{opening-and-null-result-runs}

Chapter 6 reports four null families --- a mixed-variance curriculum, a
matched token budget, hard-prompt filtering, and step-1 layer selection
(§6.4.3) --- and each rests on source-specific scientific result records
rather than an account-wide inventory. The study The rows are counted
from the per-run \texttt{name} (or mode) fields of each artefact's JSON
--- \texttt{results.json} for the curriculum, token-budget, hard-prompt
and loss-formulation blocks, \texttt{result.json} and its siblings for
P1 --- not from an inventory API, which is why this cohort is reported
as its own total. Three neighbouring blocks add nothing to it: the P3
group-size artefacts are already indexed ---
\texttt{groupsize\_zvf/results.json} reproduces the per-group deltas of
Table A.5's P3-C1 row exactly, and the second sweep file is discussed
below --- and the P2, P7 and P8 ``openings''
(\longtok{p2\_openings/collapse\_\allowbreak{}baseline\_\allowbreak{}analysis.json},
\texttt{p7\_controller/sim.json}, \texttt{p8\_openings/*.json}) are
zero-compute re-analyses that launch no runs.

\textbf{Semester-4 opening and null-result runs (46 runs).} Model is
\texttt{Qwen/Qwen3.5-4B} and dataset GSM8K unless a row says otherwise.
All rows are Tinker-managed except the P1 layer-freeze block, which ran
on Colab for per-layer gradient access. ``Curriculum'' means training
only on non-collapsed (mixed-variance) groups. Group sizes marked
\emph{default} are the launch defaults of the scripts in
\longtok{platform\_hybrid/experiments/openings/}; only the campaign
artefact records a group size per run. Δ is the change in held-out
accuracy.

Three provenance notes belong with this table, because two of its blocks
carry superseded siblings and one carries a refuted claim. First,
\texttt{campaign/FINDINGS.md} describes a nine-run, three-seed version
of the campaign on a twelve-prompt held-out set with arm means of +0.028
and −0.028; \texttt{campaign/results.json} holds fifteen runs over six
seeds on a thirty-prompt held-out set (0.8667 = 26/30), and the table
follows the per-run file. The FINDINGS warning that
\longtok{zero\_\allowbreak{}loss\_\allowbreak{}frac} was invalid (an empty-metrics-dict bug,
``since fixed'') belongs to the nine-run batch, not to that file.
Second, the P1 layer-freeze block has the same shape: the two-seed 3B
pass (overlap 0.1111 ± 0.0000) is superseded by the four-seed pass
(0.0833 ± 0.0481), and the larger-model overlap is below the simple
independent 0.25 reference, so the toy-scale overlap of 1.0 must not be
read as a transferable layer-selection signal. Third, the two
single-seed training blocks flagged SUSPECT by independent verification
(kimi, 2026-07-06) are superseded by the multi-seed blocks above: the
curriculum opening's +0.05 (campaign curriculum and baseline arms
separate by 0.006 over six seeds, at 3.3--7.2× oversample cost), and a
single-seed G = 4 gain of +0.125 in
\longtok{p3\_groupsize/sweep\_results.json} --- the campaign's G4 seed-0
baseline gains 0.0000, not 0.125, and that sweep file retains only two
of its twelve entries, so the W\&B group is its record.

\subsection{Same-stack rerun on the unsaturated
configuration}\label{m1b-run-registry}

The first version (v1, superseded) of the rerun of §6.5.5 was executed
on Modal on 2 October 2026 by
\longtok{platform\_hybrid/experiments/modal/modal\_\allowbreak{}samestack\_\allowbreak{}gsm8k\_\allowbreak{}cot.py}:
15 runs, one A10G each, about 14 minutes of training per run. Per-run
results, including per-item pre- and post-training correctness and step
logs, are in
\longtok{platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/samestack\_\allowbreak{}gsm8k\_\allowbreak{}cot\_\allowbreak{}full.json};
adapters are on the a retained result volume Modal volume under the
study adapter directory. No W\&B run was logged.

\section{E1--E14 evaluation campaign
lanes}\label{e1e14-evaluation-campaign-lanes}

The fourteen evaluation lanes are not training runs; they are
evaluations of a single frozen actor, and they carry the registry's
strictest receipt discipline. The actor is base
\texttt{Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B} (commit \texttt{995ad96e…}) with adapter
\texttt{campaign-adapter} (commit \texttt{withheld-adapter-revision…}),
bfloat16, trained on Tinker, run seed 809 taken from the adapter name.
The adapter was trained for 40 steps at G = 4 and learning rate 1 × 10⁻⁵
by REINFORCE with a group-mean baseline (no KL, no clipping), as
recorded in the local W\&B run directory for run {[}identifier
withheld{]} and the run receipt
\texttt{withheld\ actor-training\ receipt} (Chapter 8 §8.1.1). Those are
properties of the single actor rather than of any lane, and every lane
carries the same run seed (809), so algorithm, group size, seed and step
columns are omitted from Table A.8. Terminal states are from the ledger
of record, \texttt{withheld\ historical\ campaign\ chronology}; all
scores are from \longtok{outputs/E1\_\allowbreak{}E14\_\allowbreak{}FINAL\_\allowbreak{}RESULTS\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-19.md},
except the six rows marked ``finish 2026-09-27'', which are from
\longtok{outputs/finish\_\allowbreak{}pending\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-27/\textless{}lane\textgreater{}/result.json}.

Two source documents disagree on two lanes, and both observations are
retained rather than resolved into one, because the disagreement is
itself evidence: the historical snapshot records E7 as ``1/28 errored,
no grade'' against the terminal-state table's ``1/46 attempted'' ---
both real observations at different points in the lane's life. Likewise
E11's canonical figure is 129/312 = 41.35\%; a 129/311 sensitivity
figure survives in non-canonical receipts and is not used as the
headline.

\section{Completeness limitations}\label{completeness-limitations}

The historical registry is an index of its retained snapshot and is
incomplete as a scientific dataset. Many records lack model, dataset,
algorithm, group size, steps or a usable outcome. Missing fields remain
unknown; names and provider state are not used to infer them. The public
version does not include the account-wide field-coverage table or
private per-identity export.

\textbf{Scientific metadata limitations.}

  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash\hspace{0pt}}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash\hspace{0pt}}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5000}}@{}}
\caption{Metadata limitations in the historical retained registry;
private account-level field counts are omitted.}\tabularnewline
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Required scientific field
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Interpretation of missing data
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endfirsthead
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Required scientific field
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright

\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Model and dataset & Do not infer identity from a display name or
tokenizer family \\
Algorithm and group size & An unrecorded configuration remains
unknown \\
Training steps & Provider state does not establish completed
optimization \\
Outcome & A logged record without a metric is not a scored scientific
result \\
\end{longtable}

Four further limits apply.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Aggregate presentation.} This appendix names 105 runs
  individually: the 4 cross-framework runs of Table A.2, the 20 Modal
  campaign runs of A.3, the 12 sweep runs (in 4 rows) of A.4, the 46
  opening runs (in 16 rows) of A.6, and the 23 E-lane rows of A.8 ---
  plus the 18 claim rows of Table A.5, which index claims rather than
  runs. Other historical inventory entries are not published in this
  derivative.
\item
  \textbf{Non-runs inside the count.} Three entries are not training
  results: \texttt{verl-dryrun} and \texttt{openrlhf-dryrun} are seeded
  deterministic fallbacks, and the E4 reported base-model rerun is an
  unverified secondary account; to these add §A.3.1's P1 gradient probe,
  which takes no optimizer steps at all. They are included as historical
  record rows, not as evidence of completed training or of the excluded
  E4 execution.
\item
  \textbf{Identity conflicts.} Historical source-identity disagreements
  remain a limitation; private provider reconciliation and its counts
  are withheld.
\item
  \textbf{Snapshot scope.} Historical source inventories differ in
  scope. They are neither current live accounts nor counts of
  independent experiments, and this public package does not provide a
  complete inventory replay.
\end{enumerate}

Named artifacts may preserve an arithmetic result while missing source
identity limits attribution to an algorithm or checkpoint. Chapters 6--8
state each claim's verification level; selected audits are not universal
verification. The registry records reported provenance and identity,
shows that the run population is far larger than the reported claims,
and shows that those claims rest on a small, explicitly tiered subset
with the P1--P8 empirical rows at Tier C or X and P11 at Tier C under
the stated per-run step threshold. The four Tier X P1--P8 rows must not
be cited as supporting evidence under their conflicted labels. Two are
nevertheless cited elsewhere in a restricted form, and the restriction
is part of the citation: P4-C1 is cited only under the model its runs
actually record (\texttt{Qwen/Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct} in every row of
\longtok{results/drgrpo\_\allowbreak{}gsm8k\_\allowbreak{}cot\_\allowbreak{}full.json}), never under the
Qwen3-8B label of the P4 source abstract; P5-C1 (0.856 versus 0.050) is
cited only as an under-specification exhibit whose checkpoint and
back-end changed together, never as a back-end effect. P3-C2 and P8-C3
are not cited as evidence anywhere.

The run totals that appear in different chapters count different
corpora. Table A.10 lists the scientific study corpora retained
publicly.

\textbf{Scientific study corpora.} Each total is a different corpus, not
a different count of the same one.

\chapter{Reproducibility and
Provenance}\label{reproducibility-and-provenance}

\section{What this appendix
establishes}\label{what-this-appendix-establishes}

This appendix records three things that should not be confused with one
another. The first is what the source repository prescribes for a rerun:
a pinned container, a pinned dependency set, a headline training
command, and acceptance tolerances. These instructions do not guarantee
that this public document package contains every dependency, checkpoint
or source payload. The second is what the repository verifies about
itself without re-running any model: an audit suite driven by a single
entry point, and a historical deterministic ledger check with a bounded
dependency set, some of which is absent from the public checkout. The
third is provenance: which parts of this work were produced by the
Semester 3 group, which by the individual contribution reported here,
and what was lost along the way.

\section{The pinned runtime}\label{the-pinned-runtime}

The container definition is \texttt{Dockerfile}, built from
\longtok{nvidia/cuda:12.4.1-cudnn-devel-ubuntu22.04}. It installs Python
3.12 through the deadsnakes archive rather than the distribution
default, with the in-file comment that Python 3.12 ``matches the frozen
runtime validated 106/106''. It sets \texttt{PYTHONHASHSEED=42} and a
fixed \texttt{LANG}/\texttt{LC\_ALL} of \texttt{C.UTF-8}; stamps the
build commit into an OCI \longtok{org.opencontainers.image.revision}
label and a \texttt{.build\_info} file; fixes \texttt{SEED=42},
\texttt{WANDB\_MODE=offline}, \longtok{HF\_\allowbreak{}HUB\_\allowbreak{}DISABLE\_\allowbreak{}TELEMETRY=1} and
\longtok{OMP\_\allowbreak{}NUM\_\allowbreak{}THREADS=8}; self-checks by importing the core stack at
build time; and runs under \texttt{tini}.

The authoritative pin set is \texttt{requirements.txt}, headed as
``pinned to the frozen E1 runtime lock \ldots{} validated 106/106 on
this stack''. Its exact pins include \texttt{torch==2.7.1},
\texttt{numpy==2.2.6}, \texttt{transformers==5.5.4},
\texttt{trl==1.2.0}, \texttt{datasets==4.8.4},
\texttt{accelerate==1.13.0}, \texttt{peft==0.19.1},
\texttt{huggingface-hub==1.11.0} and \texttt{wandb==0.21.0}, with
bounded ranges for scipy, safetensors, tokenizers, rliable, math-verify,
latex2sympy2-extended and pytest, alongside the framework clients
\texttt{tinker}, \texttt{tinker-cookbook} and \texttt{atroposlib}.

The 106/106 figure is the recorded outcome of the frozen-runtime
validation, which \texttt{execution-notes.md} states as passing the
complete pinned pilot/preregistration suite on Python 3.12.13 with the
pinned versions listed above, and the focused amendment, protocol,
launcher and verifier suite passing 55/55. The same record binds five
artefacts by SHA-256 --- protocol document, screening manifest, launch
manifest, launch fingerprint and source binding --- and states that a
mismatch in package, Python, A100, FLOP, token or gradient conditions
fails closed rather than proceeding.

\section{The reviewer's entry point}\label{the-reviewers-entry-point}

\texttt{REPRODUCE.md} is the reviewer-facing document. Its fastest path
is the structural smoke test at
\longtok{platform\_modal/scripts/smoke\_test.sh}, which runs in about two
minutes without a Tinker key and about eight when a live key is supplied
so that the wire protocol is exercised; the keyless path needs no GPU
and no account, and is offline only once the GSM8K dataset is cached,
because one step loads it.

The headline result is GRPO on Qwen3-8B over GSM8K for 30 steps at LoRA
rank 32, group size 8 and seed 42, recorded as a peak per-step training
accuracy (the binary reward) of 62.5 \% with a last-10 average of 34.4
\%; these summarise the training curve of the seed-42 run, not a
held-out evaluation, and Table 1.1 lists them beside the other Qwen3-8B
GSM8K figures they are sometimes confused with. Reviewer-side reruns are
accepted within ±5 percentage points on the last-10 figure and ±10
points on peak, justified in the same document by the residual
non-determinism discussed in §B.10. Per-seed reference values and W\&B
run identifiers are tabulated for seeds 42, 123, 456 and 789, with seed
1024 marked as a reviewer-only rerun; the canonical multi-seed set is
\texttt{\{42,\ 123,\ 456,\ 789,\ 1024,\ 2048,\ 4096,\ 8192,\ 16384,\ 32768\}}.
The group-size ablation is expected to reproduce a peak of 50.0 \% at
G=2, 75.0 \% at G=4, 100 \% at G=8 and 71.9 \% at G=16, with last-10
values of 37.5 \%, 52.1 \%, 84.4 \% and 38.0 \%. Five-seed aggregation
runs through \texttt{utils/stats.py}, which now accepts
\texttt{-\/-bootstrap-samples} (default 10,000) and prints the mean,
standard error and a percentile-bootstrap 95\% interval; its
\texttt{-\/-format} and \texttt{-\/-rliable} flags are accepted but not
yet acted on, and it reads a
\longtok{results/\textless{}experiment\textgreater{}/seed\_\textless{}N\textgreater{}/*.jsonl}
layout that the trainer does not write, so a reviewer must write one
metrics file per seed first, as \texttt{REPRODUCE.md} §3.4 states.
\texttt{utils/verify\_results.py} checks a rerun against
\longtok{platform\_hybrid/paper/expected\_results.json} at those
tolerances. It parses the per-seed \texttt{avg\_last10} /
\texttt{peak\_reward} block that \texttt{grpo\_cli} actually prints, and
it exits non-zero when a value is outside tolerance or missing, when no
result file matches an expected experiment, or (exit 2) when the
expectations file or results directory is missing. Before the 3 October
fix it parsed a line format the trainer never prints and exited 0 when
nothing matched, so a failed rerun would have been reported as a pass;
\longtok{tests/test\_\allowbreak{}verify\_\allowbreak{}results.py} now covers both formats and the
failure cases. One limitation remains: every seed is checked against the
single seed-42 headline (0.344 / 0.625), so a seed whose recorded values
differ by more than the tolerance, such as seed 456 with a 100\% peak,
is reported as outside tolerance.

The cost is stated plainly: about five GPU-hours on a single A100-80GB
for the headline run, against roughly 446 GPU-hours for a full paper
reproduction. Dataset provenance is tabulated with pinned revisions ---
GSM8K at \texttt{740312a} with 7,473 training and 1,319 test rows, plus
MATH-500, HumanEval, ArenaHard Auto, NoRobots and OpenThoughts3 each at
a named snapshot --- with exact shas in \texttt{ARTIFACT.md} §3.4.

\section{Where the documentation has drifted from the
code}\label{where-the-documentation-has-drifted-from-the-code}

Four discrepancies existed between the repository's prose and its live
tree; \texttt{REPRODUCE.md} was corrected on 3 October 2026 and
\texttt{ARTIFACT.md} was not. The training driver has moved to
\longtok{platform\_tinker/grpo\_\allowbreak{}gsm8k\_\allowbreak{}base.py}. \texttt{REPRODUCE.md}
now invokes it at that path, while \texttt{ARTIFACT.md} still invokes
\longtok{grpo\_\allowbreak{}gsm8k\_\allowbreak{}base.py} at the repository root; the flags are
unchanged, because the CLI in
\longtok{platform\_tinker/tinkerrl/grpo\_cli.py} carries a compatibility
entry point, \texttt{legacy\_main}, that translates the documented flag
spellings onto the current ones. The same correction pointed
\texttt{REPRODUCE.md}'s Modal, figure and Atropos commands at their live
paths (\longtok{platform\_modal/scripts/modal\_\allowbreak{}run\_\allowbreak{}experiments.py},
\longtok{platform\_modal/scripts/make\_\allowbreak{}paper\_\allowbreak{}figures.py},
\texttt{platform\_tinker/atropos}), removed an Atropos \texttt{-\/-seed}
flag the launcher does not accept, replaced the expected-output blocks
with the format the trainer prints, and added a verify step to the quick
start. Every documented command file now exists and the Python entry
points answer \texttt{-\/-help}; the Atropos scripts need
\texttt{tenacity}, \texttt{latex2sympy2\_extended} and
\texttt{atroposlib}, which \texttt{requirements.txt} installs in the
Docker image but the local development environment lacks. The analysis
scripts \longtok{platform\_modal/scripts/p5p8/p5\_\allowbreak{}headline\_\allowbreak{}cis\_\allowbreak{}full.py}
and \longtok{platform\_modal/scripts/length\_\allowbreak{}bias\_\allowbreak{}iter24.py} previously
resolved their repository root and results directory incorrectly and
created directories on import; both now resolve paths from the
repository root, and their regenerated outputs are the source of the
corrected §6.5.3 and §7.1 values. \texttt{ARTIFACT.md} names the smoke
test at \texttt{scripts/smoke\_test.sh} rather than at
\longtok{platform\_modal/scripts/smoke\_test.sh}, though
\texttt{REPRODUCE.md} gives the correct path. The pins quoted in prose
are stale: \texttt{REPRODUCE.md} §9 states that
\texttt{requirements.txt} pins
\longtok{transformers\textgreater{}=4.56.2,\textless{}4.60.0} and a
\texttt{datasets\textless{}3.2} bound, whereas the live file pins
\texttt{transformers==5.5.4} and \texttt{datasets==4.8.4}, and
\texttt{ARTIFACT.md} §3.3 quotes a third, older set; similarly,
\texttt{ARTIFACT.md} describes the environment as Python 3.10 from the
Ubuntu 22.04 archive, whereas the \texttt{Dockerfile} installs Python
3.12 via deadsnakes and the frozen-runtime record validates on Python
3.12.13. The \texttt{ARTIFACT.md} items are explained by that document's
frozen status --- it records a Semester 3 artefact at commit
\longtok{9019b5c946f17ffe4761b5f4eac13b86d1e51cc0}, dated 19 April 2026
--- but \texttt{REPRODUCE.md} §9 is live guidance, not a frozen record,
and carries the same defect.

\section{The audit apparatus}\label{the-audit-apparatus}

The integrity suite is driven by a single entry point,
\longtok{platform\_local/run\_\allowbreak{}all\_\allowbreak{}audits.py}, which imports a shared
result interface from \texttt{utils/audit\_utils.py} and registers its
audits as a tuple of name-and-function pairs. As it stands in this tree,
that tuple contains \textbf{nine} audits: \texttt{claim\_issues},
\texttt{sync\_issues}, \texttt{anon\_issues}, \texttt{strength\_issues},
\texttt{package\_issues}, \texttt{workflow\_issues},
\longtok{export\_\allowbreak{}guard\_\allowbreak{}issues}, \texttt{caveat\_issues} and
\texttt{scientific\_issues}. Each is implemented by a corresponding
\texttt{platform\_local/} module carrying a \texttt{\_\_main\_\_} block,
so each can also run on its own; \longtok{tests/test\_\allowbreak{}audit\_\allowbreak{}runner.py}
asserts that the registered modules and the
\texttt{platform\_local/*\_audit.py} files are the same set.

Some repository documents give different counts, and the difference is
historical. At the blind-review sweep the suite was thirteen audits,
registered as a list of script filenames at the repository root and
executed as subprocesses; the committed log of that run records
\texttt{METRIC\ audits\_total=13} with ten passing and three failing,
and the same sweep states that all seventeen \texttt{*\_audit.py}
scripts then at the root were executed, with the suite covering thirteen
of them. The registry now holds nine audits, all passing: a pruning
commit removed obsolete audits, and a later restructuring replaced the
subprocess runner with the in-process interface, so the seventeen-script
description has narrowed by the same route. No script now prints a
metric line directly; the line is constructed in one place,
\texttt{AuditResult.metric\_line()} in \texttt{utils/audit\_utils.py},
and emitted by the nine audit modules plus the runner. A repository-wide
scan finds several dozen files matching \texttt{*\_audit.py}, but that
population is not a suite: it includes unrelated analyses, test modules
and a third-party file in the virtual environment.

The output contract is uniform. \texttt{AuditResult.metric\_line()}
emits
\texttt{METRIC\ \textless{}name\textgreater{}=\allowbreak{}\textless{}issue\_\allowbreak{}count\textgreater{}},
so every audit reports one machine-readable line whose right-hand side
is the number of issues it found, and an audit passes exactly when that
count is zero. \texttt{render\_suite} prints each audit's metric line
and issue codes, then three suite-level lines ---
\texttt{METRIC\ suite\_issues}, \texttt{METRIC\ audits\_total} and
\texttt{METRIC\ audits\_passing} --- before either listing the failing
audits by name or stating that all audits are passing. The entry point
exits \texttt{0} when the suite passes and \texttt{1} otherwise, which
makes it usable as a gate; the packaging step runs the suite before
exporting and refuses to package if it fails. The runner's own test
asserts this rendering against a synthetic two-audit suite, checking for
\texttt{METRIC\ audits\_total=2} and \texttt{METRIC\ suite\_issues=1}.

\section{The deterministic ledger
check}\label{the-deterministic-ledger-check}

Where the suite above audits the submission package's internal
consistency, a second instrument audits the campaign's reported figures.
\longtok{outputs/verification/LEDGER\_\allowbreak{}CODE\_\allowbreak{}CHECK\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-19.json},
under schema \texttt{ledger-code-check-v1}, records eleven checks of
which eleven passed and none failed. They cover E1, E8 coverage, E9, E11
and E14 counts and hashes and the lost-source probes; they do not cover
the E10 score, E8 accuracy, the E2, E4, E5 and E7 lanes or anything
recorded after 19 September, and the E14 check confirmed the official
scorer's 4,426-row denominator rather than the 4,428-row denominator
this report uses. Each recomputes a claim from the underlying evidence
rather than trusting a summary of it, and the four classes of check
prove four different things.

and separate all-disposition diagnostic 2,271/4,428 (51.29\%)

& That the sealed request and the validation receipt are byte-identical
to the artefacts recorded at sealing time \\

historical temporary execution workspace paths are genuinely absent, so
a later re-creation cannot be mistaken for the lost original \\
\end{longtable}

The distinction matters under questioning. An exact division proves the
arithmetic is not a rounding convenience or a transcription error; it
cannot prove the underlying evidence is sound. A receipt-presence check
proves coverage claims are backed by artefacts; it cannot prove those
artefacts were produced correctly. A hash-equality check proves identity
between two artefacts --- here, between what was sealed and what was
later validated --- but does not prove correct execution or scientific
validity. An absence probe establishes non-discovery within its dated
search scope; it cannot establish universal absence or deletion. The
four checks answer different questions rather than forming a single
evidence ranking. Two instances show the classes in use. The historical
E1 records describe matching request and validation bindings without
establishing that execution occurred. Private request identifiers are
withheld. The E9 records show 41 graded rows resolving to 40 unique
competitions against a denominator of 75 in the streaming arm and
exactly one valid grade in the merged-VLLM arm, which is the check that
confirms the two serving arms were never pooled.

\section{The receipt model}\label{the-receipt-model}

A receipt in this campaign is a durable JSON artefact binding an outcome
to the inputs that produced it, and the binding carries the evidential
weight rather than the file's existence. The clearest statement of the
principle is the receipt-bundle auditor's docstring, which records that
the campaign and the receipt builders are kept deliberately separate
from the auditor, and that the auditor ``never imports a provider SDK,
contacts a service, launches a job, or treats
\texttt{status}/\texttt{online}/a URL/ID alone as provenance''. The
auditor reconstructs contract coverage, receipt predicates, canonical
hash rules, budget caps and cross-artefact bindings, carrying its
expectations as constants rather than reading them from what it checks
--- suite counts of 12 training, 14 primary-evaluation and 26 total ---
so a receipt set that quietly loses a suite fails the audit rather than
redefining it.

Receipts bind four dimensions of a run: protocol, runtime, source and
event order. The frozen-runtime record applies that discipline to the
runtime dimension alone, binding five artefacts --- protocol document,
screening manifest, launch manifest, launch fingerprint and source
binding --- by SHA-256 and failing closed on any mismatch. The judgment
layer for subjectively scored questions follows the same pattern: one
durable receipt per judgment binds the state bytes, the question text
and the raw answer, and battery execution is gated on a health check
rather than the service's availability, so a degraded answer cannot be
laundered into a valid one. Where a lane could not be scored, its
terminal state is recorded in the ledger of record with a named reopen
condition, so the absence of a result is itself receipted.

\section{Evidence loss in the finish
era}\label{evidence-loss-in-the-finish-era}

The historical record reports that four finish-era execution-source
components are unavailable: an E1 recovery driver, restored E9 runtime
dependencies, an E6 receipt validator and an E1 multilingual metadata
driver. This limits end-to-end reproduction. Operational
scratch-workspace names, executed request identifiers and private source
paths are omitted from this public derivative.

The four lost execution-source paths do not themselves contain the score
artefacts, but their loss limits end-to-end reproducibility. The
surviving evidence supports three narrower observations. First, none of
the four paths is the source of any reported score. The E1 and E9
replacement lanes were eventually run on 27 September with new thin
runners rather than the lost code (§8.4). The loss still bears on E1:
the source-context collector had to be re-implemented for that run and
was checked byte-for-byte against the original on only three recorded
tasks, which is one reason the 1/190 and 4/110 figures are not pooled.
Second, the sealed requests, validation receipts, regression fixtures
and the 10-of-10 targeted-test log from the lost E1 code all survive, so
a replacement can be checked against surviving fixtures, while full
historical behavioral equivalence remains unverified; that
re-implementation has since landed under a tracked path and passes the
lost suite's recorded regressions, four process-group and six cleanup.
Third, the loss is confined to finish-era iteration code: the
replacement-lane native runners survive in tracked locations, including
a 515-line multilingual runner carrying no reference to the deleted
tree. The policy adopted in response --- that execution sources live in
tracked repository paths and never in untracked scratch trees --- is the
durable outcome of the incident.

\section{The Semester 3 and Semester 4 provenance
boundary}\label{the-semester-3-and-semester-4-provenance-boundary}

The boundary between inherited and individual work is a git tag.
\texttt{capstone-final-2026-04-25} resolves to commit
\longtok{21a99ef766543a1ca86bfbad188445d3596ea73c}, dated 23 April 2026,
whose subject line records the final metric-separation rewrite that
segregated training rewards, held-out capability and proxy diagnostics.
Two hundred and fifty commits precede that tag. A reviewer can reproduce
the state at the boundary by adding it as a worktree,
\texttt{git\ worktree\ add\ ../tinker-rl-lab-sem3\ capstone-final-2026-04-25},
which materialises the Semester 3 tree without disturbing the current
checkout.

The Semester 3 group comprised six students --- Arvind C R, Sandhya
Jeyaraj, Madhu Kumara L, Mohammad Rafi, Dhruva N Murthy and Arumugam
Chetty K --- under guides Anwesh Reddy Paduri and Narayana Darapaneni.
Their work is the multi-framework benchmark infrastructure and the
initial GRPO baseline experiments. Semester 4 was carried out
individually under Ramesh Prakash Guledgudd, and the declaration facing
this report assigns to that contribution the research direction, the
experimental expansion, the statistical analyses, the P1--P8 paper
series and the E1--E14 campaign. The solo-authorship commit
\longtok{282e6b4f0f158de11b3f5bb4656e2d871f68f5c0}, dated 7 July 2026,
records that division at paper level. The provenance record states that
584 commits and 5,050 changed files separated the boundary from the tree
as it then stood, and that the eight paper PDFs were compiled at commit
\longtok{006e90a38e4dabe2d0b4778be01dc7b27f2d3462} on 9 July 2026, one
recompiled the following day after a findings-ledger rename. Those
counts carry an as-of date rather than describing the current tree, so a
reviewer recomputing them will obtain a larger number.

One consequence of this history shapes the repository's structure: the
implementation remains shared, with experiment drivers, result tables,
figures, LaTeX sources and audit scripts referencing one another across
the boundary, and the provenance record is explicit that this is why the
code is not split into separate trees. What the boundary separates is
authorship of the research contribution, not access to the machinery.
Two reporting rules follow and are enforced throughout this report:
original-contract and replacement-scope results are never pooled, with
no cross-suite aggregate, which the ledger check independently verifies
through the arm-separation and component-split checks; and no
replacement benchmark's score is presented as the score of the benchmark
it replaces, with every substitution named as such at its point of use.

\section{Residual non-determinism and the limits of
reproduction}\label{residual-non-determinism-and-the-limits-of-reproduction}

Not everything here is reproducible in the strict sense, and the
tolerances exist because of it. Three sources of residual
non-determinism survive even with fixed seeds and deterministic cuDNN
settings: remote LoRA training is not bit-deterministic across scheduler
decisions, with runs replayed months apart drifting by two to four
percentage points on GSM8K last-10, documented through three reruns of
the same seed-123 configuration; some matmul operations on H100 and A100
hardware introduce non-determinism through CUDA and cuBLAS; and sampling
at temperature 0.8 with top-p 0.95 executes in a remote virtual machine,
so a fixed seed does not fix the sampled trajectory. The record states
the consequence itself: run-to-run variance on this setup is of the same
order as the effect reported in the paper's PPO-versus-GRPO comparison,
which limits the historical saturated comparison. The superseded
unsaturated v1 comparison (§6.5.5) is baseline-specific and
test-sensitive, with paired-t p = 0.016 but exact sign-flip p = 0.0625;
these are historical v1 difference tests, not the provisional v2 TOST,
and neither supports a general algorithm ranking.

Three limits remain. Lanes blocked on external access cannot be
reproduced from this repository, because the blocking resource is not in
it. The source record identifies runners for the six lanes completed on
27 September under \longtok{outputs/finish\_\allowbreak{}pending\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-27/}. A
rerun requires access to those sources, cloud accounts, a served copy of
the actor and the benchmark assets; the public document package alone
does not supply that execution environment. E6 also requires an
available judge, and CORE-Bench's answer keys are deliberately kept out
of the repository. The evidence-loss paths cannot be reproduced in their
original form, only re-implemented against surviving fixtures, and that
distinction should be preserved in any future citation. And figures
originating at the Semester 3 boundary describe the tree at that tag, so
reproducing them requires the worktree checkout rather than the current
HEAD.

\section{Primary-artefact recovery and
availability}\label{primary-artefact-recovery-and-availability}

The 2 October checkout audit correctly recorded five named primary paths
as absent from that checkout. A bounded read-only search of the author's
Mac on 3 October recovered all five originals in a separate live
research checkout, plus an earlier pending-experiments snapshot. The
files were ignored by the local \texttt{outputs/*} rule. Their absence
from the frozen cloud checkout and inspected Git/archive corpus was
therefore a scope boundary, not evidence of deletion. The frozen
repository is unchanged; exact recovered copies are retained privately
and are not included in this public derivative.

The additional September 12 \texttt{Pending\_Experiments.md} is a
distinct 1,535-byte snapshot, not a duplicate of the finish ledger. All
six UTF-8 bodies total 23,486 bytes; independently recomputed byte
lengths and SHA-256 values match the authenticated source export and the
materialized copies. Source filesystem timestamps and existence come
from the Mac export; no historical Git commit or pre-execution
attestation is invented. These are recovered files, not newly generated
experiment receipts.

The E14 denominator erratum is explained in §8.2: native judged-row
accuracy is 2,271/4,426 (51.31\%), while the conservative
all-disposition diagnostic is 2,271/4,428 (51.29\%). The finish ledger
explicitly says the E4 rerun had not launched at its September 21
snapshot, while the later secondary field dates the claimed run to that
same day. Without a primary timestamp or raw receipt, the chronology
remains unresolved and the full-run outcome is excluded from empirical
conclusions (Appendix D §D.7).

A separate read-only payload check on the Mac verified all 29 explicit
reference records covering 27 distinct file paths. For E8, 1,979
declared archive members match their retained local copies, including
1,967 unique native-grade records; independent retallying of the
exported per-task records gives 450 correct, 1,517 incorrect, 1,086
length finishes, 881 stop finishes, 1,960 sure and seven unsure
responses, and zero parser-error flags. The 1,259 unparsed-answer total
remains supported by the recovered diagnostic rather than re-counted
from the compact grade export. For E10, all 525 declared archive-member
bindings match and the selected 97 distinct native traces each match
their receipt hash; the exported trace records independently sum to 88
utility passes and zero handled errors. Historical partial traces are
not pooled into that selection.

The Mac also inspected the retained E14 disposition and judge files,
reporting 4,428 distinct dispositions, 4,426 accepted and 2,271 correct.
The two excluded rows, 1195 and 2044, have recorded skip reasons and no
Equivalence Judgement heading in the retained judge text. The compact
export carries those counts, the two exclusion records and source
hashes; it does not include all judge text or establish independent
mathematical correctness. The source-native score distinguishes the
4,426 judged denominator from the 4,428-disposition diagnostic,
supporting the two labelled arithmetic conventions without changing the
source bytes. No scorer, judge or model was rerun. The training-identity
file also matches its archived copy, but its original configuration,
metadata and output-log payload paths and dirty source diff remain
unresolved.

Recovery closes the five named author-side search gaps only. The public
release still omits those receipts and underlying rows, so the bounded
stored-grade/hash reports do not make the original ledger check
independently executable from this checkout. These checks do not
reproduce model execution, regrade answers or establish decontamination.
The original checkout audit remains a dated record in
\longtok{campaign\_\allowbreak{}evidence\_\allowbreak{}availability.json}; the new source export is
\texttt{withheld\ primary-source\ recovery\ record}, the companion
\texttt{withheld\ original-source\ preservation\ record} binds every
recovered copy, and
\texttt{withheld\ stored-payload\ validation\ record} records the
bounded payload checks. The separately lost execution-source incident in
§B.8 remains unresolved by this recovery.

\section{Fresh-Sampling Diagnostic: Frozen Contract and
Audit}\label{fresh-sampling-diagnostic-frozen-contract-and-audit}

The new evidence in §7.4 is run
\texttt{V2\ frozen-policy\ sampling\ study}, a sampling-only experiment
with no weight updates. Its protocol, code, tests, expected cohort and
parser were hash-bound before allocation or generation at 18:15:03 UTC
on 2 October 2026. The freeze covers 26 files. Prompt ordering was
deterministic and independent of scores, using seed 20261002; the V2
smoke and main cohorts are disjoint from each other and from the failed
V1 smoke, and the request-seed namespace is disjoint from V1. The main
cohort is 512 prompts and the independent smoke is 32 prompts, each with
eight initial plus 24 fresh draws.

\textbf{Model and decoding.} Model and tokenizer are
\texttt{Qwen/Qwen3-8B}, both at revision
\longtok{b968826d9c46dd6066d109eabc6255188de91218}. The dataset is
\texttt{openai/gsm8k}, configuration \texttt{main}, \textbf{train}
split, revision \longtok{740312add88f781978c0658806c59bc2815b9866} (7,473
rows). The model used BF16 with thinking disabled, temperature 0.7,
top-p 0.8, top-k 20, min-p 0, a 1,024-generated-token cap and
4,096-token context. Each request had n = 1 and its own seed; batching
grouped four prompts. The recorded stack used vLLM 0.11.0, PyTorch
2.8.0, Transformers 4.57.1, eager execution and disabled prefix caching
on one NVIDIA A100-SXM4-80GB. The supervised process completed in
approximately 26.6 minutes, including loading and both cohorts.
Hardware/kernel changes need not reproduce samples bit-for-bit.

\textbf{Parser and review contract.} The V2 parser accepts specified
explicit final-answer forms, normalises exact rational numbers and fails
closed on malformed, conflicting or invalid claims; it does not search
arbitrary prose for a number. Gold parsing passed offline on all 7,473
training rows before generation. This is a stricter contract than the
existing GSM8K test evaluator and must not be retrospectively
substituted for it. Automated validation checked the frozen source and
manifest bindings, cohort identity, completeness, ordered draw indices,
unique seeds, reward recomputation and quality counts. A separate
set-based computation reproduced 461 eligible groups and 33 events. The
audit covered the first reward-changing response plus all eight initial
responses for every event. Two assistant reviewers handled disjoint
event partitions; this does not mean that each event received two
independent reviews. The coordinator's additional arithmetic checks
covered both recoveries and the ambiguous bread-rise case. The
implementation field \longtok{manual\_\allowbreak{}review\_\allowbreak{}complete} records
completion of this assistant review, not human validation; residual
model-review error is possible.

\textbf{Descriptive checks, not controller validation.} Among the
original 461 homogeneous groups, the combined-16 prefix became mixed for
15 (3.25\%); the fresh-16-only comparison was mixed for 24 (5.21\%). The
all-32-parseable-and-uncapped subgroup was 12/439, selected on later
outcomes and therefore not an unbiased replacement primary. Fixed
Beta(1,1) and Beta(1/2,1/2) posterior-predictive assumptions assigned
rescue probabilities of 72.73\% and 49.41\%, respectively, versus
observed strict-reward mixedness of 7.16\%. Their descriptive Brier
scores were 0.4964 and 0.2450, against 0.0716 for plug-in zero. These
uncalibrated fixed-prior assumptions substantially overpredict this
endpoint; the exercise does not fit or validate a controller and does
not vindicate certainty of zero rescue. The post-hoc later-change check
in the 20 parser-first groups is exploratory and remains separate from
the predeclared first-event numeric subset.

\textbf{Receipt locations and hash boundary.} Raw ordered completions
are in the run directory's \texttt{study.jsonl} and
\texttt{smoke.jsonl}; the per-prompt analysis is
\texttt{analysis\_final/per\_prompt.csv}. The final summary, validation
and completed audit preserve the unchanged raw primary endpoint
alongside the conservative numeric subset. The main \texttt{study.jsonl}
has SHA-256
\longtok{859ee12d9c146a3e9db65882b0a305d123bd385c573fa6ac4692b25220ac422f}.
The summary and validation list the freeze, manifest and source hashes,
distinguishing the manifest's canonical-content binding from its
file-byte checksum. Hashes bind the receipts to the trusted
pre-execution freeze; they are not cryptographic attestation of GPU
execution. Token counts and identities were cross-checked, not
independently decoded. The failed V1 smoke is retained as engineering
evidence and is never pooled with V2. These new sampling receipts are
separate from the original historical files subsequently recovered in
§B.11.

\section{C1 scientific source
availability}\label{c1-scientific-source-availability}

The C1 analysis preserves the frozen 11/64 endpoint, the separate three
conservative numerical witnesses, all sixty-four case classifications
and all eleven event records. The included case ledgers and public
scientific reference bindings retain disagreements and hash-level
scientific source descriptions. This public derivative excludes runtime
allocation, session, deletion and raw-transfer records, account resource
limits and operational provenance. No live runtime or GPU execution is
attested by publication of this document.

The historical thesis and evidence archives remain unchanged outside
this public package. The public derivative has its own editable
Markdown, LaTeX, builder and selected scientific ledgers. Appendix G is
the complete scientific case index; Appendix I and the public
availability manifest explain omitted source categories.

The benchmark identities in Chapter 8 likewise distinguish public
releases from the exact historical contracts. Public availability of
AgentHarm, BinaryAudit, App-Bench or another named suite does not
establish access to the lane's contracted private payload, split,
deployment or verifier. Conversely, an unavailable historical contract
is not evidence that the entire benchmark is private or nonexistent.
Tau3 here means the pinned \texttt{banking\_knowledge} component
associated with tau-Knowledge; ML-Dev-Bench's 34 campaign configurations
are not relabelled as the paper's 30-task benchmark. These identity
checks repair attribution, not the missing primary campaign evidence.

\section{Scope and convention}\label{scope-and-convention}

This appendix records the symbols and abbreviations used across this
report and the papers behind it, as the sources actually use them rather
than as a house style would prefer. Where a symbol carries more than one
meaning across documents, both are listed and the collision flagged,
because a reader carrying one definition into another chapter will
misread a figure.

plus KL penalty. Inspected custom-loss Tinker loops use REINFORCE with a
